On the wave turbulence theory of 2D gravity waves, II: propagation of randomness Thanks: Y. D. was supported in part by a Simons Collaboration Grant on Wave Turbulence, NSF grant DMS-2246908, and a Sloan Fellowship. A. D. I. was supported in part by NSF-FRG grant DMS-2245228, by NSF-UEFISCDI grant DMS-2407694, and a Simons Collaboration Grant on Wave Turbulence. F. P. was supported in part by a start-up grant from the University of Toronto, and NSERC grants RGPIN-2018-06487 and RGPIN-2025-06419.
Yu Deng, Alexandru D. Ionescu, Fabio Pusateri

Abstract
. This is a sequel to our work [ 22 ] , initiating the rigorous study of wave turbulence in the context of the water waves equations. We combine energy estimates, normal forms, and probabilistic and combinatorial arguments to complete the construction of long-time solutions with random initial data for the 2 2 d ( 1 1 d interface) gravity water waves system on large tori. More precisely, we consider solutions to the Cauchy problem with spatial period R ≫ 1 R\gg 1 , which have randomly distributed phases at the initial time, with an L ∞ L^{\infty} norm of size ≈ ϵ \approx\epsilon and large energy of size ≈ ϵ 2 ​ R \approx\epsilon^{2}R . Under the assumption ϵ ∈ [ R − 8 / 3 + , R 0 − ] \epsilon\in[R^{-8/3+},R^{0-}] we show that randomness propagates on a time interval [ 0 , T 1 ] [0,T_{1}] with T 1 = O ( ϵ − 8 / 3 + ) T_{1}=O(\epsilon^{-8/3+}) ; in particular, solutions satisfy similar L 2 L^{2} and L ∞ L^{\infty} type bounds as those satisfied at the initial time on this long time interval. This is the first long-time regularity result for solutions of water waves systems with large energy (but small local energy), which is the correct setup for applications to wave turbulence. Such a result is only possible in the presence of randomness. The proof requires several novel ideas, including a robust framework involving Feynman trees, compatible with normal forms, a new time and frequency dependent renormalization procedure, and new combinatorial arguments that are specific to one dimensional problems.



1.
Introduction

Our work in this paper is motivated by the outstanding problem of understanding
rigorously the wave turbulence theory of water waves.
This is a classical problem in Mathematical Physics, going back to work of Hasselmann
[
25
,
26
]
, and a key part of Hasselman’s
theory of climate variability
. We refer the reader to the recent book
[
40
]

for a more complete description of the problem and many examples.

The wave turbulence problem has received intense attention in recent years, mainly in the context of simpler
dispersive models such as semilinear Schrödinger equations;
see for example
[
8
,
9
,
12
,
13
,
35
,
17
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
43
]
.
The focus in these papers has been the rigorous derivation of the
wave kinetic equation
(WKE).
At the analytical level, this amounts
to understanding precisely the dynamics of solutions corresponding to randomized initial data,
in the large box and weakly nonlinear limit, and on long time intervals.

Our goal is to initiate a similar investigation for irrotational water waves models.
There is a fundamental difference between water waves, which are quasilinear evolutions,
and semilinear models: the solution cannot be constructed using the Duhamel formula,
due to unavoidable loss of derivatives. In particular, the solution cannot be described using
iterations of the Duhamel formula (Feynman trees), which is the central idea of the analysis of semilinear models.

To address this fundamental difficulty we propose a new mechanism, based on a combination of two main ingredients:

(1)
Energy estimates:
In our first paper
[
22
]
we prove energy estimates. These are
deterministic
estimates, in the sense that they hold for all solutions. The key point is to show that the energy increment is controlled by an
L^{\infty}
-based
norm of the solution, which can then be exploited effectively in the analysis of solutions with random initial data.

(2)
Propagation of randomness and pointwise bounds:

The second step consists in proving propagation of randomness
(in a suitable form) then deriving optimal pointwise bounds on randomized solutions in a large box.
For this one can use the iterated Duhamel formula; the point is that losses
of derivatives are allowed at this stage, thanks to the accompanying estimate on the high order energy.

In this paper we complete the second part of this program for the problem of pure
gravity water waves in two dimensions (1d interface).

1.1.
The water waves equations

We consider the two-dimensional irrotational gravity water wave problem on a large torus of size
R
, written in the form

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}h&=G(h)\phi,\\
\partial_{t}\phi&=-h-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\frac{(G(h)\phi+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}}{2(1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2})},\end{aligned}\right.

where
G(h)=\sqrt{1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2}}\cdot\mathcal{N}(h)
and
\mathcal{N}(h)
is the Dirichlet
to Neumann operator associated with the (periodic) domain below the graph of a function
h
,

\Omega_{t}:=\{(x,y)\,:\,y<h(x),\,x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}\}
where
\mathbb{T}_{R}:=\mathbb{R}/(2\pi R)
.
We denote the complex physical unknown

(1.2)

u:=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi,

where
\phi
is the trace on the interface
\partial\Omega_{t}
of the fluid’s velocity potential in
\Omega_{t}
.

In this work, which is a sequel to our work
[
22
]
, we prove the long-time existence of
random solutions for the full equations (
1.1
) for the Cauchy problem with random data.

1.2.
The random data problem and main result

We now set up the random initial data problem for the equation (
1.1
).
Let
\{g_{k}(\omega)\}
be i.i.d. normalized complex Gaussian random variables,
and
\psi
be a fixed, rapidly decaying Lipschitz function on
\mathbb{R}
.
We set the random initial data
u(0)=u_{\mathrm{in}}
, for the unknown
u
defined in (
1.2
), as

(1.3)

u_{in}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}\setminus\{0\}}\epsilon R^{1/2}\sqrt{\psi(k)}g_{k}(\omega)e^{ikx}

where
\mathbb{Z}_{R}=R^{-1}\mathbb{Z}
. Without loss of generality we may assume that
\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}h=0
, so that our solution has zero mean, consistent with the assumption that the sum in (
1.3
) is taken over
k\neq 0
;
this condition is preserved by the solution flow of (
1.1
).
We now state our main result as follows.

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that
0<\theta_{0}\leq 1/10
,
N_{0}
is an integer
\geq(100/\theta_{0})^{3}
, and
\eta_{0}:=(\theta_{0}/100)^{3}
. Assume that
R\gg 1
is sufficiently large (depending on
\theta_{0},N_{0}
),
\epsilon\in[R^{-8/3+\theta_{0}},R^{-\theta_{0}}]
, and set
T_{1}:=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
. Finally, assume that
\psi\in C^{1}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies the bounds

(1.4)

|\psi(x)|+|\psi^{\prime}(x)|\leq(1+|x|)^{-N_{0}-10},\qquad\text{ for any }x\in\mathbb{R}.

Then, with probability
\geq 1-e^{-R^{\eta_{0}}}
, the equation (
1.1
) with initial data (
1.3
) has a smooth solution
u=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi\in C([0,T_{1}]:H^{N_{0}})

on the time interval
[0,T_{1}]
, which satisfies the estimates

(1.5)

\displaystyle\|u(t)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta_{0}}R^{1/2},\qquad\sum_{\alpha\leq 6}\|D^{\alpha}_{x}u(t)\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta_{0}},\qquad\text{ for any }t\in[0,T_{1}].

1.3.
Remarks

We make some comments on the statement of Theorem
1.1
.

(1)
The smallness assumption.
We emphasize that our solutions are assumed to be small in
L^{\infty}
-type norms (or, equivalently, in local energy norms per unit area), but have very large total energy. This is the proper framework for understanding wave turbulence and for physically relevant applications connected to the Hasselman theory, since this is the natural appearance of oceans: locally small perturbations of the flat solution, but which have large total energy due to the large size of the oceans.

This is in sharp contrast with all the previous (deterministic) extended lifespan results for solutions of the water waves system, such as those in
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
7
,
21
,
24
,
27
,
28
,
29
,
30
,
32
,
34
,
46
,
50
,
51
,
52
,
54
,
55
]
and others, in which the total energy of the solution at the initial time (and, in fact, high order energies involving high Sobolev norms) was assumed to be small.

(2)
Propagation of randomness and pointwise control
. Given that our solutions have large total energy, long time existence is only possible due of randomness. The main point of the theorem is therefore to prove that the solutions retain
some form of randomness on long time intervals, which, in particular, leads to uniform pointwise control as stated in (
1.5
).

This requires an elaborate scheme, starting from the Duhamel formula and further expanding the solutions in terms of the initial data using Feynman trees. A key difficulty is that our water waves system is quasilinear, so the standard scheme based on the Duhamel formula loses derivatives. We address this by truncating the solution in frequency, and then controlling the original solution
u
in high Sobolev norms (using a deterministic energy argument) and the truncated solution
u_{\mathrm{tr}}
as well as the difference
u-u_{\mathrm{tr}}
in
L^{\infty}
-type norms (using a probabilistic argument based on Feynman expansions).

(3)
Lifespan of the solution.
Wave turbulence can only be observed if we control the evolution for a long time, and only in the presence of randomness. At the analytical level, the basic question then becomes for how long we can control the solution given random initial data of
L^{\infty}
norm
\|u_{in}\|_{L^{\infty}}\approx\epsilon\ll 1
and energy
\mathcal{E}\approx\epsilon^{2}R\gg 1
(as in (
1.3
)).

Ideally, in a cubic problem one would like to control the solution for a time
T_{\epsilon}\approx\epsilon^{-4}
, which is the
kinetic time
at which one could hope to observe wave turbulence and derive the wave kinetic equation. This is precisely the conclusion in the case of the cubic NLS in dimensions
\geq 2
, as proved in a sequence of
remarkable papers
[
8
,
9
,
17
,
18
,
19
]
, but the problem remains wide open for more complex (especially quasilinear) evolutions such as the water waves systems.

In the case of water waves we are not able to reach the kinetic time due to multiple issues, as described
in Subsection
1.4
below. A more realistic first step is to establish control of the solution for as long as possible, both at the energy level and in terms of propagation of randomness, with the hope that this is a key step towards understanding a full wave turbulence theory. Our main claim in this paper is that in the case of irrotational gravity water waves in 2D we can have such control for a time
T_{1}\approx\epsilon^{-8/3+}
.

(4)
Parameters.
For the rest of this paper, we fix several parameters. As in Theorem
1.1
assume that
\theta_{0}\in(0,1/10]
is given, and set
\theta:=\theta_{0}/100
. We also fix
N_{0}\geq\theta^{-3}
as the highest Sobolev regularity we will propagate on our solution, and
\eta_{0}:=\theta^{3}
sufficiently small. Later on, in Section
3
, we will fix two more constants
A=\lfloor\theta^{-1}\rfloor
and
N=\lfloor\theta^{-2}\rfloor
.

The implicit constants in
\lesssim
may depend on the parameters
\theta_{0},N_{0}
, while
R
is assumed to be sufficiently large relative to these parameters, and
\epsilon\in[R^{-8/3+\theta_{0}},R^{-\theta_{0}}]
.

1.4.
Main ideas of the proof

We provide a complete outline of the proof in Section
2
.
The basic idea is to adapt and extend the analysis developed recently by Deng-Hani
[
17
,
18
,
19
]
in the case of the cubic NLS to our more complicated water waves system. There are several significant differences, which lead to substantial new difficulties
1
1

1

These issues should be thought of as generic, and are likely to appear in the study of the wave turbulence theory of many dispersive evolution equations, both semilinear and quasilinear.
, such as:

\bullet\,\,\,
Truncation of the solution in frequency. The water waves system is a quasilinear system, so the solution cannot be described using just the Duhamel formula due to loss of derivatives. However, in order to be able to use the main randomness assumption on the data, we need to express a substantial part of the solution as, essentially, an algebraic expression in terms of the initial data.

Our approach is to first truncate the solution at a certain frequency level (depending on
R
and
\theta_{0}
). We can then control the high frequencies using the deterministic energy estimates proved in our earlier work
[
22
]
, and the low frequencies using Feynman expansions (derived from the Duhamel formula) and the randomness of the initial data.

\bullet\,\,\,

Normal form variable.
The original water waves system (
1.1
) is quadratic in the variable
u
. However, in order to control the solution for a longer time, we need to perform a normal form and write the system in terms of a new variable
w
. This is possible, of course, since the system does not contain quadratic resonances, but the issue is that our randomness assumption is in terms of the original variable
u
(as in (
1.3
)), not in terms of the modified normal form variable
w
.

To address this issue we develop a new and more elaborate notion of trees, in the context of Feynman expansions. Our trees naturally contain two types of nodes: the interaction nodes, corresponding to the nonlinear interactions, and the normal form nodes which correspond to the transition to the normal form variable, and which appear right before the leaves.

In addition to a first (quadratic) normal form, it turns out that we also need
to perform a second (cubic) partial normal form which eliminates non-resonant contributions.
For the remaining, potentially resonant, interactions we prove a
C^{1/2}
smoothness property for the symbol.
Such a regularity is then needed in the combinatorial part of the argument to prove a necessary
cancellation between“irregular chains” (first introduced in
[
17
]
for the cubic NLS, where the symbol
is the constant
1
).

\bullet\,\,\,

Time and frequency dependent renormalization.

Renormalization is a key step in order to remove certain nonlinear interactions that do not satisfy good estimates and identify a “profile” of the solution that can be controlled for a long time. In the case of the cubic NLS only a simple renormalization procedure is needed, namely multiplication by a linear oscillatory factor that depends only on the (conserved) mass of the solution.

In our case, such a simple renormalization is not possible. We employ instead a two-step renormalization process. The first step is explicit and involves weakening the contribution of the so-called “trivial cubic resonances”, which represent the largest nonlinear contribution in the problem. The second step is more subtle and involves an implicit renormalization factor that depends on both time and frequency, and which weakens higher order resonant contributions.

It is important to notice that both of these renormalization steps are
deterministic
:
they depend only on the profile
\psi
in (
1.3
), not on the full random initial data
u_{in}
.

\bullet\,\,

Unfavorable counting in dimension
1
evolutions.

One dimensional problems appear naturally in fluids and other physical situations, but have not been treated
in the series of papers on the cubic NLS cited above. Only the recent work
[
45
]
dealt with a
1
d MMT model.
In this work we notice some tree structures
2
2

2

This could be named “broken chains” for their
resemblance to the “irregular chains” of Deng-Hani
[
17
]
.

whose size diverges for times sightly
larger than our existence time
T_{1}
. See Appendix
8
for details.
This divergence is due to some unfavorable counting in one dimension, where a small ball
of size
\lambda
only contributes linearly, while it contributes
\lambda^{d}
in dimension
d
;
see (
8.3
) and (
8.6
).
Moreover, unlike the case of “irregular chains”
[
17
]
there do not seem to be apparent
cancellations
3
3

3

It is conceivable that (approximate) integrability would play a role here,
but we leave this problem open for further investigation.
within these structures.

1.5.
Further results

The ideas developed here can be adapted to other models, to prove long-time regularity results for data of small local energy, in the presence of randomness. There are two main factors that ultimately determine the length of time on which one can establish control: (1) the combinatorics of the problem, in particular the linear dispersion relation, the structure of resonances, and the dimension of the underlying space, and (2) the range of validity of a high order energy estimate.

For example, in the case of irrotational water waves in 3D (a system similar to (
1.1
) on a 2D large torus
\mathbb{T}_{R}^{2}
), with random initial data similar to (
1.3
), our approach would give control the solution on the time interval
[0,T_{1}]
, where

(1.6)

T_{1}\approx\epsilon^{-2}.

The limitation here is mainly due to the energy estimate: since there are nontrivial cubic resonances we can only control high order energy norms of the solution on a time of length
\approx\epsilon^{-2}
, where
\epsilon
measures
L^{\infty}
-type norms of the solution. Randomness and pointwise control can then be propagated on the time interval
[0,T_{1}]
by adapting the construction in this paper.

Another widely used wave turbulence model is the MMT equation

(1.7)

\partial_{t}\psi+i|\partial_{x}|^{\alpha}\psi=\pm i|\partial_{x}|^{\beta/4}\big(|\partial_{x}|^{\beta/4}\psi\cdot\big||\partial_{x}|^{\beta/4}\psi\big|^{2}\big)

in one or two dimensions
x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}
or
x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}^{2}
, for various parameters
\alpha\in(0,2]\setminus\{1\}
and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
. The model has been introduced by Majda–McLaughlin–Tabak
[
39
]
, and has been studied extensively both at the numerical and at the theoretical level.

In the special (semilinear) case
\beta=0
our analysis in this paper leads to a time of existence of the solution
T_{1}\approx\epsilon^{-8/3+}
, improving on the recent result of Vassilev
[
45
]
. Energy estimates are not needed in this case, since there is no derivative loss in the Duhamel formula, and one can proceed like in this paper with several simplifications (no truncation step is needed, one can use only standard ternary Feynman trees, without normal form branching nodes, and the renormalization involves only the
L^{2}
norm of the solution).

On the other hand, for larger values
\beta>0
one would likely need to also prove energy estimates to keep control of some high order energy of the solution, which would lead to a shorter lifespan of the solution with
T_{1}\approx\epsilon^{-2}
as in (
1.6
).

1.6.
Organization

The rest of the paper is organized as follows:
in Section 2 we collect all the ingredients needed for the proof of Theorem
1.1
and reduce matters to proving two propositions: Proposition
2.1
concerning normal forms and long expansions of the nonlinearity, and Proposition
2.4
concerning propagation of randomness and pointwise control of the solution.

In Section
3
we introduce the main ideas of the probabilistic argument, including truncation, the construction of approximate solutions using suitable Feynman trees expansions,
and the time and frequency dependent renormalization procedure.
The point is to reduce the proof of Proposition
2.4
to Propositions
3.8
and
3.9
.
The last part of Section
3
contains some results on counting bounds that will be used throughout the paper.

Section
4
treats the irregular chains. Here, to obtain the main cancellation as in
[
17
]
we
need to exploit the
C^{1/2}
smoothness of the symbols of the transformed water waves equations
proved in Proposition
2.1
.
Section
5
contains the heart of the combinatorial analysis and the estimates for molecules,
eventually leading to the main bound in Proposition
5.10
.
Section
6
is dedicated to the proof of Proposition
3.9
which
estimates the difference between the profile of the approximate solution constructed by Feynman trees
expansions, and the true (renormalized) profile.

Section
7
contains the proof of the normal form Proposition
2.1
.
Finally, Section
8
shows an example Feynman diagrams whose size diverges
in the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
for times larger than
\epsilon^{-8/3-}
, showing the almost optimality
of our current arguments.

2.
Preliminaries and strategy of the proof

In this section we describe the general strategy for the proof of Theorem
1.1
,
which is composed of several steps.
We state all the main propositions that lead to the proof of the main theorem, and prove some of the more immediate supporting results.

After introducing some notation,
we begin our proof with a normal form transformation that eliminates all quadratic terms (in
u
)
in the equations, as well as the non-resonant cubic terms.
This is the main content of Proposition
2.1

together with an homogeneity expansion for the water waves equations up to arbitrary order
A
,
with suitable control on the remainders. The proofs of these results are given in Section
7
.
Then we show how the initial data for the transformed variable essentially preserves
the initial randomness (
1.3
) and recall the main energy increment Proposition from
[
22
]
.
Subsection
2.4
contains the main probabilistic result of the paper with the
“propagation of ramdomness” stated in Proposition
2.4
. The strategy for this proposition
is explained in more details in Section
3
.
Using Proposition
2.4

we perform our main bootstrap argument in Proposition
2.5
, thereby obtaining the main theorem.

2.1.
Some notation

We introduce first some notation. For functions
f\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
we define the Fourier transform

(2.1)

\widehat{f}(\xi)=\mathcal{F}(f)(\xi):=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}f(x)e^{-i\xi x}\,dx,\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R:=\{n/R:\,n\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

The definition shows easily that

(2.2)

\begin{split}&f(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi)e^{i\xi x},\\
&\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}f(x)\overline{g(x)}\,dx=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi)\overline{\widehat{g}(\xi)},\\
&\mathcal{F}(fg)(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta).\end{split}

For any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
let
k^{+}:=\max(k,0)
and
k^{-}:=\min(k,0)
. We fix an even smooth function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to[0,1]
supported in
[-8/5,8/5]
and equal to
1
in
[-5/4,5/4]
,
and define

\varphi_{k}(x):=\varphi(x/2^{k})-\varphi(x/2^{k-1}),\qquad\varphi_{\leq k}(x):=\varphi(x/2^{k}),\qquad\varphi_{>k}(x):=1-\varphi(x/2^{k}),

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Let
P_{k}
,
P_{\leq k}
, and
P_{>k}
denote the operators on
\mathbb{T}_{R}
defined by the Fourier multipliers
\varphi_{k}
,

\varphi_{\leq k}
, and
\varphi_{\geq k}
respectively. Notice that
P_{k}=0
if
2^{k}R\leq 1/2
.
We also let
\overline{\mathbb{Z}}:=\mathbb{Z}\cup\{-\infty\}
and define the function
\varphi_{-\infty}
and the operator
P_{-\infty}
by

\varphi_{-\infty}(\xi):=\mathbf{1}_{\{0\}}(\xi),\qquad P_{-\infty}f(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}f(y)\,dy.

We define the Sobolev spaces
\dot{H}^{N,b}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
and the
L^{\infty}
-based spaces
\dot{W}^{N,b}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
by the norms

(2.3)

\begin{split}\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{N,b}}&:=\big\{\|P_{-\infty}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}R^{-2b}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}(2^{2Nk}+2^{2bk})\big\}^{1/2},\\
\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{N,b}}&:=\|P_{-\infty}f\|_{L^{\infty}}R^{-b}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\|P_{k}f\|_{L^{\infty}}(2^{Nk}+2^{bk}),\end{split}

where
b\in[-1,1]
and
N\geq\max(b,0)
, see (2.25)–(2.26) in
[
22
]
.

Many of our operators are defined by multipliers in the Fourier space. A convenient norm to estimate multipliers is the Wiener norm. For
d\geq 1
we define the class of multipliers

(2.4)

S^{\infty}=S^{\infty}((\mathbb{Z}/R)^{d}):=\{m:(\mathbb{Z}/R)^{d}\to\mathbb{C}:\,{\|m\|}_{S^{\infty}}:={\|\mathcal{F}^{-1}(m)\|}_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{R}^{d})}<\infty\}.

Finally we introduce paradifferential operators on
\mathbb{T}_{R}
,
which are defined for functions
f,g\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
by the formula

(2.5)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}\big(T_{f}g\big)(\xi)&:=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\chi(\xi-\eta,\eta)\widehat{f}(\xi-\eta)\widehat{g}(\eta),\\
\chi(x,y)&:=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\varphi_{\leq k-20}(x)\varphi_{k}(y+x/2).\end{split}

We remark that paradifferential is an important technical tool in
[
22
]
;
however, in this paper the notation (
2.5
) and associated bounds
are only used in Propositions
2.3
and
2.5
.

2.2.
Normal form reduction

We consider solutions
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{2}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{2}+1/2,1/2})
of the system (
1.1
)
on large one-dimensional tori
x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}
, satisfying

(2.6)

\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t)\|_{H^{N_{2}}}\leq B_{2},\qquad\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty},0}}\leq\varepsilon_{\infty},

for some parameters
N_{2}\geq 20
,
N_{\infty}\in[4,N_{2}-4]
,
B_{2}\in[0,\infty)
,
\varepsilon_{\infty}\ll 1
, and for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
.
We will prove the following:

Proposition 2.1

(Normal form reduction)
.

Assume
A\in[3,N_{2}/2]
is a fixed integer. Then we can construct a normal form transformation

(2.7)

w:=u+\mathcal{A}_{2}(u)+\mathcal{A}_{3}(u),

such that the new variable
w
satisfies the equation

(2.8)

(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})w=\sum_{r=3}^{A}\mathcal{N}_{r}(w)+\mathcal{N}_{>A}.

Here
\mathcal{A}_{r}
and
\mathcal{N}_{r}
are homogeneous expressions of degree
r
, which can be represented in the Fourier space in the form

(2.9)

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{A}_{r}(u)}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{r-1}}\sum_{\iota_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{r}=\xi}a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})\prod_{j=1}^{r}\widehat{u^{\iota_{j}}}(\eta_{j}),\\
\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{r}(w)}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{r-1}}\sum_{\iota_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{r}=\xi}n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})\prod_{j=1}^{r}\widehat{w^{\iota_{j}}}(\eta_{j}),\end{split}

where the symbols
a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
and
n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
vanish unless there is
r^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,r\}
such that
\iota_{1}=\ldots=\iota_{r^{\prime}}=+
and
\iota_{r^{\prime}+1}=\ldots=\iota_{r}=-
. Here and below we define
z^{+}=z
and
z^{-}=\overline{z}
for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
.

The functions
w
,
\mathcal{N}_{n}(w)
, and
\mathcal{N}_{>A}
satisfies the bounds

(2.10)

\begin{split}&\|w\|_{H^{N_{2}-2}}\lesssim B_{2},\qquad\|w\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-2,0}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\infty},\\
&\|\mathcal{N}_{n}(w)\|_{H^{N_{2}-n-3/2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\infty}^{n-1}B_{2},\qquad\|\mathcal{N}_{>A}\|_{H^{N_{2}-A-5/2}}\lesssim\varepsilon_{\infty}^{A}B_{2}.\end{split}

In addition, the symbols
a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
,
n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
in (
2.9
) satisfy the following properties:

(1)

For any
r\in\{3,\ldots,A\}
the symbols
n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
are homogeneous of degree
r-1/2
and satisfy suitable
S^{\infty}
estimates, i.e. for any
k,k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\lambda\in[0,\infty)

(2.11)

\begin{split}&n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\lambda(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}))=\lambda^{r-1/2}n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}),\\
&\big\|n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi_{k_{r}}(\xi_{r})\varphi_{k}(\xi_{1}+\ldots+\xi_{r})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k/2}2^{(r-1)\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})}.\end{split}

(2)

For
r\in\{2,3\}
the symbols
a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}
are homogeneous of degree
r-1
and satisfy suitable
S^{\infty}
estimates, i.e. for any
k,k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\lambda\in[0,\infty)

(2.12)

\begin{split}&a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\lambda(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}))=\lambda^{r-1}a_{\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}),\\
&\big\|a_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi_{k_{r}}(\xi_{r})\varphi_{k}(\xi_{1}+\ldots+\xi_{r})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k/2}2^{(r-3/2)\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})}.\end{split}

(3)

For
r=3
the symbols
n_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}
vanish unless
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})=(++-)
. The symbol
n_{3,++-}
is purely imaginary, symmetric in the first two variables, supported in the set
\{(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\eta_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,\eta_{1}\eta_{2}\eta_{3}(\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\eta_{3})\leq 0\}
, and satisfies the
C^{1/2}
bounds

(2.13)

|n_{3,++-}(\underline{\xi})-n_{3,++-}(\underline{\eta})|\lesssim\big|\underline{\xi}-\underline{\eta}\big|^{1/2}(|\underline{\xi}|+|\underline{\eta}|)^{2}\qquad\text{ for any }\underline{\xi},\underline{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

2.3.
Initial data and energy estimate

Recall the initial data
u_{\mathrm{in}}
given by (
1.3
).
By (
2.7
)

(2.14)

w(0)=w_{\mathrm{in}}=u_{\mathrm{in}}+\mathcal{A}_{2}(u_{\mathrm{in}})+\mathcal{A}_{3}(u_{\mathrm{in}}).

We start with some general bounds on products of functions with random Fourier coefficients.

Proposition 2.2
.

(i) [Multilinear Gaussian estimates] Assume that
g_{k}:\Omega\to\mathbb{C}
,
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, are i.i.d. normalized complex Gaussian random variables on a probability space
\Omega
and define

(2.15)

G(\omega):=\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}}a_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}g_{k_{i}}^{\iota_{i}}(\omega),

where
r\geq 1
,
\omega\in\Omega
,
\iota_{j}\in\{+,-\}
, and
a_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}}\in\mathbb{C}
. Then, for any
A\geq 1

(2.16)

\mathbb{P}\big\{\omega:\,|G(\omega)|\geq A\|G(\omega)\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}\big\}\leq Ce^{-A^{2/r}/C},

for some constant
C\geq 1
.

(ii) Assume that
\mathcal{M}
is an
r
-linear operator defined by

(2.17)

\mathcal{F}\big[\mathcal{M}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{r})\big](\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{r-1}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{r}=\xi}m(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})\widehat{f}_{1}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{r}}(\eta_{r}),

with an associated symbol
m
satisfying

(2.18)

|m(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{r})|\leq(1+|\eta_{1}|+\ldots+|\eta_{r}|)^{A},

for some
A\geq 0
. Let
f=f(\omega)
be a random function with Fourier coefficients
\widehat{f}(k)=R^{1/2}\varphi(k)\cdot g_{k}(\omega)
with i.i.d Gaussians
g_{k}
, where
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
|\varphi(x)|\leq(1+|x|)^{-A^{\prime}}
,
A^{\prime}\geq A+r+2
.
Then, for
\eta\in(0,1/10)
and
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{r}\in\{+,-\}
, with high probability
\geq 1-e^{-R^{\eta/r}}
one has

(2.19)

\|\mathcal{M}(f^{\iota_{1}},\dots,f^{\iota_{r}})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}+R^{-1/2}\|\mathcal{M}(f^{\iota_{1}},\dots,f^{\iota_{r}})\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\lesssim R^{\eta}.

(iii) In particular, there is a set
\Omega^{\prime}\subseteq\Omega
with
\mathbb{P}(\Omega\setminus\Omega^{\prime})\leq e^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
such that for all
\omega\in\Omega^{\prime}
the initial data
u_{in}
defined in (
1.3
) satisfies the bounds

(2.20)

\|u_{in}\|_{H^{N_{0}+4}}+\|w_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{H^{N_{0}+4}}\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2},\quad\|u_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{\dot{W}^{8,0}}+\|w_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{\dot{W}^{8,0}}\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}.

Proof.

(i) The general bounds (
2.16
) follow by the hypercontractivity estimate for Ornstein-
Uhlenbeck semigroups; see for example
[
17
]
and references therein.

(ii) Let

(2.21)

\begin{split}G(x,\omega)&:=\langle\nabla\rangle_{x}\mathcal{M}(f^{\iota_{1}},\dots,f^{\iota_{r}})(x)\\
&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{r}}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}/R}e^{ix(k_{1}+\ldots+k_{r})}m(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})(1+|k_{1}|^{2}+\ldots+|k_{r}|^{2})^{1/2}\\
&\times\varphi(k_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi(k_{r})\cdot R^{r/2}g_{\iota_{1}k_{1}}^{\iota_{1}}(\omega)\cdot\ldots\cdot g_{\iota_{r}k_{r}}^{\iota_{r}}(\omega).\end{split}

For any
x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}
fixed we use the bounds (
2.18
) and the decay assumption on
\varphi
to estimate

\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}^{2}\lesssim_{r}R^{-r}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in(\mathbb{Z}/R)^{r}}(1+|k|)^{-r-1}(1+|k^{\prime}|)^{-r-1}\int_{\Omega}\prod_{a,b\in\{1,\ldots,r\}}g_{\iota_{a}k_{a}}^{\iota_{a}}(\omega)\overline{g_{\iota_{b}k_{b}}^{\iota_{b}}(\omega)}\,d\omega.

Notice that, due to independence, the variables
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r},k^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,k^{\prime}_{r}
must come in pairs in order for the
\omega
-integral to be nonzero. Thus the summation over
k,k^{\prime}
contributes a factor
O(R^{r})
, and it follows that

(2.22)

\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}\lesssim 1\qquad\text{ for any }x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}.

It follows from (
2.16
) that for any
x\in\mathbb{T}_{R}
and
A\geq 1

\mathbb{P}\big\{\omega:\,|G(x,\omega)|\geq A\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{2}_{\omega}}\big\}\leq Ce^{-A^{2/r}/C},

for some constant
C\geq 1
. Therefore, using also (
2.22
),

\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}}\lesssim_{r}p^{r/2}\qquad\text{ for any }x\in\mathbb{T}_{R},\,p\in[2,\infty).

In particular
\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{p}_{\omega}L^{p}_{x}}\lesssim_{r}p^{r/2}R^{1/p}
for any
p\in[2,\infty)
, so

R^{\eta}\mathbb{P}\big\{w:\,\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\geq R^{\eta}\big\}^{1/p}\lesssim_{r}p^{r/2}R^{1/p}.

We apply this with
p=\eta^{-2}R^{\eta/r}
, so
|\mathbb{P}\big\{w:\,\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}\geq R^{\eta}\big\}|\leq e^{-R^{\eta/r}}
for
R
sufficiently large. The
L^{\infty}
bound in (
2.19
) follows since
\|\mathcal{M}(f^{\iota_{1}},\dots,f^{\iota_{r}})\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\lesssim\|G(x,\omega)\|_{L^{p}_{x}}
for any
p\in[2,\infty]
and
\omega\in\Omega
. The
L^{2}
bound in (
2.19
) then follows by integration.
∎

Next, we recall the following energy estimate proved in
[
22
]
:

Theorem 2.3

(Energy increment)
.

Assume that
N_{2}\geq 6
,
N_{\infty}\in[4,N_{2}-2]
,
t_{1}\leq t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
,
and
(h,\phi)\in C([t_{1},t_{2}]:H^{N_{2}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{2},1/2})

is a solution of the system (
1.1
). Let

(2.23)

\displaystyle B:=\frac{G(h)\phi+\partial_{x}h\partial_{x}\phi}{1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2}},\qquad\omega:=\phi-T_{B}h,

see (
2.5
) for the definition of the paraproduct
T_{a}b
. Assume that

(2.24)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|h(t)\|_{H^{N_{2}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{H^{N_{2}}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{H^{N_{2}-1/2}}\leq A,\\
&\|h(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty},0}}+\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-1/2,0}}\leq\varepsilon(t),\end{split}

for any
t\in[t_{1},t_{2}]
, where
A\in(0,\infty)
and
\varepsilon:[t_{1},t_{2}]\to[0,\infty)

is a continuous function satisfying
\varepsilon(t)\ll 1
.
Then there is a constant
C_{N_{2}}\geq 1
such that

(2.25)

\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{2})\big\|_{H^{N_{2}}}^{2}\leq C_{N_{2}}\big\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t_{1})\big\|_{H^{N_{2}}}^{2}+C_{N_{2}}A^{2}\int_{t_{1}}^{t_{2}}[\varepsilon(s)]^{3}\,ds.

2.4.
Propagation of randomness and main bootstrap

The key step in the proof of Theorem
1.1
is the following result,
which shows propagation of randomness in the form of decay of an
L^{\infty}
-type norm:

Proposition 2.4

(Propagation of randomness)
.

Recall
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
and
\theta=\theta_{0}/100
. With probability
1-3e^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
the following statement is true: assume
t\in[0,T_{1}]
and
u=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi\in C([0,t]:H^{N_{0}})
is a solution of the system (
1.1
),
w
is defined as in (
2.7
), and

(2.26)

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big[\|u(s)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\|w(s)\|_{H^{N_{0}-2}}\big]\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2},\quad\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big[\|u(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{6,0}}+\|w(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\big]\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}.

Then

(2.27)

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\|w(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}.

The proof of Proposition
2.4
will occupy most of the the paper,
and will follow from the main steps described in Section
3
. We show now that Proposition
2.4
can be used as a black box, together with Propositions
2.1
,
2.2
(ii) and Theorem
2.3
, to obtain Theorem
1.1
.

Proposition 2.5

(Main Bootstrap)
.

Assume
N_{0},T_{1},u=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi
are as in Theorem
1.1
and define
\omega
as in (
2.23
). Let
N_{1}:=6
and assume that initially
u(0)=u_{\mathrm{in}}
is a random function satisfying (
1.3
). Then, with probability at least
1-4e^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
, the following statement holds: assume
t\in[0,T_{1}]
and
u=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi\in C([0,t]:H^{N_{0}})
is a solution of the system (
1.1
) satisfying

(2.28)

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\Big(\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}+1/2}}+\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2},

(2.29)

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\Big(\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}+1/2,0}}+\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}.

Then

(2.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\Big(\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}+1/2}}+\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{1-3\theta/4}R^{1/2},

(2.31)

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\Big(\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}+1/2,0}}+\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1},0}}\Big)

\displaystyle\lesssim\epsilon^{1-3\theta/4}.

In particular, Theorem
1.1
holds.

Proof.

For convenience let us introduce

(2.32)

v:=h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega=u-i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}T_{B}h,

and recall that from
[
22
, Lemma 3.1]
we have

(2.33)

\displaystyle\|B(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}-1}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2},\qquad\|B(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}+\sum_{k\geq 0}2^{(N_{1}-1)k}\|P_{k}B(t^{\prime})\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta},

for all
t^{\prime}\in[0,t]
. Recall the definition (
2.7
) of the variable
w=u+\mathcal{A}_{2}(u)+\mathcal{A}_{3}(u)

and that the multilinear operators
\mathcal{A}_{r}
have symbols satisfying (
2.12
). In view of Lemma
7.1
we have

(2.34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\|\mathcal{A}_{r}(u)\|}_{H^{N_{0}-r+1}}\lesssim R^{1/2}\epsilon^{r(1-\theta)},\qquad{\|\mathcal{A}_{r}(u)\|}_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}-r+1,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{r(1-\theta)}.\end{split}

for
r=2,3
.

The bounds (
2.20
) on
u_{in}
, the bounds (
2.33
), and the definition
\omega:=\phi-T_{B}h
show that

(2.35)

\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(0)\|_{H^{N_{0}+2}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2}.

We apply now Theorem
2.3
, use the bootstrap assumption (
2.28
)–(
2.29
), and recall that
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
to see that

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}+1/2}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2}.

Since
\phi=\omega+T_{B}h
, it follows using (
2.33
) that
\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2}
. Thus

(2.36)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\Big(\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\omega)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}+1/2}}+\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\Big)\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2},

which, in particular, implies the desired bounds (
2.30
).

To prove the pointwise bounds (
2.31
) we notice that
\|w(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{1}-2,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta}
and
\|w(t^{\prime})\|_{H^{N_{0}-2}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}
, due to the assumptions (
2.29
) and the bounds (
2.34
). The hypothesis (
2.26
) of Proposition
2.4
is therefore satisfied, so we can apply the proposition to conclude that

(2.37)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}.

Since
u=w-\mathcal{A}_{2}(u)-\mathcal{A}_{3}(u)
, it follows using also (
2.34
) that

(2.38)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|u(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}.

Using the inequality
\|u\|_{H^{N_{0}}}(t^{\prime})\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2}
proved in (
2.36
) we can further estimate

\begin{split}\|u(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{8,0}}&=\sum_{k\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}}\|P_{k}u\|_{L^{\infty}}(2^{8k}+1)\\
&\lesssim\sum_{k\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}},\,k\leq K_{0}}2^{4K_{0}}\|P_{k}u\|_{L^{\infty}}(2^{4k}+1)+\sum_{k\geq K_{0}}\|P_{k}u\|_{L^{2}}2^{N_{0}k}2^{(9-N_{0})k}\\
&\lesssim 2^{4K_{0}}\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}+2^{(9-N_{0})K_{0}}\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}R^{1/2},\end{split}

for any
K_{0}\geq 0
. We fix now
K_{0}
such that
2^{N_{0}K_{0}}\approx R^{1/2}
. Since
R^{5/N_{0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{-\theta_{0}/4}
(recall the assumptions
\epsilon\leq R^{-\theta_{0}}
and
N_{0}\geq\theta^{-3})
it follows that
\|u\|_{\dot{W}^{8,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-3\theta/4}
, which suffices to prove the desired bounds (
2.31
).
∎

3.
Propagation of randomness: Proof of Proposition
2.4

In this section we break down the proof of Proposition
2.4
into several steps. It is here that we need to introduce
probabilistic and combinatorial tools, including Feynman trees, couples, and renormalization.

We begin with a first approximation of the normal form variable
w
, which we denote by
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
, which is obtained by truncating the equations in homogeneity and projecting away all high-frequencies;
see Proposition
3.1
.

Then we introduce the renormalized interaction coefficients
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)
, see the definition (
3.18
),
obtained by renormalizing the Fourier coefficients of
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
by the phase
\Gamma

(and applying some convenient time and amplitude rescaling).
The introduction of this renormalization is one of the main new ingredients in our proof. Note how at this initial stage
\Gamma
is composed of a term
\Gamma_{0}=\Gamma_{0}(k)
which takes into account
the standard trivial resonances of the form
(k,k,k_{1},k_{1})
,
and a yet-to-be-determined time-frequency dependent contribution
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{1}(t,k)
. We note also that the functions
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{1}
are deterministic, i.e. they do not depend on the choice of the random coefficients
g_{k}(\omega)
.

Then, from the Duhamel’s formula (
3.24
) associated to
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}

we construct our main approximation, denoted
w_{\mathrm{app}}
, by long Feynman trees expansions (depending on the parameter
\theta_{0}
), leading to the formulas (
3.33
)-(
3.35
) and (
3.30
).
Note that our definitions of trees and other combinatorial objects (as well as the estimates in later sections)
need to take into account the normal form transformation which,
in particular, implies that the initial data for
w
(and
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
)
is not a standard Gaussian sum as in the case of the original physical variable
u
.

In Subsection
3.5
we introduce the expressions for couples,
which will be the main object to be estimated,
and define the renormalizing phase
\Gamma_{1}
by a fixed point identity.

Subsection
3.6
contains the statements of all the main estimates:
Proposition
3.8
gives the main bounds for trees/couples, and for
\Gamma_{1}
,
while Proposition
3.9
estimates the difference between the approximate solution’s
profile
{(a_{k})}_{\mathrm{app}}
and the truncated solution’s profile
{(a_{k})}_{\mathrm{tr}}
.

Finally in Subsection
3.7
we give an estimate for oscillatory integrals
involving multilinear phases obtained from the modified time oscillations
|k|^{1/2}t+\Gamma(t,k)
to be used in the rest of the arguments,
and in Subsection
3.8
we provide some basic counting estimate for modified quadratic and cubic phases.

3.1.
Frequency and homogeneity truncation

For the rest of this paper we fix one more parameter
A:=\lfloor\theta^{-1}\rfloor
, and then we fix

K_{\mathrm{tr}}:=\lfloor\log_{2}(R^{1/A})\rfloor.

With the notation in Proposition
2.1
, we define
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
as the solution to the Cauchy problem

(3.1)

(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})w_{\mathrm{tr}}=\sum_{r=3}^{A}P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{r}(w_{\mathrm{tr}})\big],\qquad w_{\mathrm{tr}}(0)=w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}},

where
P_{\leq K}
denote the standard Littlewood-Paley projections (as defined in section
2.1
), and

(3.2)

\displaystyle w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}:=P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}}+P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}})+P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{A}_{3}(P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}}).

The equation (
3.1
) is obtained by truncating the higher order nonlinearities,
as well as all projecting away high frequencies.
The following proposition shows how to control the difference between the real solution
w

and the truncated solution
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
:

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume that
t\leq T_{1}
and
u\in C([0,t]:H^{N_{0}})
is a solution of the system (
1.1
) satisfying the assumption (
2.26
)

(3.3)

\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big[\|u(s)\|_{H^{N_{0}}}+\|w(s)\|_{H^{N_{0}-2}}\big]\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2},\quad\sup_{s\in[0,t]}\big[\|u(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{6,0}}+\|w(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\big]\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}.

Recall the nonlinearities
\mathcal{N}_{r}(w)=\mathcal{N}_{r}(w,\ldots,w)
defined in (
2.9
) and define

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{r}^{j}(F)[G]=\mathcal{N}_{r}(F,\dots,G,\dots,F),\quad 0\leq j\leq r,

where the function
G
appears in the
j-
th position and the other
r-1
arguments are
F
.

In addition to (
3.3
), we assume that any solution
W
to the linear equation with forcing

(3.5)

(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})W=\sum_{r=3}^{A}\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{N}_{r}^{j}(w_{\mathrm{tr}})[W]+\mathcal{R}

satisfies the bounds

(3.6)

\|W\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}\dot{W}^{4,0}_{x}}\lesssim R^{8}(\|W(0)\|_{H^{5}}+\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}H^{5}_{x}}).

Then, as a conclusion we have that

(3.7)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w(t^{\prime})-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\leq\epsilon^{A/4}.

The above proposition has been formulated in a way to keep the logic of the proof simpler and separate the deterministic parts of the argument and the probabilistic ones.
However, the claim that the bounds (
3.6
) hold for all linear solutions of the equation (
3.5
) (which is an implicit claim about the function
w_{tr}
) relies on
probabilistic arguments, and therefore holds for a set of large probability.
The proof of (
3.6
) is given at the end of Section
6
.

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

Recall that
w
satisfies the equation (
2.8
) with
w(0)=w_{\mathrm{in}}
satisfying the bounds in Proposition
2.2
,
while
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
solves the equation (
3.1
).
Then, their difference
W=w-w_{\mathrm{tr}}
satisfies the equation (
3.5
) with

(3.8)

\displaystyle W(0)=w_{\mathrm{in}}-w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}

and

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{R}&:=\mathcal{N}_{>A}(u)+\sum_{r=3}^{A}P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{N}_{r}(w)+\sum_{r=3}^{A}P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{r}(w)-\mathcal{N}_{r}(w_{\mathrm{tr}})-\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}\mathcal{N}_{r}^{j}\big(w_{\mathrm{tr}}\big)[W]\Big).\end{split}

We bound first the contribution of the initial data, i.e. we show that

(3.10)

\displaystyle{\big\|w_{\mathrm{in}}-w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\big\|}_{H^{5}}\lesssim\epsilon^{2A/3}.

For this we estimate

\displaystyle{\big\|w_{\mathrm{in}}-w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}\big\|}_{H^{5}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\big\|P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}}\|}_{H^{5}}+\sum_{r=2,3}{\big\|\mathcal{A}_{r}(u_{\mathrm{in}})-P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{A}_{r}(P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}})\big\|}_{H^{5}}.

Since
\|u_{\mathrm{in}}\|_{H^{N_{0}}}\leq\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}

(see (
3.3
)) we can estimate

(3.11)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\big\|P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}}\|}_{H^{5}}&\lesssim(R^{1/A})^{-(N_{0}-5)}{\|u_{\mathrm{in}}\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}\lesssim R^{-A}\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}.\end{split}

Moreover, by writing
\mathcal{A}_{2}(f)=\mathcal{A}_{2}(f,f)
(with the natural definition of the bilinear operator
\mathcal{A}_{2}
)
and using Lemma
7.7
(ii) and the bounds (
2.12
), we can estimate

(3.12)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\big\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(u_{\mathrm{in}})-P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}})\big\|}_{H^{5}}\\
&\lesssim{\big\|P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{A}_{2}(u_{\mathrm{in}},u_{\mathrm{in}})\big\|}_{H^{5}}+{\big\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}},u_{\mathrm{in}})\big\|}_{H^{5}}+{\big\|\mathcal{A}_{2}(P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}},P_{>K_{\mathrm{tr}}}u_{\mathrm{in}})\big\|}_{H^{5}}\\
&\lesssim(R^{1/A})^{-(N_{0}-10)}{\|u_{\mathrm{in}}\|}_{H^{N_{0}}}{\|u_{\mathrm{in}}\|}_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim R^{-A}\epsilon^{2-2\theta}R^{1/2}.\end{split}

A similar argument can be used to obtain similar bounds for the contribution of the cubic term
\mathcal{A}_{3}(u_{in})
. The desired bounds (
3.10
) follow.

Using now the assumption (
3.6
) we would like to prove the following bootstrap estimate:
if

(3.13)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w(t^{\prime})-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\leq 2\epsilon^{A/4}

then

(3.14)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w(t^{\prime})-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\leq\epsilon^{A/4}.

We need to bound
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{L^{\infty}_{t}H^{5}_{x}}
. The first two terms in (
3.9
) can be directly estimated using the estimates in the last line of (
2.10
) and the apriori assumptions (
3.3
): for any
s\in[0,t]

(3.15)

\displaystyle\big\|\mathcal{N}_{>A}(u(s))\big\|_{H^{5}}\lesssim{\|w(s)\|}_{H^{N_{0}-2}}{\|w(s)\|}_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}^{A}\lesssim R^{1/2}\cdot(\epsilon^{1-\theta})^{A+1}\lesssim\epsilon^{2A/3},

and

(3.16)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|P_{>K_{tr}}\mathcal{N}_{r}(w(s))\|_{H^{5}}\lesssim R^{-A/2}\|\mathcal{N}_{r}(w(s))\|_{H^{N_{0}/2}}\lesssim R^{-A/3}.\end{split}

Finally, we estimate the last term in (
3.9
). Observe that the expression
in the square parenthesis is a linear combination of terms
of the form
\mathcal{N}_{r}(w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{r})
such that there exists
1\leq i\neq j\leq r

with
w_{i}=w_{j}=w-w_{\mathrm{tr}}
,
and, for
k\neq i,j
,
w_{k}\in\{w,w_{\mathrm{tr}},w-w_{\mathrm{tr}}\}
.
For each
r=3,\dots,A
, we can use (
2.9
), the
S^{\infty}
bounds (
2.11
),
and the product estimates in Lemma
7.7
(ii) to bound

(3.17)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Big\|P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\big[\mathcal{N}_{r}(w(s))-\mathcal{N}_{r}(w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s))-\sum_{1\leq j\leq r}\mathcal{N}_{r}^{j}\big(w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s),\dots,w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s),\dots,w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\big]\Big\|_{H^{5}}\\
&\lesssim\big\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\big\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\cdot\big(\|w(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}+\|w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\big)^{r-3}\\
&\qquad\times\Big[\big\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\big\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\big(\|w(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}+\|w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}\big)\\
&\qquad+\big\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\big\|_{H^{r+5}}\big(\|w(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}+\|w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\big)\Big]\\
&\lesssim\epsilon^{A/4}\epsilon^{(1-\theta)(r-3)}\Big[\epsilon^{A/4}\cdot(\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}+\|w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{H^{r+5}})+\epsilon^{1-\theta}\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}\Big].\end{split}

Next, we use
w_{\mathrm{tr}}=P_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}+2}w_{\mathrm{tr}}
and the bounds (
3.3
) and (
3.13
)
to see that

\displaystyle\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|P_{\leq K_{tr}+2}[w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)]\|_{H^{r+5}}+\|P_{>K_{tr}+2}w(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}

\displaystyle\lesssim R^{2}\epsilon^{A/4}+R^{-A}\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}.

In particular
\|w_{tr}(s)\|_{H^{r+5}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta}R^{1/2}
(since
\epsilon^{A/4}\leq R^{-25}
due to the assumption
\epsilon\leq R^{-\theta_{0}}
), so the right-hand side of (
3.17
) is bounded by
C\epsilon^{A/2}R^{2}
.
Using also (
3.15
)–(
3.16
) it follows that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{H^{5}}\lesssim\epsilon^{A/2}R^{2}
.
Using the assumption (
3.6
) it follows that
\|w(s)-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{A/2}R^{10}
for any
s\in[0,t]
, which implies the desired bootstrap bounds (
3.14
).
∎

3.2.
Renormalization

We define the
renormalized profile

(3.18)

(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)=(R^{1/2}\epsilon)^{-1}\cdot e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}s+\Gamma(s,k))}\cdot\widehat{w_{\mathrm{tr}}}(T_{1}s,k),

for
(s,k)\in[0,1]\times\mathbb{Z}_{R}
, where
\mathbb{Z}_{R}:=\mathbb{Z}/R
and
\Gamma\in C_{s}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}([0,1]\times\mathbb{Z}_{R})
is a (deterministic) renormalization factor to be defined below. The equation (
3.1
) becomes

(3.19)

\partial_{s}(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)=iT_{1}\partial_{s}\Gamma(s,k)(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)+(R^{1/2}\epsilon)^{-1}e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}s+\Gamma(s,k))}\cdot T_{1}\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{tr}}}(T_{1}s,k),

where
\mathcal{N}_{tr}
is the nonlinearity in the right-hand side of (
3.1
). Using (
2.9
) we can write

\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{N}_{\mathrm{tr}}}(T_{1}s,k)&=\sum_{r=3}^{A}\frac{\varphi_{\leq K_{tr}}(k)}{(2\pi R)^{r-1}}\sum_{\iota_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R},\,\iota_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k}\negmedspace n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\iota_{1}k_{1},\ldots,\iota_{r}k_{r})\prod_{j=1}^{r}\widehat{w_{\mathrm{tr}}}(T_{1}s,k_{j})^{\iota_{j}}\\
&=\sum_{r=3}^{A}\frac{\varphi_{\leq K_{tr}}(k)}{(2\pi R)^{r-1}}\sum_{\iota_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k}^{\ast}n_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\iota_{1}k_{1},\ldots,\iota_{r}k_{r})(R^{1/2}\epsilon)^{r}\\
&\qquad\qquad\prod_{j=1}^{r}\Big\{[(a_{k_{j}})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)]^{\iota_{j}}e^{-iT_{1}\iota_{j}(|k_{j}|^{1/2}s+\Gamma(s,k_{j}))}\Big\},\end{split}

where, for simplicity of notation,
\sum\limits_{\iota_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k}^{\ast}
denotes the sum over all the signs
\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{r}\in\{+,-\}
and all
k_{1},\ldots,k_{r}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}
satisfying
\iota_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k
. The formula (
3.19
) becomes

(3.20)

\begin{split}\partial_{s}(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)&=iT_{1}\partial_{s}\Gamma(s,k)(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)+\sum_{r=3}^{A}\varphi_{\leq K_{tr}}(k)(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r-1}T_{1}\\
&\times\sum_{\iota_{1}k_{1}+\ldots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k}^{\ast}i\widetilde{n}_{\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{r}(s,k;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}[(a_{k_{j}})_{\mathrm{tr}}(s)]^{\iota_{j}},\end{split}

where

(3.21)

\begin{split}\widetilde{n}_{\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})&:=-in_{r,\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(\iota_{1}k_{1},\ldots,\iota_{r}k_{r})(2\pi)^{-r+1},\\
\Omega_{r}(s,k;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})&:=s\big[|k|^{1/2}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}|k_{j}|^{1/2}\big]+\big[\Gamma(s,k)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}\Gamma(s,k_{j})\big].\end{split}

We are looking for renormalization factors
\Gamma
of the form

(3.22)

\displaystyle\Gamma({t},k)=\int_{0}^{t}(\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k)+T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k))\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}=\epsilon^{2}{t}\cdot\Gamma_{0}(k)+T_{1}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k)\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}.

where, with
n_{3}:=\widetilde{n}_{++-}
,

(3.23)

\Gamma_{0}(k):=-R^{-1}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}2n_{3}(k_{1},k,k_{1})\psi(k_{1})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{1})^{2},

and
\Gamma_{1}({t},k)\in C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}([0,1]\times\mathbb{Z}_{R})
will be defined implicitly in Proposition
3.7
below to achieve a higher order cancellation. Then

(3.24)

\displaystyle(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}({t})=R^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\widehat{w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)+i\sum_{r=3}^{A}(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r-1}T_{1}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\iota_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\iota_{r}k_{r}=k}^{\ast}\widetilde{n}_{\iota_{1}\ldots\iota_{r}}(k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\int_{0}^{t}e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{r}(t^{\prime},k;k_{1},\ldots,k_{r})}\prod_{j=1}^{r}(a_{k_{j}})_{\mathrm{tr}}({t}^{\prime})^{\zeta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle+iT_{1}\int_{0}^{t}(\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k)+T_{1}^{-1}\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k))(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}({t}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime},

where the resonance factors
\Omega_{r}
in (
3.24
) are defined as

(3.25)

\begin{split}\Omega_{r}({t},k;k_{1},\cdots,k_{r}):=\Big[|k|^{1/2}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}|k_{j}|^{1/2}&\Big]\cdot{t}+\epsilon^{2}\Big[\Gamma_{0}(k)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}\Gamma_{0}(k_{j})\Big]\cdot{t}\\
&+T_{1}^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k_{j})\Big]\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}.\end{split}

3.3.
Duhamel expansion, trees and couples

We shall expand the solution
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
to (
3.1
), or equivalently the solution
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)
to (
3.24
), using Duhamel iterations; the terms occurring in such expansions are naturally indexed by trees, and correlations between them naturally correspond to couples, which are two trees with their leaves paired. Therefore we start by introducing these combinatorial structures.

Definition 3.2
.

Define a
tree

\mathcal{T}
to be a rooted tree with two kinds of branching nodes: the interaction or I-branching nodes, and the normal form or N-branching nodes. We assume that each I-branching node has at least
3
and at most
A
children nodes; moreover each N-branching node has either 2 or 3 children nodes, and all of them are leaves. Define the order
n=n(\mathcal{T})
(resp.
n_{I}=n_{I}(\mathcal{T})
and
n_{N}=n_{N}(\mathcal{T})
) to be the number of branching nodes (resp. number of I- and N-branching nodes) and the rank
r=r(\mathcal{T})
to be the number of leaves. We also refer to
n_{I}(\mathcal{T})
and
r(\mathcal{T})
as the
order
and
rank
of the tree
\mathcal{T}
.

For each tree
\mathcal{T}
, we also choose a sign
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\{\pm\}
for each node
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{T}
. We require that for any (I- or N-) branching node
\mathfrak{n}
, the children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
that have the same sign as
\mathfrak{n}
must occur to the left of those that have opposite sign as
\mathfrak{n}
. The sign of the root
\mathfrak{r}
of
\mathcal{T}
is called the sign of
\mathcal{T}
.

We may also turn certain leaves of a given tree
\mathcal{T}
into pairs, such that each pair contains two leaves of opposite sign; the resulting structure is called a
paired tree
, and we denote the pairing structure by
\mathcal{P}
. Finally, given two trees
(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
such that
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}
has sign
\pm
and the total number of leaves is even, we may
partition
the set of leaves of
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}
into two-element opposite-sign pairs, to form a
couple

\mathcal{Q}
. Note that a couple
\mathcal{Q}
naturally turns each tree
\mathcal{T}^{\pm}
into a paired tree.

For any tree, paired tree or couple, we shall use the letter
\mathfrak{r}
,
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{m}
and
\mathfrak{l}
to respectively denote the root(s), arbitrary nodes, arbitrary branching nodes and arbitrary leaves. For any branching node
\mathfrak{m}
, denote all its children nodes by
\mathfrak{m}_{1},\cdots,\mathfrak{m}_{r}
from left to right. The sets
\mathcal{B}
,
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{N}
and
\mathcal{L}
denote the sets of branching nodes, I- and N-branching nodes, and leaves.

Definition 3.3

(Admissibility)
.

Given a paired tree
\mathcal{T}
, we say
\mathcal{T}
is
admissible
if for any I-branching node
\mathfrak{n}

with exactly
3
children nodes and any two children nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{1},\mathfrak{n}_{2}
of
\mathfrak{n}
with opposite signs,
the subtrees rooted as
\mathfrak{n}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{2}

must not
have their leaves completely paired.

Definition 3.4
.

Given complex Gaussian random variables
(X_{1},\cdots,X_{m})
and
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
for
1\leq j\leq m
, suppose that any two
X_{j}
are either equal or independent, define (inductively) the renormalized product

(3.26)

\mathfrak{R}(X_{1}^{\zeta_{1}}\cdots X_{m}^{\zeta_{m}}):=X_{1}^{\zeta_{1}}\cdots X_{m}^{\zeta_{m}}-\sum_{\mathcal{P}\neq\varnothing}\prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal{P}}\mathbb{E}(X_{i}^{\zeta_{i}}X_{j}^{\zeta_{j}})\cdot\mathfrak{R}\bigg(\prod_{j\in O}X_{j}^{\zeta_{j}}\bigg),

where
\mathcal{P}
runs over all pairing structures of
\{1,\cdots,m\}
, i.e. collections of disjoint two-element subsets, such that
\zeta_{i}=-\zeta_{j}
for each
\{i,j\}\in\mathcal{P}
, and
O
is the set of elements in
\{1,\cdots,m\}
not in any pairing in
\mathcal{P}
. For example, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}(XY\overline{Z}\overline{W})

\displaystyle=XY\overline{Z}\overline{W}-\mathbb{E}(X\overline{Z})Y\overline{W}-\mathbb{E}(X\overline{W})Y\overline{Z}-\mathbb{E}(Y\overline{W})X\overline{Z}-\mathbb{E}(Y\overline{Z})X\overline{W}

\displaystyle+2\mathbb{E}(X\overline{Z})\mathbb{E}(Y\overline{W})+2\mathbb{E}(X\overline{W})\mathbb{E}(Y\overline{Z}).

Definition 3.5
.

For any paired tree
\mathcal{T}
or couple
\mathcal{Q}
, define a
decoration
of it to be a vector
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
, where
\mathfrak{n}
runs over all nodes of
\mathcal{T}
or
\mathcal{Q}
, such that
k_{\mathfrak{l}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
for any two paired leaves
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}
, and

(3.27)

\iota_{\mathfrak{m}}k_{\mathfrak{m}}=\sum_{j}\iota_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}k_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}

for any branching node
\mathfrak{m}
of
\mathcal{T}
or
\mathcal{Q}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
are all children nodes of
\mathfrak{m}
. We call it a
k
-decoration if
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k
where
\mathfrak{r}
is the root. For any decoration
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
and any I-branching node
\mathfrak{m}
with children nodes
\mathfrak{m}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
, and any time variable
{t}_{\mathfrak{m}}
associated to
\mathfrak{m}
, define

(3.28)

\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}=\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}};k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}):=\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot\Omega_{r}({t}_{\mathfrak{m}},k_{\mathfrak{m}};k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})

where
\Omega_{r}
is defined in (
3.25
) but with the corresponding signs
\zeta_{j}
replaced by
\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}\cdot\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Definition 3.6
.

For any decorated tree
\mathcal{T}
and any decorated couple
\mathcal{Q}
we define the integral

(3.29)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}}):=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}{t}_{\mathfrak{m}},\\
&\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{G}}:=\big\{(t_{\mathfrak{m}})_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}:0<{t}_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}<{t}_{\mathfrak{m}}<{t}\text{ whenever }\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}\mathrm{\ is\ a\ child\ of\ }\mathfrak{m}\big\},\end{split}

where
\mathcal{G}\in\{\mathcal{T},\mathcal{Q}\}
. For each paired tree
\mathcal{T}
with pairing structure
\mathcal{P}
, define the expression

(3.30)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}({t})

\displaystyle:=(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{T})-1}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{T})}\sum_{(k_{\mathfrak{n}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{T}}\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}})\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{P}}^{(+)}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{P}}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{h}})^{1/2}\Re\Big(\prod_{\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{P}}g_{k_{\mathfrak{h}}}^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{h}}}\Big)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}[i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})]^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}}\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}}),

where

(3.31)

\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}):=a_{r,\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}).

Here
n_{I}(\mathcal{T})
and
r(\mathcal{T})
are the order and rank of
\mathcal{T}
as in Definition
3.2
,
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
runs over all
k
-decorations of
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathfrak{n}
,
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{h}
run over all nodes, all paired leaves of sign
+
, and all unpaired leaves respectively,
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{N}
denote the sets of I-branching and N-branching nodes of
\mathcal{T}
, and the Wick product
\Re
is as in Definition
3.4
.

3.4.
The approximate solution

We now define the approximate solution
w_{\mathrm{app}}
,
which is an approximation to
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
and approximately solves the equation (
3.1
) as follows: let

(3.32)

(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}({t})=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}({t}),

where
\mathcal{T}
runs over all admissible
4
4

4

We emphasize that it is important in the proof that the sum is taken only over the admissible trees, see Definition
3.3
. The main role of the renormalization coefficient
\Gamma_{1}
is to eliminate the higher order contributions of the paired trees that are not admissible.
paired trees of rank
r(\mathcal{T})\leq N:=\lfloor\theta^{-2}\rfloor
that have sign
+
, and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
is defined in (
3.30
). Let

(3.33)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&w_{\mathrm{app}}(t,x):=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}e^{ik\cdot x}\widehat{w_{\mathrm{app}}}(t,k),\\
&(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=R^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\cdot e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}t+\Gamma(t,k))}\cdot\widehat{w_{\mathrm{app}}}(T_{1}t,k).\end{split}

(which is the same relation between
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
in (
3.18
)).
We also define

(3.34)

(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}({t})=\sum_{r(\mathcal{T})=r}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}({t}),

where the sum is taken over all admissible paired trees of rank
r
that have sign
+
, so we have

(3.35)

(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}({t})=\sum_{r=1}^{N}(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}({t}).

We remark that all these definitions depend on the choice of the second renormalization
\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma_{1}(t,k)
defined in Section
3.2
; in fact, this
\Gamma_{1}(t,k)
will be constructed by a fixed point equation, see Proposition
3.7
below.

3.5.
Expressions
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
for couples
\mathcal{Q}

A major part of the proof in this paper is to estimate the second moment

\mathbb{E}|(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}(t)|^{2}
, which naturally leads to the study of the quantity
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
for couples
\mathcal{Q}
. Namely, for any couple
\mathcal{Q}
we define

(3.36)

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k)

\displaystyle:=(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{Q})-2}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{Q})}\sum_{(k_{\mathfrak{n}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}}^{(+)}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}[i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})]^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}}\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}})\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}}\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\ldots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\ldots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}}).

Here
n_{I}(\mathcal{Q})
and
r(\mathcal{Q})
are the order and rank of
\mathcal{Q}
, and
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
runs over all
k
-decorations of
\mathcal{Q}
. Moreover
\mathfrak{n}
and
\mathfrak{l}
run over all nodes and all leaves with sign
+
respectively, and
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined as in (
3.29
). Note that the definition (
3.36
) depends on the choice of
\Gamma_{1}
in Section
3.2
; the following proposition allow us to fix a unique choice of this
\Gamma_{1}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

There exists a unique
\Gamma_{1}\in C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}([0,1]\times\mathbb{Z}_{R})
with
\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1
,
such that

(3.37)

\Gamma_{1}(t,k)=\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot R^{-1}\sum_{k_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}(-2)n_{3}(k_{1},k,k_{1})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{1})^{2}\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k_{1}),

where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is defined as in (
3.36
) (which depends on
\Gamma_{1}
itself),
and the summation is taken over all nontrivial admissible couples with the rank of each tree being at most
N
.

3.6.
Main estimates

To prove Proposition
2.4

we will need estimates of the approximate solution
w_{\mathrm{app}}
, as well as its relation to the actual solution
w
.
As Proposition
3.1
already provides the estimate of
w-w_{\mathrm{tr}}
, now we only need to control the error
w_{\mathrm{tr}}-w_{\mathrm{app}}
(or equivalently
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}-(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}
). These estimates are summarized in the following propositions.

Proposition 3.8
.

Suppose
\Gamma_{1}
is arbitrary with
\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1
.
Then, for any
1\leq r\leq N
and any
{t}\in[0,1]
, we have

(3.38)

R^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\langle k\rangle^{10}\mathbb{E}|(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}({t})|^{2}\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta r/8}.

Moreover, for any choice of
\Gamma_{1}
, denote the right hand side of (
3.37
) by
\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}]
,
then we have that

(3.39)

\|\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}]\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq\epsilon^{\theta/8}\quad\mathrm{if}\quad\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1;

and that

(3.40)

\|\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}]-\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}]\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq\epsilon^{\theta/8}\|\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\quad\mathrm{if}\quad\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}},\|\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1.

Proof.

This is proved in Section
5
, using the preparations in Subsection
3.7

and Section
4
.
∎

Proposition 3.9
.

Assume that the functions
u
and
w
satisfy the bootstrap assumptions (
2.26
) on the time interval
[0,t_{0}]
,
t_{0}\leq T_{1}
, and define the coefficients
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}
and
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}
as before. Then, with probability
\geq 1-2e^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
, for any
t^{\prime}\in[0,t_{0}/T_{1}]
we have

\displaystyle R^{-1}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\langle k\rangle^{10}|(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}({t}^{\prime})-(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}({t}^{\prime})|\leq\epsilon^{N\theta/12}

Moreover, the function
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
has the property that the bounds (
3.6
) hold for solutions of the linear equation (
3.5
).

Proof.

This is proved in Section
6
.
∎

With Propositions
3.8
and
3.9
, we can now prove Proposition
3.7
.
Then combining with Proposition
3.1
, we can prove Proposition
2.4
.

Proof of Proposition
2.4
.

Notice first that Proposition
3.7
follows from Proposition
3.8
by a fixed point argument. Then we notice that we are making the bootstrap assumptions (
2.26
), which match with (
3.3
), so that the conclusion (
3.7
) of Proposition
3.1
gives us

(3.41)

\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\|w(t^{\prime})-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\leq\epsilon^{A/4}.

Notice that the assumption (
3.6
) on solutions of (
3.5
), which is needed to obtain
(
3.7
) is proved in Subsection
6.2
. Moreover, by the definitions (
3.35
) and (
3.33
), we have, for
t\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle(1-\partial_{x}^{2})^{p}w_{\mathrm{app}}(T_{1}t,x)=\frac{\epsilon}{2\pi R^{1/2}}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\langle k\rangle^{2p}(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}t+\Gamma(t,k))}

which is a multilinear Gaussian sum, and, by (
3.35
) and the main estimates (
3.38
), we know that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big|(1-\partial_{x}^{2})^{p}w_{\mathrm{app}}(T_{1}t,x)\big|^{2}\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{R}\mathbb{E}\Big|\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\langle k\rangle^{5}(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)e^{ik\cdot x}e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}t+\Gamma(t,k))}\Big|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{R}\mathbb{E}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\sum_{r=0}^{N}\langle k\rangle^{10}\big|(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}(t)\big|^{2}=\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{R}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\langle k\rangle^{10}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k)\lesssim T_{1}^{-1},

where the sums are over all possible admissible trees and couples.
Using the hyper-contractivity estimates of Proposition
2.2
(i), we obtain,
with probability
\geq 1-e^{-R^{\eta_{0}}}
, for all
t\leq T_{1}
,

(3.42)

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\leq t}\|w_{\mathrm{app}}(t^{\prime})\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{1-\theta/2}+\epsilon^{-\theta}T_{1}^{-1/2}.

Finally, we recall the definitions (
3.33
) and (
3.18
),
and use Proposition
3.9
to obtain, for all
t\leq T_{1}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t]}\big\|w_{\mathrm{app}}(t^{\prime})-w_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})\big\|_{\dot{W}^{4,0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\epsilon}{R^{1/2}}\sup_{t^{\prime}\in[0,t/T_{1}]}\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}}\langle k\rangle^{5}|(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t^{\prime})-(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(t^{\prime})|\lesssim\epsilon^{N\theta/12}.

Putting this together with (
3.42
) and (
3.41
), and in view of our choice of parameters,
we obtain (
2.27
) thus completing the proof of Proposition
2.4
and of Theorem
1.1
.
∎

3.7.
An
L^{1}
estimate on time integration

In this section, we estimate the time integrals
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
defined in (
3.29
). Note that the integrals depends on the functions
\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})
for all I-branching nodes
\mathfrak{m}
, which are defined as in (
3.28
) and (
3.25
).
In particular, they depend on the values

(3.43)

\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}:=T_{1}\Big[\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}|k_{\mathfrak{m}}|^{1/2}-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}|k_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}|^{1/2}\Big]+\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot\Big[\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\mathfrak{m}})-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}})\Big]

as well as the function
\Gamma_{1}(k,t)
.

Lemma 3.10
.

For any function
\Gamma_{1}
that satisfies
\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1
, regardless of the exact choice of
\Gamma_{1}
, we always have

(3.44)

|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}})|\leq\mathcal{A}\big((\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big),

where
\mathcal{A}
is an explicit function depending on
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
only, and satisfying the bounds

(3.45)

\sum_{q_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\sup_{|\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}-q_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}|\leq 1}\big|\mathcal{A}\big((\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big)\big|\leq(\log R)^{r(\mathcal{Q})},

where each
\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is a subset of
\mathbb{Z}
of at most
R^{20}
elements possibly depending on
\mathfrak{m}
.

Proof.

Using (
3.28
) and (
3.25
), we can write the integral
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}
as

(3.46)

\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}=\int_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{Q}}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,dt_{\mathfrak{m}},

where for each
\mathfrak{m}
the function
\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is defined by

(3.47)

\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\Big[\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{\mathfrak{m}})-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}})\Big]\,dt^{\prime}.

By our assumption on
\Gamma_{1}
, we may assume that
\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}
is supported on
[-2,2]
, and is uniformly bounded in
C_{t}^{1}
.

Now in (
3.46
) we start by integrating in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
for some branching node
\mathfrak{m}
whose children are all leaves.
With all the other
t_{\mathfrak{m}^{\prime}}
fixed, we have an integral of form

(3.48)

\int e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}}=\frac{-1}{\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}}\int e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}}+\frac{1}{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\bigg|_{\mathrm{boundary\ values}}.

Here the boundary values are either an absolute constant, or the value
t_{\mathfrak{q}}

for the parent node
\mathfrak{q}
of
\mathfrak{m}
. We may assume
|\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}|\geq 1

as the integral in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
is trivially bounded in
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
and the integrability in
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}

on bounded sets follows trivially; then in the above expression,
the boundary terms are either constant or has the form

(3.49)

\frac{1}{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}}\cdot e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{q}})}

which will be carried to the next integration involving
t_{\mathfrak{q}}
, but with a decay factor
(i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})^{-1}
.

As for the bulk term in (
3.48
), since we already have the
(\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})^{-1}
factor,
we can just fix the value of
t_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
and view the expression

e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\prime}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}

as a bounded constant, then integrate in the other variables and finally take average over
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

In either cases, we have reduced the integral
\mathcal{I}
to
(i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})^{-1}
times another integral
involving one fewer time variables, with possibly the additional factor

e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}}e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{q}})}
inserted into the integrand.
But this factor only shifts the phase

e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}}
to
e^{i(\alpha_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}+\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})t_{\mathfrak{q}}}
;
moreover the additional factor

e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{q}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}
is changed to
e^{i(\Psi_{\mathfrak{q}}(t_{\mathfrak{q}})+\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{q}}))}
,
but the function
\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}+\Psi_{\mathfrak{q}}
is still supported on
[-2,2]
and uniformly bounded in
C^{1}
.

As such, we can keep integrating in the order of time variables from bottom to top (i.e. each time we integrate in some variable
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
where
\mathfrak{m}
is one of the lowest nodes that still remains), so that for each integration in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
the boundary only contains
t_{\mathfrak{q}}
for
\mathfrak{q}
being the parent node of
\mathfrak{m}
. Moreover, each time we get a denominator
(\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})^{-1}
, where this
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
may also be shifted by some linear combination of previous
\alpha_{\mathfrak{r}}^{0}
variables. Putting together (again we may ignore the cases where
|\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}|\leq 1
etc.), we get that

|\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}(t,(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}})|\lesssim\sum_{(\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}\langle\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}\rangle^{-1},

where
\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
is defined from
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
by the following rule:

•

If
\mathfrak{m}
has no child that is a branching node, then
\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}=\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
;

•

Otherwise we have
\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}=\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\iota_{j}\beta_{\mathfrak{m}_{j}}^{0}
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
are children nodes of
\mathfrak{m}
and
\iota_{j}\in\{0,1\}
are chosen arbitrarily.

Now, for fixed
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
there are only finitely many choices for
\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
, and for each choice we have that

\sum_{q_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\sup_{|\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}-q_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}|\leq 1}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}\langle\beta_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}\rangle^{-1}\lesssim(\log R)^{r(\mathcal{Q})},

by taking supremum and summation
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
in the order of
\mathfrak{m}
that is opposite from the integration in
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
, and using the elementary inequality that

\sup_{\#\mathcal{S}\leq R^{20},\,\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbb{Z}}\sup_{\beta}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{S}}\sup_{|\alpha-q|\leq 1}\frac{1}{\langle\alpha+\beta\rangle}\lesssim\log R.

This completes the proof, where we note also that
r(\mathcal{Q})
is the total number of branching nodes
\mathfrak{m}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, that is, the total number of variables
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
.
∎

3.8.
Base counting estimates

For
r\in\{2,3\}
and
\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
,
k^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}^{r}
,
k_{*}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}
, and
\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, consider the sets

(3.50)

\Sigma_{r}:=\bigg\{(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r})\in(\mathbb{Z}_{R})^{r}:|k_{j}-k_{j}^{0}|\leq 1,\sum_{j=1}^{r}\zeta_{j}k_{j}=k_{*},\bigg|\sum_{j=1}^{r}\zeta_{j}(|k_{j}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{j}))-\beta\bigg|\leq T_{1}^{-1}\bigg\}.

Then we have the following estimates for
\#\Sigma_{r}
:
