Proposition 3.11

(
2
vector counting)
.

Suppose
r=2
and
\zeta_{1}=-\zeta_{2}
, then we have

(3.51)

\#\Sigma_{2}\lesssim R\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}\cdot\min(1,T_{1}^{-1}\cdot|k_{*}|^{-1}).

Suppose
r=2
and
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}
, then we have

(3.52)

\#\Sigma_{2}\lesssim R\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}T_{1}^{-1/2}.

Proof.

Consider (
3.51
). First trivially
\#\Sigma_{2}\lesssim R
because
k_{1}
has only
\approx R
choices and
k_{2}
is uniquely determined by
k_{1}
. Now, suppose
k_{*}\neq 0
and (say)
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=(+,-)
, then we have

|(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))-(|k_{1}-k_{*}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}-k_{*}))-\beta|\leq T_{1}^{-1}.

Recalling the definition (
3.23
) we can estimate

\big|\partial_{k_{1}}\{(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))-(|k_{1}-k_{*}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}-k_{*}))\}\big|\gtrsim|k_{*}|\langle k^{0}\rangle^{-2}.

Therefore (
3.50
) implies that
k_{1}
belongs to a set of measure
\lesssim T_{1}^{-1}\cdot|k_{*}|^{-1}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}
(which is a union of finitely many intervals), hence we also have
\#\Sigma_{2}\lesssim RT_{1}^{-1}|k_{*}|^{-1}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}
.

Now consider (
3.52
), assume
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=+
, then we have

|(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))+(|k_{*}-k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{*}-k_{1}))-\beta|\leq T_{1}^{-1}.

Moreover
k_{1}
also belongs to a fixed unit interval, and the second derivative

\big|\partial_{k_{1}}^{2}\{(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))+(|k_{*}-k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{*}-k_{1}))\}\big|\gtrsim\langle k^{0}\rangle^{-2}.

This implies that
k_{1}
belongs a set of measure
\lesssim T_{1}^{-1/2}
which is a union of finitely many intervals, and therefore
\#\Sigma_{2}\lesssim RT_{1}^{-1/2}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}
.
∎

Proposition 3.12

(
3
vector counting)
.

Suppose
r=3
, then we have

(3.53)

\#\Sigma_{3}\lesssim R^{2}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\log R.

Proof.

First assume the signs
\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{3}
are not all the same, say
\zeta_{1}=-\zeta_{2}
. Then with
k_{3}
fixed, the number of choices for
(k_{1},k_{2})
is bounded by
R\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}T_{1}^{-1}|k^{\prime}|^{-1}
due to (
3.51
), where
k^{\prime}
is a shift of
k_{3}
by a constant vector (either
k_{*}
or
-k_{*}
). Summing over
k_{3}
then yields (
3.53
), as

\sum_{k_{3}}|k_{3}\pm k_{*}|^{-1}\lesssim\log R

for summation in
k_{3}
over any fixed unit interval.

Now assume (say)
\zeta_{1}=\zeta_{2}=\zeta_{3}=+
, then we have
k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}=k_{*}
and

(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))+(|k_{2}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{2}))+(|k_{3}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{3}))=\beta+O(T_{1}^{-1}).

Let
\max(|k_{1}-k_{2}|,|k_{2}-k_{3}|,|k_{1}-k_{3}|)\approx L
, and assume
|k_{1}-k_{2}|\approx L
. Then by the same argument as in the proof of (
3.52
) in Proposition
3.11
, and using the bound

\big|\partial_{k_{1}}\{(|k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{1}))+(|k_{*}-k_{3}-k_{1}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{*}-k_{3}-k_{1}))\}\big|\gtrsim L\langle k^{0}\rangle^{-2},

we see that for fixed
k_{3}
, the number of choices for
(k_{1},k_{2})
is bounded by
RT_{1}^{-1}L^{-1}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}
. Moreover by assumption we also have
|k_{3}-k_{*}/3|\lesssim L
, so the number of choices of
(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})
with fixed
L
is
\lesssim RT_{1}^{-1}L^{-1}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}\cdot RL=R^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\langle k^{0}\rangle^{2}
.
Finally summing over
L
yields an extra logarithmic factor, which proves (
3.53
).
∎

4.
Irregular chains

In this section we define and analyze the
irregular chains
,
which were first introduced in
[
17
]
.
In particular we will exhibit an important cancellation between these structures.
In addition to the arguments in
[
17
]
we need to use a
C^{1/2}
regularity property of
the symbols of the water waves equations after our normal form transformations,
which is quite non-trivial to verify; see (
2.13
) and the proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Definition 4.1
.

An
irregular chain

\mathcal{H}
is
a sequence of branching nodes
(\mathfrak{n}_{0},\cdots,\mathfrak{n}_{q})
, such that

•

Each
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(0\leq j\leq q)
is an I-branching node of exactly
3
children nodes, and
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
is a child node of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
;

•

Each
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
has a child node
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
, which has opposite sign with
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
, and is paired with a child node
\mathfrak{p}_{j+1}
of
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
as leaves.

Figure 1.
An example of an irregular chain (Definition
4.1
).

Definition 4.2

(Congruence relation)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an irregular chain as in Figure
1
.
By definition, we know that
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
is a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}

that has the same sign with
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
, thus it is either the first (left) child or the second (middle) child.
Let us fix these relative positions; then, let the sign
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}=\zeta_{j}
.
It is clear that, given the sign
\zeta_{0}
and the relative positions of
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
,
the structure of
\mathcal{H}
is uniquely determined by the signs
\zeta_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q)

(this is because, if we fix
\zeta_{j+1}/\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}
,
then this fixes the relative position of
\mathfrak{n}_{j+1}
as a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
). See Figure
2
.

We define two irregular chains to be
congruent
(denoted
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{H}
),
if the sign
\zeta_{0}
and the relative position of each
\mathfrak{p}_{j}
as a child of
\mathfrak{n}_{j}

is the same for these two irregular chains. In other words, given a congruence class,
the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}
in this congruence class is uniquely determined by the signs

\zeta_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q)
. For two congruent irregular chains, we also call them
twists
of each other.
Finally, suppose
\mathcal{H}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
are disjoint irregular chains in a given couple
\mathcal{Q}
,
then we can perform twists separately and arbitrarily at each
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, starting from
\mathcal{Q}
;
we define any couple
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
formed in this way to be
congruent
to
\mathcal{Q}

(relative to the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
), denoted
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}
.

Figure 2.
An example of two congruent irregular chains (Definition
4.2
) which are twists of each other.
Here the signs of the involved nodes are shown in the picture.

Now we fix one irregular chain
\mathcal{H}
in a couple
\mathcal{Q}
, and consider a decoration of
\mathcal{Q}
.
Then the restriction of this decoration to nodes in
\mathcal{H}
can be uniquely determined by the
2q+4
vectors
(k_{j},\ell_{j}):0\leq j\leq q+1
, which are defined as follows: for each
0\leq j\leq q
we have
\{k_{j},\ell_{j}\}=\{k_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}},k_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}}\}
, and
(k_{j},\ell_{j})=(k_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}},k_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}})
if
\zeta_{j}=+
, and
(k_{j},\ell_{j})=(k_{\mathfrak{p}_{j}},k_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}})
if
\zeta_{j}=-
.
For
j=q+1
we have
(k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1})=(k_{\mathfrak{e}},k_{\mathfrak{f}})
where
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{f}
are the two other children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{q}
other than
\mathfrak{p}_{q}
, such that
\mathfrak{e}
has sign
+
and
\mathfrak{f}
has sign
-
. In fact we have the following equalities:

(4.1)

k_{0}-\ell_{0}=k_{1}-\ell_{1}=\cdots=k_{q+1}-\ell_{q+1}:=h,

and

(4.2)

\displaystyle T_{1}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}_{j}}(t)=\alpha_{j}^{0}\cdot t+G_{j}(t),

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}^{0}=T_{1}\cdot\big(|k_{j}|^{1/2}-|k_{j}-h|^{1/2}-|k_{j+1}|^{1/2}+|k_{j+1}-h|^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot\big(\Gamma_{0}(k_{j})-\Gamma_{0}(k_{j}-h)-\Gamma_{0}(k_{j+1})+\Gamma_{0}(k_{j+1}-h)\big);

\displaystyle G_{j}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\big(\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{j})-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{j}-h)-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{j+1})+\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{j+1}-h)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

Note that these are independent of
\zeta_{j}
, i.e.
they depend only on the congruence class which is invariant under twists.

With the above discussions, we can consider an irregular chain
\mathcal{H}
in a couple
\mathcal{Q}
and all its twists. Note that if we twist
\mathcal{H}
, then this twist can be trivially extended to
\mathcal{Q}
, as long as we keep the remaining part of the couple
\mathcal{Q}
unchanged (note that the positions of
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{f}
may be switched, but the sub-trees attached at
\mathfrak{e}
and
\mathfrak{f}
are unchanged). Consider the expression
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
defined in (
3.36
) and the associated sub-expression where we restrict the sum to only those
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
for
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathcal{H}
(but excluding
\mathfrak{n}\in\{\mathfrak{n}_{0},\mathfrak{p}_{0},\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{f}\}
) and restrict the integration to only those
t_{\mathfrak{m}}
for
\mathfrak{m}
in
\mathcal{H}
(but excluding
\mathfrak{m}=\mathfrak{n}_{0}
); denote this sub-expression by
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}}
.
We will assume
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}=+
without loss of generality. Then, the combination

\sum_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}},

where the sum is taken over all twists
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{H}
, can be rearranged into the following form:

(4.3)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}=:\mathcal{G}\big((k_{0},\ell_{0},k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1}),({t}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}},{t}_{\mathfrak{e}_{0}},{t}_{\mathfrak{f}_{0}})\big)\\
&\qquad=\int_{\max({t}_{\mathfrak{e}},t_{\mathfrak{f}})<{t}_{q}<\cdots<{t}_{1}<{t}_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}}\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{q}}\prod_{j=0}^{q}e^{i(\alpha_{j}^{0}\cdot t_{j}+G_{j}(t_{j}))}\,\mathrm{d}{t}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}{t}_{q}\cdot in_{3}(\ell_{0},k_{1},\ell_{1})\\
&\qquad\times\prod_{j=1}^{q}\big[\psi(\ell_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{j})in_{3}(\ell_{j},k_{j+1},\ell_{j+1})-\psi(k_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\ell_{j})in_{3}(k_{j},\ell_{j+1},k_{j+1})\big].\end{split}

Here note that, in the case
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}=-
, the factor
n_{3}(\ell_{0},k_{1},\ell_{1})
in (
4.3
)
should be replaced by
n_{3}(k_{0},\ell_{1},k_{1})
, but this does not affect the proof of Proposition
4.3
below.

Proposition 4.3

(Explicit statement of the cancellation estimate)
.

Note that the function

\mathcal{G}:=\sum_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}^{\prime}}

defined in (
4.3
)
is a function of
(k_{0},\ell_{0},k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1})
and
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}):=(t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}},t_{\mathfrak{e}},t_{\mathfrak{f}})
, where

(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})\in O:=\{(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime}):0<\max(t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})<t<1\}.

We may also assume each of
(k_{0},\ell_{0},k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1})
belongs to a fixed unit ball.

Then there exists an extension of
\mathcal{G}
that equals
\mathcal{G}
on
O
, which we still denote by
\mathcal{G}
, such that

(4.4)

\big\|\mathcal{F}\big(\mathcal{G}\cdot e^{-iT_{1}\Omega_{*}(t)}\big)\big\|_{L_{(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})}^{1}\ell_{(k_{0},\ell_{0},k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1})}^{\infty}}\leq\epsilon^{2q/3}(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{q},

where
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
is the Fourier transform of
\mathcal{G}
in the
(t,t^{\prime},t^{\prime\prime})
variables with the corresponding Fourier variables being
(\xi,\xi^{\prime},\xi^{\prime\prime})
.
The function
\Omega_{*}(t)
is defined by

(4.5)

\displaystyle T_{1}\Omega_{*}(t)=\alpha_{*}^{0}\cdot t+G_{*}(t),

\displaystyle\alpha_{*}^{0}=T_{1}\cdot\big(|k_{0}|^{1/2}-|k_{0}-h|^{1/2}-|k_{q+1}|^{1/2}+|k_{q+1}-h|^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot\big(\Gamma_{0}(k_{0})-\Gamma_{0}(k_{0}-h)-\Gamma_{0}(k_{q+1})+\Gamma_{0}(k_{q+1}-h)\big);

\displaystyle G_{*}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\big(\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{0})-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{0}-h)-\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{q+1})+\Gamma_{1}(t^{\prime},k_{q+1}-h)\big)\,\mathrm{d}t^{\prime}.

Finally, the bound (
4.4
)
is uniform in the choice of the unit balls containing each of
the quartets
(k_{0},\ell_{0},k_{q+1},\ell_{q+1})
.

Proof.

We may assume
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}=+
(the case
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}=-
is the same;
note that the twisting does not affect
\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}
, see Definition
4.2
).
Let
h
be defined as in (
4.1
), and let
\psi_{O}

be a fixed compactly supported smooth cutoff function that equals
1
on
O
.
Also fix
K\gg N_{0}
to be a sufficiently large constant, and note that
q\leq N_{0}
.
We may form an extension of
\mathcal{G}
by multiplying by
\psi_{O}
;
after this we make the linear change of variables and define
\mathcal{U}(t,t_{1},t_{2})=\mathcal{G}(t,t+t_{1},t+t_{2})
.
Then, by making the substitution
t_{j}=t+s_{j}
in (
4.3
) and using that
\alpha_{*}^{0}=\sum_{j}\alpha_{j}^{0}
, we have

(4.6)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{U}(t,t_{1},t_{2})&\cdot e^{-i(\alpha_{*}^{0}\cdot t+G_{*}(t))}=\int_{\max(t_{1},t_{2})<{s}_{q}<\cdots<{s}_{1}<0}\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{q}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}e^{i(\alpha_{j}^{0}\cdot s_{j}+G_{j}(s_{j}+t))}\mathrm{d}{s}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}{s}_{q}\\
&\times in_{3}(\ell_{0},k_{1},\ell_{1})\cdot e^{i(G_{0}(t)-G_{*}(t))}\\
&\times\prod_{j=1}^{q}\big[\psi(\ell_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{j})in_{3}(k_{j+1},\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j})-\psi(k_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\ell_{j})in_{3}(\ell_{j+1},k_{j+1},k_{j})\big].\end{split}

Define the left hand side of (
4.6
) to be
\mathcal{V}(t,t_{1},t_{2})
, then we have

(4.7)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mathcal{V}}(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})&=\sum_{k_{1},\cdots,k_{q}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\big[\psi(\ell_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{j})in_{3}(k_{j+1},\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j})-\psi(k_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\ell_{j})in_{3}(\ell_{j+1},k_{j+1},k_{j})\big]\\
&\times in_{3}(\ell_{0},k_{1},\ell_{1})\int_{t_{1},t_{2}<s_{q}<\cdots<s_{1}<0;\,t\in\mathbb{R}}e^{i(\xi t+\xi_{1}t_{1}+\xi_{2}t_{2})}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{q}e^{i\alpha_{j}^{0}s_{j}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{q}e^{iG_{j}(s_{j}+t)}\,\\
&\times e^{i(G_{0}(t)-G_{*}(t))}\cdot\psi\cdot\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}t_{1}\mathrm{d}t_{2}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\mathrm{d}s_{j}.\end{split}

First assume
\max(|\xi|,|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|)\leq R^{K}
. In (
4.7
), we can integrate by parts first in
(s_{q},\cdots,s_{1})
in this order, then in
(t_{1},t_{2})
, and finally in
t
; by a similar argument to the proof of Proposition
3.10
, we can bound the absolute value of the integral in (
4.7
) by a sum of at most
2^{q}
terms which are bounded by

(4.8)

\prod_{j=1}^{q}\langle\beta_{j}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\langle\xi_{1}+\gamma_{1}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\langle\xi_{2}+\gamma_{2}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\langle\xi+\gamma\rangle^{-1},

where each
\beta_{j}
equals
\alpha_{j}^{0}
plus an algebraic sum of the
(\alpha_{k}^{0})_{k<j}
, and similarly
\gamma_{1}
equals an algebraic sum of
(\alpha_{j}^{0})
, and
\gamma_{2}
equals an algebraic sum of
(\alpha_{j}^{0})
and
\gamma_{1}
, and
\gamma_{3}
equals an algebraic sum of
(\alpha_{j}^{0})
and
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
. Here we note that previous integration by parts may lead to factors of form
G_{j}^{\prime}(s_{j}+t)
for certain
j
(if we choose the bulk term instead of the boundary term), but these terms come with integration in
s_{j}
, so while integrating by parts in
t
we can choose the vector field
\partial_{t}-\sum_{j}\partial_{s_{j}}
instead of
\partial_{t}
to avoid derivatives falling on these
G_{j}^{\prime}
factors. In the end this will not affect the validity of (
4.8
).

Now, for each
\alpha_{j}^{0}
, we may fix
q_{j}^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|\alpha_{j}^{0}-q_{j}^{0}|\leq 1
, and
|q_{j}^{0}|\leq R^{2}
; this allows us to decompose
\widehat{\mathcal{V}}=\sum_{(q_{j}^{0})}\mathcal{W}_{(q_{j}^{0})}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{(q_{j}^{0})}
is defined as in (
4.7
) but with the vector
(k_{j},h)
restricted to the set where
|\alpha_{j}^{0}-q_{j}^{0}|\leq 1
for each
j
. For fixed
(q_{j}^{0})
, by (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) we then have

(4.9)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\|\mathcal{W}_{(q_{j}^{0})}\|_{L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}L_{(k_{0},k_{q+1},h)}^{\infty}}\\
&\qquad\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{q}\langle\beta_{j}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\int_{\max(|\xi|,|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|)\leq R^{K}}\langle\xi_{1}+\gamma_{1}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi_{2}+\gamma_{2}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi+\gamma\rangle^{-1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{q})\\
|\alpha_{j}^{0}-q_{j}^{0}|\leq 1\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\Big|\psi(\ell_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{j})\cdot n_{3}(k_{j+1},\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j})-\psi(k_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\ell_{j})\cdot n_{3}(\ell_{j+1},k_{j+1},k_{j})\Big|;\end{split}

in (
4.9
) the
\beta_{j}
can also be replaced by an algebraic sum of
(q_{j}^{0})
.
Now in (
4.9
), the integral is bounded by
(\log R)^{C}
,
and then the factor
\prod_{j=1}^{q}\langle\beta_{j}\rangle^{-1}
depends only on
(q_{j}^{0})
,
and the summation of this factor over all choices of
(q_{j}^{0})
gives another
(\log R)^{C}
.
As such, to bound (
4.9
) we only need to bound the final sum in
(k_{j})
.

Consider now the sum in
(k_{j})
in (
4.9
);
in view of the decay of
\psi
, we may restrict each
k_{j}
to a unit interval
|k_{j}-k_{j}^{0}|\leq 1
;
then we claim that

(4.10)

\displaystyle\big|\psi(\ell_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{j})\cdot n_{3}(k_{j+1},\ell_{j+1},\ell_{j})-\psi(k_{j})\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(\ell_{j})\cdot n_{3}(\ell_{j+1},k_{j+1},k_{j})\big|\lesssim|h|^{1/2}.

To see this, it suffices to recall that we are assuming that
\psi
satisfies (
1.4
), and that the symbol
n_{3}

satisfies the bound in (
2.13
), together with the fact that
k_{j}-\ell_{j}=h
.

Putting all these together, we can bound the summation in
(k_{j})
in (
4.9
) by
|h|^{q/2}\cdot\mathfrak{A}
,
where
\mathfrak{A}
is the number of choices for
(k_{1},\cdots,k_{q})
such that
|\alpha_{j}^{0}-q_{j}^{0}|\leq 1
for each
j
.
Finally, to bound this
\mathfrak{A}
, we note that
(k_{0},\ell_{0})
has been fixed; for each
j
,
we inductively construct
(k_{j},\ell_{j}=k_{j}-h)
using the condition that
\alpha_{j}^{0}
belongs to a fixed unit interval.
Then, Proposition
3.11
gives that, for each fixed
(k_{j-1},\ell_{j-1})
,
the number of choices for
(k_{j},\ell_{j})
is bounded by
R\cdot T_{1}^{-1/2}|h|^{-1/2}
.
By iterating this for each
j
, we get the upper bound

|h|^{q/2}\cdot\mathfrak{A}\lesssim(R\cdot T_{1}^{-1/2})^{q}\lesssim(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{q}\epsilon^{2q/3},

using that
T_{1}\leq\epsilon^{-8/3}
, which completes the proof of (
4.4
)
by summing over all
(q_{j}^{0})
in (
4.9
).

Finally, assume
\max(|\xi|,|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|)\geq R^{K}
. In this case we can first fix the values of
(k_{j},h)
at a loss of
R^{10N}\ll R^{M}
, using the trivial bound

\|\mathcal{W}\|_{L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}L_{(k_{j},h)}^{\infty}}\leq\|\mathcal{W}\|_{L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}L_{(k_{j},h)}^{1}}=\|\mathcal{W}\|_{L_{(k_{j},h)}^{1}L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}}\leq R^{10N}\|\mathcal{W}\|_{L_{(k_{j},h)}^{\infty}L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}}

for any function
\mathcal{W}
.

Note that with fixed
k_{j}
, the function
G_{j}(t)
, see (
4.5
),
is compactly supported on
\mathbb{R}
(by suitably extending it for
t\geq 1
)
and belongs to
C_{t}^{1}
; by Plancherel and Cauchy-Schwartz, we know that

\|\langle\lambda\rangle^{1/4}\widehat{G_{j}}(\lambda)\|_{L^{1}_{\lambda}}\lesssim 1,

thus we can write
G_{j}(t)
as a linear superposition of functions of form
\langle\lambda\rangle^{-1/4}e^{i\lambda t}
with summable coefficients in
\lambda
. Applying this for each
G_{j}
and
(G_{0},G_{*})
, we can get rid of these
e^{iG_{j}(s_{j}+t)}
factors in (
4.7
).
Then, by fixing all the
s_{j}\,(j\neq q)
, integrating in
(t_{1},t_{2})
, then in
s_{q}
and finally integrating by parts in
t
twice, we get that

|\widehat{\mathcal{V}}|\leq R^{10N}\cdot\langle\xi_{1}\rangle^{-1}\langle\xi_{2}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\langle\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\alpha_{q}^{0}+\gamma_{1}\rangle^{-1}\cdot\langle\xi+\gamma_{2}\rangle^{-2}\cdot\max(\langle\gamma_{1}\rangle,\langle\gamma_{2}\rangle)^{-1/4}.

Using that
\max(|\xi|,|\xi_{1}|,|\xi_{2}|)\geq R^{K}
, we can easily bound the above in
L_{(\xi,\xi_{1},\xi_{2})}^{1}
norm by
R^{-K/8}
, which overcomes the loss
R^{10N}
and is sufficient to prove (
4.4
). Therefore, the proof of (
4.4
) is complete in all cases.
∎

Definition 4.4

(Splicing irregular chains)
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a couple and
\mathcal{H}
be an irregular chain. We define the new couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
as follows. As in Definition
4.1
, in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
we remove all the nodes in Figure
1
except
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
and the two children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{q}
other than
\mathfrak{p}_{q}
. Then we turn these two children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{q}
into children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
(the left children node of
\mathfrak{n}_{0}
is still
\mathfrak{p}_{0}
), and connect these four nodes to the rest of the couple as in
\mathcal{Q}
. We call this the
splicing
of
\mathcal{Q}
at
\mathcal{H}
. Similarly, for any set of disjoint irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, we can define the splicing at these chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a couple and
\mathcal{H}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
be disjoint irregular chains; for each
j
we have two nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{0j}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{0j}
as in Definition
4.1
.
Let
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}
run over all couples that are congruent to
\mathcal{Q}
relative to
\mathcal{H}_{j}
(Definition
4.2
).
Moreover, in the definition of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}
in (
3.36
), we restrict (by inserting indicator functions of the vector variables
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
) that for each
j
we have
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
; clearly this restriction is not affected by twisting. Let
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
be the result of splicing the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, as in Definition
4.4
. Then we have

(4.11)

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}=\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})-2}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})}\sum_{(k_{\mathfrak{n}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sb}}}^{(+)}\widetilde{\psi}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sb}}}\mathcal{G}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}{t}_{\mathfrak{m}}.

Here
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
is a
k
-decoration of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and all notions
(\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{sb}},\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}},\mathcal{E}_{\mathrm{sb}})
and
\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}
etc. are the same as in (
3.36
)
but associated with the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
.
Moreover
q_{\mathrm{tot}}
is the sum of the lengths
q_{j}
of the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
(cf. Definition
4.1
).
Finally the function
\mathcal{G}
is a function of all vector variables
(k_{\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}})
and time variables
(t_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}})
, such that:

(1)

let
\widehat{\mathcal{G}}
be the time Fourier transform of
\mathcal{G}
, then the norm

(4.12)

\|\widehat{\mathcal{G}}\|_{L_{\xi_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}}^{1}L_{k_{\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}}}^{\infty}}\lesssim 1,

where
\xi_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
are the Fourier variables corresponding to
t_{\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
;

(2)

For each irregular chain
\mathcal{H}_{j}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, after splicing, we get two nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{0j}
and
\mathfrak{p}_{0j}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, see Definition
4.4
. Then, in the support of
\mathcal{G}
, we have that
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
for each
j
;

(3)

The
\widetilde{\psi}
is another rapidly decaying function which may be different from the
\psi
in (
3.36
).

Proof.

We start from the expression
\sum_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}

in (
4.11
), where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
is as in (
3.36
) with the restriction
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
for each
j
described as above. We first sum and integrate in the variables associated with each irregular chain
\mathcal{H}_{j}
(i.e. those occurring in the expressions of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{j}}
as in (
4.3
)), and treat the other variables as fixed; note that these frozen variables are in one-to-one correspondence with the variables
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
and
(t_{\mathfrak{m}})_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
. Then, for each
\mathcal{H}_{j}
we obtain the factor which is

\sum_{\mathcal{H}_{j}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{j}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{H}_{j}^{\prime}},

and by (
4.4
) in Proposition
4.3
, we can reduce it to
e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{*}(t_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}})}
multiplied by some function whose time Fourier transform is bounded in the
L_{\xi}^{1}L_{k}^{\infty}
norm, The desired expression (
4.11
) then follows by putting together these factors for each
j
. Here we note that:

(1)

All the symbols
\widetilde{n}(\cdots)
and
\widetilde{a}(\cdots)
can be absorbed by the rapidly decaying factors
\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})

in view of (
2.11
), at the price of replacing
\psi
with another rapidly decaying function
\widetilde{\psi}
.

(2)

The
\mathcal{G}
factor in (
4.11
) is the product of all the factors coming from application of (
4.4
) for each
j
, and the bound (
4.12
) follows from the tensor product of the bounds (
4.4
).

(3)

The support condition
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
follows from the extra restrictions defined above.

(4)

Finally, it is easy to see that

r(\mathcal{Q})=r(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})+2q_{\mathrm{tot}},\quad n_{I}(\mathcal{Q})=n_{I}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})+q_{\mathrm{tot}},

so the powers of
\epsilon R^{-1/2}
and
T_{1}
in (
4.11
) and (
3.36
) exactly match in view of the right hand side of (
4.4
).

The proof of Proposition
4.5
is now complete.
∎

5.
Molecules and counting algorithm

In this section we prove an estimate for the expression (
4.11
),
which is a key step in the proof of Proposition
3.8
.
The first main goal is to reduce this estimate
to a counting problem associated with combinatorial structures called
molecules
,
which are essentially directed graphs with weights and linear equations
associated to their vertices (called atoms) and edges (called bonds).
Molecules have been introduced and used in the context of the cubic NLS in
[
17
]

and then used in a series of subsequent works.
We define molecules and related concepts in Subsection
5.1
.
In Subsection
5.2
we first form molecules from couples
after splicing irregular chains (see Section
4
),
and then, in Proposition
5.12
,
we give an upper bound for the main quantity that need to be estimated in Proposition
3.8
.
This upper bound depends on combinatorial/graph theoretical quantities associated to the molecule
(such as its
circuit rank
),
and on a
counting problem
for approximate resonance equations at each atom.
To efficiently carry out this complex counting problem we use a cutting operation,
described in Subsection
5.3
, and perform a cutting algorithm
which decomposes molecules in relatively simpler objects, keeping track of how the counting
problem changes at each step. Eventually we complete the necessary counting estimates in Subsection
5.4

which is the more technical part of the proof, by ‘removing’ atoms one by one from a molecule until it becomes trivial.
The proof of the main Proposition
3.8
is then completed at the end of the section.

5.1.
Basic definitions and notations

We begin with the general definition of a molecule and related concepts.

Definition 5.1

(Molecules)
.

A
molecule

\mathbb{M}
is a directed graph, formed by vertices, called
atoms
,
and edges, called
bonds
, where multiple and self-connecting bonds are allowed.
The atoms are classified into two types, the I-atoms and N-atoms;
denote by
\mathbb{M}_{I}
and
\mathbb{M}_{N}
the set of all I-atoms and N-atoms.
We will write
v\in\mathbb{M}
and
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
for atoms
v
and bonds
\ell
in
\mathbb{M}
,
and write
\ell\sim v
if
v
is one of the two endpoints of
\ell
.
For a molecule
\mathbb{M}
we define
V
(resp.
V_{I}
and
V_{N}
) to be the number of atoms
(resp. I- and N-atoms),
E
the number of bonds and
F
the number of components.
Define

\chi:=E-V+F

to be its
circuit rank
.
For any atom
v\in\mathbb{M}
we define its degree
d(v)
as the number of bonds
\ell\sim v
.

Definition 5.2

(Molecules from couples)
.

Given a couple
\mathcal{Q}
, define the molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
associated with
\mathcal{Q}
, as follows. The atoms of
\mathbb{M}
are the branching nodes
\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{B}
of
\mathcal{Q}
(including both I- and N-branching nodes). For any two atoms
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
, we connect them by a bond if either (i)
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is the parent of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
,
or (ii) a child of
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
is paired to a child of
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
as leaves. We fix the direction of each bond as follows: in case (i) the bond should go from
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
if
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
has sign
-
, and from
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
to
\mathfrak{m}_{1}
if
\mathfrak{m}_{2}
has sign
+
;
in case (ii) the bond should go from the
\mathfrak{m}_{j}
whose paired child has sign
-
to the one whose paired child has sign
+
.
Note that in case (ii) we allow
\mathfrak{m}_{1}=\mathfrak{m}_{2}
hence a self-connecting bond in
\mathbb{M}
.
Finally, we add one bond from the root with sign
+
to the root with sign
-
;
if one of the roots is a leaf then this bond should be between the other root
and the parent node of the root paired with the leaf.

Definition 5.3

(Decorations of molecules and restricted decorations)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule, suppose we also fix the vectors

c_{v}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}
for each
v\in\mathbb{M}
. we then define a
(c_{v})
-
decoration
(or just a decoration) of
\mathbb{M}
to be a set of vectors
(k_{\ell})
for all bonds
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
, such that
k_{\ell}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}
and

(5.1)

\sum_{\ell\sim v}\zeta_{v,\ell}k_{\ell}=c_{v}

for each atom
v\in\mathbb{M}
. Here the sum is taken over all bonds
\ell\sim v
, and
\zeta_{v,\ell}
equals
1
if
\ell
is outgoing from
v
, and equals
-1
otherwise.
For each such decoration and each I-atom
v
, define also the
resonance factor

(5.2)

\Gamma_{v}=\sum_{\ell\sim v}\zeta_{v,\ell}(|k_{\ell}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\ell})).

Suppose
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
comes from a couple
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
as in Definition
5.2
. Let
\mathfrak{r}_{\pm}
be the root of
\mathcal{T}_{\pm}
, we define a
k
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}
to be a
(c_{v})
-decoration where
c_{v}=0
for all
v
, and
k_{\ell_{0}}=k
where
\ell_{0}
is the bond connecting two roots (cf. Definition
5.1
).

Given any
k
-decoration of
\mathcal{Q}
in the sense of Definition
3.5
,
define a
k
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
, such that for each bond
\ell
:

•

If
\ell
corresponds to a leaf pair in
\mathcal{Q}
between leaves
\mathfrak{l}
and
\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}
, then define
k_{\ell}:=k_{\mathfrak{l}}=k_{\mathfrak{l}^{\prime}}
.

•

If
\ell
corresponds to a branching node
\mathfrak{n}
which is a child of another branching node
\mathfrak{m}
,
then define
k_{\ell}:=k_{\mathfrak{n}}
;

•

If
\ell
corresponds to the two roots, the define
k_{\ell}=k
.

It is easy to verify that this
k_{\ell}
is well-defined, and gives a one-to-one correspondence between

k
-decorations of
\mathcal{Q}
and
k
-decoration of
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
. Moreover for such decorations we have

(5.3)

\Gamma_{v}=-\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}(v)}\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}(v)}

for each I-atom
v
(see Remark
5.5
below for the slight abuse of notation that we are making here).

Finally, given
\beta_{v}\in\mathbb{R}
for each
v\in\mathbb{M}_{I}
and
k_{\ell}^{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{R}

for each
\ell\in\mathbb{M}
, we define a decoration
(k_{\ell})
to be
restricted by

(\beta_{v})
and/or
(k_{\ell}^{0})
, if we have
|\Gamma_{v}-\beta_{v}|\leq T_{1}^{-1}

for each I-atom
v
and/or
|k_{\ell}-k_{\ell}^{0}|\leq 1
for each bond
\ell
.
In what follows we will assume, without loss of generality, that
|k^{0}_{\ell}|\lesssim 1

by using the fast decay of the functions
\psi=\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
(see (
1.4
) and (
3.36
)).
Similarly, we can also disregard the factors of
\langle k^{0}\rangle
which appear in the counting estimates
(
3.51
)-(
3.52
) and (
3.53
).

Definition 5.4

(Counting Problem)
.

For any molecule
\mathbb{M}
, define the counting problem (abbreviated CP) denoted by
\mathfrak{C}
, to be the
supremum in the parameters
\beta_{v}
and
k_{\ell}^{0}
, of the number of restricted decorations
in the sense of Definition
5.3
.

Remark 5.5

(Resonance factors)
.

Note how we are only using
\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}
to modify the frequencies of oscillations in
(
5.2
), instead of the full factor in the definition of
\Gamma
in (
3.22
).
Using only
\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}

is equivalent to using the whole
\Gamma
, since the size of
\Gamma(t)-\epsilon^{2}t\Gamma_{0}
is less than
T_{1}^{-1}
(see also Proposition
3.7
), and therefore it does not impact the oscillation
and counting estimates.
In the rest of the argument in this section, e.g. in (
5.3
),
we are then dropping the distinction between
\Gamma(t)
and
\epsilon^{2}t\Gamma_{0}
;
cfr. (
5.3
) with (
3.25
) and (
3.28
).

Remark 5.6

(Restriction on decoration after splicing)
.

For the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})

which is the result of splicing any set of irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
as in Definition
4.4
,
where the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is defined as in Definition
4.4
,
in the definition of
\mathfrak{C}
above we shall further restrict
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
,
as in Proposition
4.5
.
Here we note that, under the one-to-one correspondence between decorations of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
,
the condition
|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0j}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0j}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}

is translated to
|k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}|\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}

for some two bonds
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
of opposite sign at
the I-atom
v_{j}\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
corresponding to the I-branching node
\mathfrak{n}_{0j}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
.
For more details see Definition
5.11
.

5.2.
Choice of the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}

To prove the estimate for (
4.11
),
in this subsection we will choose a set of disjoint irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
in
\mathcal{Q}
, and splice them as in Definition
4.4
, to form the new couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
.
Then, using Proposition
4.5
, we can reduce (
4.11
) to an expression of
the form (
4.11
) which involves the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
instead of
\mathcal{Q}
.
We start with a lemma concerning chains of double bonds in molecules.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}
be a couple, and
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
be the corresponding molecule as in Definition
5.2
. Suppose there exists a chain of double bonds in
\mathbb{M}
,
i.e. a chain of molecules
v_{1},\cdots,v_{q}

of degree 4
,
and each
v_{j}
and
v_{j+1}
are connected by a double bond. Then exactly one of the followings is true:

(i)

The atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq q)
form an irregular chain in the sense of Definition
4.2
;
or

(ii)

The atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
and the atoms
\mathfrak{n}_{j}\,(r+1\leq j\leq q)

form two irregular chains, and
\mathfrak{n}_{r}
and
\mathfrak{n}_{r+1}
each has two children nodes that form two leaf pairs.

We shall call the chain in case (i) an irregular chain of
type (CL)

and the chain in case (ii) an irregular chain of
type (CN)
.
Note that if we remove all the type (CN) chains from
\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
, then this molecule still remains connected.

Proof.

This is proved in
[
18
]
which actually only deals with the case where all branching nodes in
\mathcal{Q}
have 3 children,
but since all the branching nodes
\mathfrak{n}_{j}
considered here do have 3 children, the proof is the same.
∎

Remark 5.8

(Structure of chains in molecules)
.

By Lemma
5.7
we know that (i) each irregular chain in
\mathcal{Q}
corresponds
to a chain of double bonds in
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
; and (ii) each chain of double bond in
\mathbb{M}
corresponds to either one irregular chain or two irregular chains connected by two leaf pairs.
Moreover, for any irregular chain
\mathcal{H}

and the corresponding double bond chain
\mathbb{H}
in
\mathbb{M}
,
if
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is the result of splicing
\mathcal{H}
as in Definition
4.4
, then
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})
can be formed from
\mathbb{M}
by merging all atoms in
\mathbb{H}
to a single atom.

Definition 5.9
.

Given a couple
\mathcal{Q}
and the corresponding molecule
\mathbb{M}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q})
, define
\mathscr{V}=\{\mathcal{H}_{j}\}
to be the set of all maximal irregular chains (i.e. those that are not proper subsets of any other irregular chain) in
\mathcal{Q}
. Note that these
\mathcal{H}_{j}
must be disjoint. Subsequently, define the congruence relation
\equiv
as in Definition
4.2
associated with these
\mathcal{H}_{j}
.

Using Definition
5.9
, we can state the main estimate in this section as follows:

Proposition 5.10
.

Consider the expression

(5.4)

\mathcal{I}(t,k):=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}}(t,k)

as in (
4.11
),
where the congruence relation is defined using the set of irregular chains
\mathscr{V}=\{\mathcal{H}_{j}\}

in Definition
5.9
. Then we have the following estimate

(5.5)

R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\lesssim\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta r/8}

uniformly in
t
, where
r=r(\mathcal{Q})
is the rank of
\mathcal{Q}
(cf. Definition
3.2
),
and we assume
\mathcal{Q}
is nontrivial.

Definition 5.11

(Gaps and tame atoms)
.

Given any irregular chain
\mathcal{H}\subset\mathcal{Q}
and
any decoration

(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
of
\mathcal{Q}
, define the
gap
of
\mathcal{H}
to be
r:=|k_{\mathfrak{n}_{0}}-k_{\mathfrak{p}_{0}}|
in the notations of Definition
4.1
. We say
\mathcal{H}
is
small gap
(SG) or
large gap
(LG) with respect to this decoration, if
r\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
or
r>\epsilon^{2-\theta}
. Note also that
r=|k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}|
where
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are the two bonds at one endpoint of
\mathbb{H}
other than the double bond (and
\mathbb{H}
is the double bond chain in
\mathbb{M}
corresponding to
\mathcal{H}
), cf. Remark
5.6
.

More generally, let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule,
v
be an atom and
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})

be two bonds at
v
with opposite directions. Given a decoration
(k_{\ell})
of
\mathbb{M}
,
we define the
gap
of the atom
v
at the two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
relative to this decoration, to be

\rho:=|k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{2}}|.

We say the gap is
large
(large gap, or LG)
if
|\rho|\geq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
, and is
small
(small gap, or SG) if
|\rho|<\epsilon^{2-\theta}
.
If
v
is a degree 4 I-atom, we say
v
is
tame
if
v
is SG
in two different ways
;
that is, if the gap at
v
at the bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
is small, then there exists
\ell_{3}\sim v

such that the gap at the bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{3})
or at the bonds
(\ell_{2},\ell_{3})
is also small.

Using Definition
5.11
, we can further reduce the quantity
\mathcal{I}
defined in Proposition
5.10
as follows. First, the definition of
\mathcal{I}
in (
5.4
) involves summation over all decorations
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
of couples
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}
where
\mathcal{Q}^{\prime}\equiv\mathcal{Q}
; by inserting a partition of unity, for each irregular chain
\mathcal{H}_{j}\in\mathscr{V}
we may fix it to be either small gap (SG) or large gap (LG) in the sense of Definition
5.11
. Let
\mathscr{V}_{0}\subset\mathscr{V}
be the set of irregular chains that are SG, then the sum (
5.4
) over all congruent couples can be divided into finitely many sub-sums, such that each sub-sum is taken over all couples that are congruent
with respect to the irregular chains in
\mathscr{V}_{0}
. We shall keep this new notation for congruence for the rest of the proof.

Then, using Proposition
4.5
, we can reduce the quantity
\mathcal{I}

in (
5.4
) to a finite sum of terms that have the same form as the right hand side of (
4.11
).
The next proposition studies such quantities and reduce their estimate
to the counting problem for restricted decorations of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}:=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})
,
as defined in Definitions
5.3
and
5.4

Proposition 5.12
.

The quantity
\mathcal{I}
defined in (
5.4
) satisfies the bound

(5.6)

R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{-\chi}\cdot T_{1}^{-(E-2V+V_{N})}\cdot(\log R)^{C}\cdot\mathfrak{C},

where the relevant quantities
E,V,\chi
etc. are as in Definitions
5.1
-
5.2

and are all associated with the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
,
and
\mathfrak{C}
is (the supremum of) the number of restricted decorations for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}

as in Definition
5.3
, with the extra restrictions as in Remark
5.6
.

Proof.

As discussed above, to estimate
\mathcal{I}
we only need to estimate each term that has the form of the right hand side of (
4.11
). First, using the bound (
4.12
) for
\mathcal{G}
, we can expand
\mathcal{G}
in (
4.11
) as a linear combination, with summable coefficients, of functions of form

\mathcal{G}_{1}(k_{\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}})\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}e^{i\lambda_{\mathfrak{n}}\cdot t_{\mathfrak{n}}},

where
\lambda_{\mathfrak{n}}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}
is a uniformly bounded function of the
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
variables. Then we integrate in the time variables
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
using the bounds (
3.44
)–(
3.45
); note that these bounds do not change with the frequency shifts
e^{i\lambda_{\mathfrak{n}}t_{\mathfrak{n}}}
and are uniform in
(\lambda_{\mathfrak{n}})
, which is easily seen by examining the proof of Proposition
3.10
.

Now, we may replace the integral in the
(t_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{sb}}}

variables by
\mathcal{A}((\alpha_{\mathfrak{n}}^{0})_{\mathfrak{n}})
in (
3.44
);
using (
3.45
), with a loss of at most
(\log R)^{C}
,
we can restrict the value of each
(\alpha_{\mathfrak{n}}^{0})_{\mathfrak{n}}
to a unit interval, which means that each

\Omega_{\mathfrak{n}}
is restricted to a fixed interval of length
T_{1}^{-1}
.
Moreover, using the decay of
\widetilde{\psi}(k_{\mathfrak{l}})
in
k_{\mathfrak{l}}

in (
4.11
), we may absorb the weight
\langle k\rangle^{10}

and restrict the value of
k_{\mathfrak{l}}
for each leaf
\mathfrak{l}
to a unit ball;
subsequently we may also restrict
k_{\mathfrak{n}}
to a unit ball for each node
\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Then, by Definition
5.3
, we then know that the decoration

(k_{\mathfrak{n}})_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}}
is in one-to-one correspondence with a decoration
(k_{\ell})
of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
which is a
restricted
decoration in the sense of Definition
5.3
. Therefore the summation of all the decorations
(k_{\mathfrak{n}})
and integration in all the time variables
(t_{\mathfrak{m}})
in (
4.11
) contributes at most
\mathfrak{C}
as in Definition
5.3
. Here note that summing over all
k
-decorations and the summing over all
k
is equal to summing over all decorations.

Finally we calculate the powers of
R
,
\epsilon
and
T_{1}
in (
4.11
)
and match them with the powers in (
5.6
);
in view of the definition (
5.4
) it suffices to show that

(5.7)

(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})-2}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})}=R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{-\chi}\cdot T_{1}^{-(E-2V+V_{N})}.

To prove (
5.7
), first note that the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
is connected (i.e.
F=1
),
since otherwise each of the two trees forming
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
must have all its leaves paired by themselves,
which implies that
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k=0
for the root
\mathfrak{r}
of each tree;
but this implies that the contribution of this term to the right hand side of
(
4.11
) must vanish due to the symbols
\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}
and

\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}
in (
3.36
) (which enter into the
\mathcal{G}
factor in (
4.11
))
and the fact that they vanish for
k_{\mathfrak{r}}=k=0
as stated in Proposition
2.1
.
We then notice that the powers of
T_{1}
match in (
5.7
) since
the power on the left hand-side is
n_{I}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})=V_{I}=V-V_{N}

and the one on the right-hand side is
\chi-1-E+2V-V_{N}=F-1+V-V_{N}
.

To match the powers of
R
and
\epsilon
, it suffices to show that

(5.8)

r(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})-2=2\chi-2.

To verify (
5.8
) observe that, by induction, we have

r(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})-2=\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{B}}(r(\mathfrak{n})-1)

where
r(\mathfrak{n})
is the number of children nodes of the branching node
\mathfrak{n}
;
then, note that
r(\mathfrak{n})=d(v)-1
where
v=v(\mathfrak{n})
is the atom corresponding to the branching node
\mathfrak{n}
,
and that

\sum_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{B}}(d(v)-2)=2E-2V=2\chi-2F.

The equality (
5.7
) then follows from these observations, and this completes the proof of (
5.6
).
∎

Recall that we denote by
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})

the molecule obtained from the couple
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, which in turn is obtained from
\mathcal{Q}

by splicing the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, see Definition
4.4
and Proposition
4.5
.
In the next proposition, we list the properties of this molecule which will be used in the proof below.

Proposition 5.13

(Properties of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
)
.

Assume that the couple
\mathcal{Q}
occurring in the definition (
5.4
) of
\mathcal{I}
is admissible
according to Definition
3.3
.
Then, the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})
is connected and satisfies the following properties:

(1)

(Double bonds)
For each double bond in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
that is SG under the decoration (see Definition
5.11
),
it satisfies one of the following
4
properties:

(a)

One of its endpoints is an N-atom;

(b)

One of its endpoints is an I-atom
of degree at least 5
;

(c)

At each endpoint
v
of this double bond, we can find two bonds of
opposite direction that is also SG, such that
exactly one
of these two bonds belong to the given double bond;
in other words each endpoint
v
is ‘tame’, according to Definition
5.11
.

(d)

This double bond belongs to a set
\mathcal{Y}
of double bonds such that the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}

remains connected
after removing all double bonds in
\mathcal{Y}
.

(2)

(Connectivity property)
For each degree 4 atom
v
and two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
of opposite signs at
v
,

any other atom
must be connected to
v
by a path that
does not include
\ell_{1}
or
\ell_{2}
.

Proof.

Proof of (1)
. Suppose we have an SG double bond
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
between two atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
,
such that both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are I-atoms of degree 4 (otherwise we are in case (a) or (b)).
Now, suppose this same double bond
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
that exists in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}

does not exist
in the original molecule
\mathbb{M}
before merging the double bond chains
(cf. Remark
5.8
), then the two bonds
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})

must belong to two different ends of the double bond chain before merging,
which then implies that we are in case (c), that is,
\ell
must be SG with a bond other than
\ell^{\prime}

(see Definition
5.11
).
Suppose instead that this double bond
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})

exists in the original molecule
\mathbb{M}
and is preserved under merging the double bond chains.
Then we shall put this double bond into the set
\mathcal{Y}
,
and it suffices to prove that
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
remains connected after removing the double bonds in
\mathcal{Y}
.
The proof is basically the same as in
[
18
]
;
note that by Lemma
5.7
and our choice of
\mathscr{V}_{0}
, any SG double bond in
\mathcal{Y}

must be coming from two leaf pairs (it is of type CN) as in Lemma
5.7
.
If we remove all double bonds in
\mathcal{Y}
,
then the remaining molecule is still connected because all the bonds corresponding
to parent-child relations are still present, as well as the bond connecting the two roots (cf. Definition
5.2
).

Proof of (2)
.
Suppose
(v,\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
are as stated, and define
Z
to be the set of atoms other than
v
that are connected to
v
by a path not including
\ell_{1}
nor
\ell_{2}
. If
Z\neq\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, then for
any decoration
, by adding up the equations (
5.1
) for all atoms in
Z\cup\{v\}
, we conclude that
k_{\ell_{1}}=k_{\ell_{2}}
. Since this equality holds for
any decoration
, which may come from any decoration of
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, and note also that the branching node
\mathfrak{n}
corresponding to
v
has exactly 3 children, we then see that: there exist two children nodes
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime},\mathfrak{n}^{\prime\prime}
of
\mathfrak{n}
with opposite signs, such that
k_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}}=k_{\mathfrak{n}^{\prime\prime}}
for all decorations. As the decorations of leaf pairs are free variables and the decorations of branching nodes are completely determined by them as in Definition
3.5
, we conclude that the leaves in the subtrees at
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{n}^{\prime\prime}
must be completely paired, which contradicts the admissibility assumption, cf. Definition
3.3
.
∎

Remark 5.14

(Trees admissibility and molecule connectivity)
.

We saw in the proof of Proposition
5.13
that the connectivity property in (2)
holds in view of the ‘admissibility’ of the trees that are forming our couples (hence the molecules).
This property is important in what follows to avoid unfavorable scenarios in the cutting algorithm
and counting estimates in the rest of the section.
Importantly, the ‘admissibility’ of the tress is a consequence of our renormalization procedure
which involves the ‘gauge’
\Gamma_{1}
in (
3.7
) (see also (
3.22
)-(
3.24
)).

Lemma 5.15

(Degrees and power of
T_{1}
)
.

For each atom
v\in\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
we have
d(v)\geq 4
if
v
is I-atom,
and
d(v)\geq 3
if
v
is N-atom, where
d(v)
is the degree of
v
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
.
Moreover, for the quantity
T_{1}^{-(E-2V+V_{N})}
in (
5.6
), we have

(5.9)

T_{1}^{-(E-2V+V_{N})}=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{I}}T_{1}^{-(d(v)-4)_{+}/2}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{N}}T_{1}^{-1/2-(d(v)-3)_{+}/2}.

Proof.

The inequality
d(v)\geq 4
for I-atoms and
d(v)\geq 3
for N-atoms follow directly because if
v

corresponds to the branching node
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
,
then
d(v)
is equal to the number of children nodes of
\mathfrak{n}
plus
1
.
In the equality (
5.9
) we may then remove the positive part symbol;
(
5.9
)
then follows from the fact that
\sum_{v}d(v)=2E
and, therefore,

E-2V+V_{N}=E-2V_{I}-V_{N}=(1/2)\sum_{v}d(v)-2\sum_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{I}}1-\sum_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{N}}1
.
∎

Definition 5.16

(The main Counting Quantity)
.

For the rest of this section we define the main Counting Quantity (abbreviated CQ) associated to a molecule

\mathbb{M}
contained in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
as

(5.10)

\mathfrak{A}:=\mathfrak{C}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{-\chi}\cdot\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{I}}T_{1}^{-(d_{0}(v)-4)_{+}/2}\prod_{v\in\mathbb{M}_{N}}T_{1}^{-1/2-(d_{0}(v)-3)_{+}/2},

where
d_{0}(v)
is the (initial) degree of the atom
v
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
;
in other words, we understand that even if the degree of
v
changes in subsequent operations
(e.g. cutting of some bonds or removal of some atoms, see later sections) the value of
d_{0}(v)
will stay the same.
\mathfrak{C}
is the counting problem from Definition
5.4

associated to
\mathbb{M}
.

With the above definition, in view of Lemma
5.15
and (
5.6
), we have (recall (
5.4
))

(5.11)

R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}(\log R)^{C}\cdot\mathfrak{A}_{sb};

consistently with Proposition
5.12
, here
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{sb}}

is the CQ associated to the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
.

Remark 5.17

(About the symbols)
.

Notice that in (
5.11
) we are disregarding the sizes of the symbols of the interactions associated
to each node, that is, the
\widetilde{n}
in the formula (
3.36
), and the
\mathcal{G}
in (
4.11
).
This is natural in view of the restrictions on decorations in Definition
5.3
, and of (
4.12
).
At the same time, when the size of the frequencies is smaller than
1
, the symbols
\widetilde{n}
can
potentially help the estimates in view of (
2.11
).
We are in fact going to use this property for the cubic symbol
n_{++-}
in the proof of Proposition
5.20
below.
However, we are not going to carry explicitly the dependence on this symbol throughout the proof, as
it does not play a role elsewhere.

5.3.
The cutting operation and cutting algorithm

We now define the operation of
cutting
.

Definition 5.18

(
\alpha
- and
\beta
-cuts)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}
be a molecule
v
be an atom of degree 4. Note that
v
must have two incoming bonds and two outgoing bonds.
Let
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
be two bonds at
v
and
(\ell_{3},\ell_{4})
be the other two bonds.

We define the
cut
at the atom
v
, relative to
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, to be the following operation:
we split
v
into two atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
. Then we connect
v_{1}
to the two atoms that are end points of
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
other than
v
, and connect
v_{2}
to the two atoms that are end points of
\ell_{3}
and
\ell_{4}
other than
v
.
The rest of the molecule remains unchanged.
In particular in the resulting molecule, both
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
have degree
2
. See Figure
3
for an illustration.

We define this cut to be an
\alpha
-cut
, and call the resulting atoms
\alpha
-atoms
,
if this cutting operation does not create a new connected component.
In this case we
further require that
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
(and thus
(\ell_{3},\ell_{4})
)
are in opposite directions and
v
is SG with respect to them.

If this cutting creates a new connected component, then we define it to be
\beta
-cut
,
and call the resulting atoms
\beta
-atoms
.
In this latter case we do not make any restrictions on the directions
\ell_{j}
and the SG/LG of the atom
v
.

Figure 3.
An example of a cut as in Definition
5.18
.

Definition 5.19
.

In connection with Definition
5.18
,
we define the following convention for the restricted decorations of molecules with
\alpha
and
\beta
-atoms:

•

For each
\alpha
-atom
v
with two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
, we require that both equations (
5.1
) and

|\Gamma_{v}-\beta_{v}|\leq T_{1}^{-1}
(with
\Gamma_{v}
defined in (
5.2
)) hold at
v
;

•

For each
\beta
-atom
v
with two bonds
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
,
we
only require
(
5.1
)
holds at
v
and do not make requirements about
\Gamma_{v}
;

•

Nevertheless, if we naturally turn all the
\beta
-atoms into pairs
(where the two
\beta
atoms created in the same
\beta
cutting are naturally paired), then in the restricted decoration we also require that
\Gamma_{v}+\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}=(\mathrm{const})+O(T_{1}^{-1})
for any
pair
of
\beta
-atoms
v
and
v^{\prime}
,
where
\Gamma_{v}
is defined in (
5.2
).

In what follows, when we talk about restricted decorations of molecules that contain
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms
(i.e. formed after cutting in Definition
5.18
), we always take the interpretation as described above,
and define the quantities
\mathfrak{C}
and
\mathfrak{A}
, as in Definitions
5.4
and
5.16
, accordingly.

Now we describe the cutting algorithm.
Start with the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})
.
Recall the subset
\mathcal{Y}
of double bonds in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
defined in Proposition
5.13
(1d).

Step 1
: for each double bond in
\mathcal{Y}
such that both endpoints are degree 4 I-atoms,
we remove it (so that both endpoints become degree 2 atoms).
We call each of the two endpoints an
\alpha
-atom;
note that this is consistent with Definition
5.18
and Proposition
5.13
.

Step 2
: For each of the remaining degree 4 I-atoms
that is SG and not tame

(cf. Definition
5.11
), we perform a cutting operation at this atom along the two bonds that are SG;
by this we mean that if
\ell_{1}
and
\ell_{2}
are SG at such a degree 4 I-atom, we cut
v
into

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
, so that
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\sim v_{1}
.
Repeat this process until there are no more degree 4 I-atoms that are SG and not tame.

Step 3
: For each of the remaining degree 4 I-atoms
that are not tame
(and thus are LG),
where it is possible to perform a
\beta
-cut
, we then perform a
\beta
-cut at this atom.
Repeat this process until this is no longer possible.

The next proposition will summarize how the counting quantity
\mathfrak{A}
defined in (
5.10
) changes
with each operation in Steps 1–3 defined above.

Proposition 5.20

(Change in the Counting Quantity)
.

Consider each of the Steps 1–3 defined above; for each of them,
consider the value of
\mathfrak{A}
for the molecule before and after this step,
which we denote by
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}
.
Then we have

(5.12)

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\lesssim\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta|\mathcal{Y}|}\cdot\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{1/2}\epsilon^{2-\theta}T_{1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{V_{\beta}-2\Delta F}

where
|\mathcal{Y}|
is the number of edges in
\mathcal{Y}
,

\prod_{(\alpha)}
is taken over all
\alpha
atoms,
(and each
\alpha
-atom is associated with a gap
\lambda\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
),

V_{\beta}
is the number of
\beta
-atoms generated by this step,
and
\Delta F
is the increment of the number of components
F
under after this step.

Similarly to
\Delta F
, we will use
\Delta E
,
\Delta V
,
\Delta\chi
to denote the increment
of the respective quantities after each step in the cutting algorithm.

Remark 5.21

(SG at
\alpha
-atoms)
.

Note that each
\alpha
-atom in the product in (
5.12
)
is associated with a small gap
\lambda\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
.
Indeed, if two
\alpha
-atoms
v_{1}
and
v_{2}
are created in Step 1
this means that the double bond existing between them before the ‘removal’ operation
belonged to the set
\mathcal{Y}
so it is SG by definition; therefore, the remaining two bonds at

v_{1}
and
v_{2}
must also be SG since (
5.1
) holds at both ends with
c_{v_{1}}=c_{v_{2}}=0
.
If instead
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are created in Step 2, the gap is small by the definition of the operation.

Proof of Proposition
5.20
.

We consider each of the Steps 1–3 and first note that, by the definition (
5.10
), we have

(5.13)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\lesssim\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{\Delta\chi}

since we are only dealing with degree 4 I-atoms.

(1)

In Step 1, we cut out a double bond in
\mathcal{Y}
. By Lemma
5.7
, after cutting out this double bond, the molecule still remains connected; therefore after this cutting we have

\Delta E=-2,\quad\Delta V=0,\quad\Delta F=0,\quad V_{\beta}=0,

and thus
\Delta\chi=-2
.

Now, to fix the decoration of the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
,
we need to fix the two vectors
(k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}})
corresponding to the edges
(\ell_{1},\ell_{2})
in this double bond.
By the SG assumption, since one of them will have
\lesssim R
choices,
the other one will have
\lesssim R\lambda
choices with gap
\lambda\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
.
This gives
\lesssim R^{2}\lambda
choices for
(k_{\ell_{1}},k_{\ell_{2}})
, and once they are fixed,
we are reduced to a counting problem for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
, and thus

\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq R^{2}\lambda\cdot\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}.

By (
5.13
) we obtain

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\lesssim R^{2}\lambda\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{-2}=(\lambda^{1/2}\epsilon^{2-\theta}T_{1})^{2}\epsilon^{2\theta},

which is exactly what we need for (
5.12
)
since this cutting produced two
\alpha
atoms and removes a double bond (hence, two edges) in
\mathcal{Y}
.

(2)

In Steps 2 and 3, each time we are performing one cut at one atom.
If this cut is a
\beta
-cut, let the two edges involved be
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
.
By definition, after performing the cut, the molecule breaks into two connected components (so
\Delta F=1
);
by adding the equation (
5.1
) for
all atoms in one of these components
,
we conclude that the value
k_{\ell}\pm k_{\ell^{\prime}}
is
fixed

for any decoration (where the
\pm
sign depends on the
directions of
(\ell,\ell^{\prime})
at this
\beta
cut).
In view of the convention for decorations in Definition
5.19
,
this means that the counting problem for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}

are equivalent to each other, hence
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}
,
and thus
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}
, noting that
\Delta\chi=0
after this cut
as
\Delta E=0
,
\Delta V=1
and
\Delta F=1
and
V_{\beta}=2
.

Alternatively, if this cut is an
\alpha
-cut, then we have
\Delta\chi=-1
.
Let us call
v
the atom in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}
the two
\alpha
-atoms
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
generated by the cut.
Let the bonds at
v
be
\ell_{1},\ell_{1}^{\prime}
and
\ell_{2},\ell_{2}^{\prime}
so that
\ell_{j},\ell_{j}^{\prime}\sim\alpha_{j}
,
j=1,2

and recall that
(\ell_{j},\ell_{j}^{\prime})
are SG. Let us also assume, without loss of generality, that
|\ell_{1}|\gtrsim|\ell_{1}^{\prime}|
.
In this case we shall fix the value of
k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell^{\prime}_{1}}
so that
|k_{\ell_{1}}-k_{\ell_{1}^{\prime}}|\lesssim\lambda
,
and fix the interval of length
T_{1}^{-1}
that contains

\Phi(\ell_{1},\ell_{1}^{\prime}):=|k_{\ell}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\ell})-(|k_{\ell^{\prime}}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\ell^{\prime}}))
;
making these choices reduces the counting problem for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
to that of
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}}
.
The number of choices for
k_{\ell}-k_{\ell^{\prime}}
are
\lesssim R\lambda
.
Once this choice has been made the value of
\Phi(\ell_{1},\ell_{1}^{\prime})
lies in a union of intervals of length
T_{1}^{-1}

whose total length is
\lesssim|\Phi(\ell_{1},\ell_{1}^{\prime})|+T_{1}^{-1}\lesssim\lambda(\sqrt{|k_{\ell_{1}}|}+\sqrt{|k_{\ell_{1}^{\prime}}|})^{-1}+T_{1}^{-1}
.
This gives approximately
T_{1}\lambda|k_{\ell_{1}}|^{-1/2}
choices.
To compensate for the (possibly large) factor of
|k_{\ell_{1}}|^{-1/2}
, we use the vanishing
of the symbols as stated in (
2.11
) in Proposition
2.1
; see
also the explanation in Remark
5.17
.
Therefore, taking into account the size of the symbol,
we get that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}\lesssim\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}\cdot R\lambda\cdot\lambda T_{1},

and thus, from (
5.13
),

(5.15)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\lesssim(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{-1}\cdot R\lambda^{2}T_{1}=\lambda^{2}\epsilon^{2}T_{1}^{2}.

Recalling that the gap
\lambda\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}

we see that (
5.15
) is better than what we need for (
5.12
),
which is
(\lambda^{1/2}\epsilon^{2-\theta}T_{1})^{2}
,
since this cut generates two
\alpha
-atoms. ∎

After completing the above cutting operations, the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}=\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}})

is then reduced to a new molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
. The properties of
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
are described in the following proposition.

Proposition 5.22

(Properties of the reduced molecule)
.

The molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
may contain multiple connected components, let
\mathbb{M}_{0}
be any such component.
All the atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
that are not
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atoms are called
\varepsilon
-atoms.

(1)

Unless
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
has only one component, each component
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least one
\beta
-atom.
Moreover, no component
\mathbb{M}_{0}
is formed by one SG double bond only.

(2)

Each degree 4 I-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
(which must be an
\varepsilon
-atom) that is not tame must have
LG in the sense of Definition
5.11
.
Moreover, there is
no
degree 4 I-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
which is not tame and
at which one can perform a
\beta
-cut in the sense of Definition
5.18
.

(3)

Suppose
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least one
\varepsilon
-atom. Then all the
\alpha
and
\beta
-atoms form several chains; for each chain, either it has two endpoints at two different
\varepsilon
-atoms, or its two endpoints coincide at one
\varepsilon
-atom, and in the latter case this
\varepsilon
-atom must be an N-atom, or tame, or has degree at least 5.

Proof.

(1) Note that all the cuts in Step 1 do not create new component, by Proposition
5.13
.
Now, unless there is no
\beta
cut (in which case no new component is formed), each new component that is created must contain a
\beta
-atom which is formed precisely at the cut when this new component is separated from its complement.

Next, note that the SG double bonds in
\mathcal{Y}
(cf. Proposition
5.13
) have been removed in Step 1.
For the other SG double bonds in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
, either one of its endpoints
is an N-atom or has degree at least 5 so it will not be cut in Steps 1–3,
or both its endpoints are tame due to Proposition
5.13
(1c),
so neither of them will be cut in Steps 1–3.
In any case this double bond will not be separated from its complement.

(2) This follows from the definition of Steps 2 and 3;
if any such degree 4 I-atom
v
exists, then this will have been cut either in Step 2 or Step 3
and will not remain degree 4 in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
.

(3) Note that each
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atom must have degree 2, thus they must form several chains.
Each such chain must have two endpoints at two
\varepsilon
-atoms.
Assume that the endpoints of a chain of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms coincide
at an
\varepsilon
-atom, and call this atom
v
.
If
v
is not an
N
-atom or does not have degree more than 5, it is a degree 4 I-atom;
then, by (2) above,
v
must be tame, because otherwise it would be a non-tame degree 4 I-atom at which
we can perform a
\beta
-cut to disconnect the chain.
∎

5.4.
Counting estimates for reduced molecules

Here is the main result of this section.

Proposition 5.23
.

For each of the connected component
\mathbb{M}_{0}
of the reduced molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
after the above ‘cutting’ steps,
consider the counting problem associated with
\mathbb{M}_{0}
. Then for each
\mathbb{M}_{0}
we have

(5.16)

\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\epsilon^{\theta E/4}\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{2G-V_{\beta}},

where the product is taken over all
\alpha
-atoms, and
V_{\beta}
is the number of
\beta
-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
.
Finally
E
is the number of edges in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, and
G
equals
1

for each component
\mathbb{M}_{0}
if
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
contains at least two components, and
G=0
otherwise.

Before proving Proposition
5.23
we want to prove a bound
concerning the increment of
\mathfrak{A}
under the operation of
removing one single atom
at a time from
\mathbb{M}_{0}
(or intermediate molecules).
We are going to need the following simple lemma about restricted decorations of
\mathbb{M}_{0}
:

Lemma 5.24
.

Recall the convention about restricted decorations of molecules with
\alpha
and
\beta
-atoms
as in Definition
5.19
. Then, for any
\beta
-atom
v
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
,
the value of
\Gamma_{v}
(cf. (
5.2
)) must equal a fixed algebraic sum
of
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
for
some set of N-atoms

v^{\prime}
, plus a constant, up to error
O(T_{1}^{-1})
.

We postpone the proof of Lemma
5.24
to the end of this subsection,
and proceed to prove the following:

Proposition 5.25

(Atom removal)
.

Consider the process of reducing
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, as defined in Proposition
5.22
,
where we remove one atom each time.
For each step in this process, consider the
\mathfrak{A}
value before and after this step,
which we denote by
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}
and
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}
.
Note that in the definition (
5.10
) of
\mathfrak{A}
,
our convention is to keep the value of
d_{0}(v)
fixed under the reduction of
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, so that it remains the initial degree
of the atom
v
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
(and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}}
).

Suppose we remove an atom
v_{0}
in the given step, let
d_{0}(v_{0})
be the degree of
v_{0}
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
,
and
d(v_{0})
be the degree of
v_{0}
in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
.
We also assume that:

(i)

if
v_{0}
is an I-atom, then either
d_{0}(v_{0})=4
and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}=\mathbb{M}_{0}

(i.e. we are at the first operation step) or
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
contains no N-atoms at all; and

(ii)

if
v_{0}
is an I-atom and
d_{0}(v_{0})=4
, then it must be either LG or tame in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
.

Then, if
\Delta\chi:=\chi_{\mathrm{pos}}-\chi_{\mathrm{pre}}=0
in this step,
we have
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}
.

If, instead,
\Delta\chi<0
we have

(5.17)

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta d/2}\cdot\mu,

where
d
is the degree of
v_{0}
in the molecule
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
before this step,
and
\mu=1
if

(1)

v_{0}
is an N-atom and
d(v_{0})=d_{0}(v_{0})=3
, or

(2)

v_{0}
is an I-atom and
d(v_{0})\in\{4,5\}
and
d_{0}(v_{0})=5
, or

(3)

v_{0}
is an I-atom,
d_{0}(v_{0})=4
, and either
d(v_{0})<4

or
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
has a
\beta
-cut for
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
,

whereas

\mu=\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}

otherwise.

Proof.

First, let us record that

(5.18)

\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\leq\frac{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{\Delta\chi}\prod_{v\in(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}})_{I}\smallsetminus(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}})_{I}}T_{1}^{-(d_{0}(v)-4)_{+}/2}\prod_{v\in(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}})_{N}\smallsetminus(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pos}})_{N}}T_{1}^{-1/2-(d_{0}(v)-3)_{+}/2},

and recall that
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
with
\theta_{0}\gg\theta

(which will be used in the numerology calculations below).
Let
d:=d(v_{0})
and
d_{0}:=d_{0}(v_{0})
.
Suppose the connected component containing
v_{0}
becomes
q
connected components after removing
v_{0}
,
then
1\leq q\leq d
and
\Delta\chi=q-d
since
\Delta E=-d
,
\Delta V=-1
, and
\Delta F=q-1
.
If
q=d
(i.e.
\Delta\chi=0
) then each of these new components has exactly one bond
\ell
connecting to
v_{0}
,
and by adding the equations (
5.1
) for all atoms in each component,
we get that the value of each
k_{\ell}
must be fixed.
This implies that
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}
and thus
\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}
as desired.

Now assume
\Delta\chi<0
.
Consider the
q
components after removing
v_{0}
, and for the
j
-th component, consider the set of bonds connecting atoms in this component to
v_{0}
, let this set be
A_{j}
and let
p_{j}:=|A_{j}|
, so that
\sum_{j}p_{j}=d
.
By adding the equations (
5.1
) for all atoms in the
j
-th component, we get

(5.19)

\sum_{\ell\in A_{j}}\zeta_{v,\ell}k_{\ell}=(\mathrm{const}),\qquad j=1,\dots,q.

If
v_{0}
is N-atom, then (
5.19
) is the only equation(s) we will rely on.
In this case the total number of solutions to (
5.19
) is
\prod_{j}R^{p_{j}-1}=R^{d-q}

and thus
\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{C}_{\mathrm{pos}}R^{d-q}
;
therefore by (
5.18
) we have

(5.20)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\leq R^{d-q}\cdot(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{q-d}\cdot T_{1}^{-(d_{0}-2)/2}=(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{d-q}\cdot T_{1}^{-(d_{0}-2)/2}.

If
q=1
and
d=d_{0}=3
, the above right-hand side is
\epsilon^{4}T_{1}^{3/2}\leq\epsilon^{3\theta_{0}/2}
.
In all other cases (since
1\leq q<d\leq d_{0}\in\{3,4\}
) the right hand side of (
5.20
) can be improved to

\epsilon^{\theta_{0}d/2}\cdot(\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})
. This proves (
5.17
) when
v_{0}
is an
N
-atom.

Now assume
\Delta\chi<0
and
v_{0}
is I-atom, and
d_{0}=d=4
, and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}=\mathbb{M}_{0}
.
If
v_{0}
is tame (i.e. it is SG in two different ways),
using the tame condition we see that the number of solutions to (
5.19
)
is at most
R^{4-q}\epsilon^{2-\theta}
if
q\geq 2
and
R^{3}(\epsilon^{2-\theta})^{2}
if
q=1
;
in either case we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\leq(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{4-q}\cdot(\epsilon^{2-\theta})^{1+\mathbf{1}_{q=1}}\leq\epsilon^{2\theta}\cdot(\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})

which again proves (
5.17
).
If
v_{0}
is not tame, then by (2) in Proposition
5.22
it is LG
and we must have that either

q=1
and
p_{1}=4
, or
q=2
and
(p_{1},p_{2})=(1,3)
; in both cases we must also have

(5.21)

\sum_{\ell\in A_{j}}\zeta_{v,\ell}(|k_{\ell}|^{1/2}+\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k_{\ell}))=(\mathrm{const})+O(T_{1}^{-1})

for each
j
.
In the case
q=1
, by using Proposition
3.12
we get that the number of solutions
to (
5.19
) and (
5.21
) is bounded by
R\cdot R^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\log R
, which implies that

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\leq(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{3}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}\leq\epsilon^{2\theta_{0}}\cdot(\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}),

which matches the desired estimate (
5.17
).
In the case
q=2
, with
(p_{1},p_{2})=(1,3)
we can use again Proposition
3.12

to get that solutions to (
5.19
) and (
5.21
)
are at most
R^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\log R
, which implies

\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}/\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\leq(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}\leq\epsilon^{\theta_{0}}\cdot(\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}),

which suffices for (
5.17
).

Finally, assume that
v_{0}
is I-atom and
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
has no N-atom.
In this case we want to use Lemma
5.24
.
Noticing that all the N-atoms have been removed in previous steps when forming
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
,
we conclude that in
\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{pre}}
, the value of
\Gamma_{v}
belongs
to a fixed interval of length
O(T_{1}^{-1})
for each
v
.
By adding these values for all atoms
v
in the
j
-th connected component
(created after removing
v_{0}
)
we conclude that (
5.21
) holds for each
j
in addition to (
5.19
).
Using this we can then apply Propositions
3.11
and
3.12

to bound the number of solutions to the systems (
5.19
)–(
5.21
) as follows;
recalling that
p_{j}:=|A_{j}|
,
j=1,\dots,q<d
, is the number of bonds connecting the atom

v_{0}
to the
j
-th connected component created by its removal, we have:

•

If
p_{j}=1
the decoration of this single edge is fixed (i.e., there is only one solution);

•

If
p_{j}=2
and
d_{0}\neq 4
we bound the number of possible solutions trivially by
R=R^{p_{j}-1}
;

•

If
p_{j}=2
and
d_{0}=4
we use the
2
-vector counting from Proposition

3.11
to bound the number of solutions by
R(T_{1}\lambda)^{-1}

where
\lambda
is the gap; by the LG hypothesis in (ii) of the statement (see also Proposition
5.22
(2)) the
number of choices is bounded by
R(T_{1}\epsilon^{2})^{-1}\epsilon^{\theta}=(RT_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-2})^{p_{j}-1}\epsilon^{\theta}
;

•

If
p_{j}\geq 3
we first pick
p_{j}-3
edges and then apply the
3
-vector counting from Proposition
3.12

to bound the number of decoration by
R^{p_{j}-3}\cdot R^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\log R\leq R^{p_{j}-1}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}
.

From (
5.18
), and since
\sum_{j=1}^{q}p_{j}-1=d-q
, it follows that

(5.22)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}}&\leq(R\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1})^{q-d}\cdot T_{1}^{-(d_{0}-4)/2}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{d}R^{p_{j}-1}\big(T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-2}\epsilon^{\theta}\big)^{\mathbf{1}(p_{j}=2,d_{0}=4)}\big(T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}\big)^{\mathbf{1}(p_{j}\geq 3)}\\
&=T_{1}^{-(d_{0}-4)/2}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{q}\sigma_{j},\,\quad\mathrm{with}\quad\sigma_{j}:=\left\{\begin{aligned} &1,&\mathrm{if\ }p_{j}=1;\\
&\epsilon^{2}T_{1},&\mathrm{if\ }p_{j}=2\mathrm{\ and\ }d_{0}\neq 4;\\
&\epsilon^{\theta},&\mathrm{if\ }p_{j}=2\mathrm{\ and\ }d_{0}=4;\\
&(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{p_{j}-1}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta},&\mathrm{if\ }p_{j}\geq 3.\end{aligned}\right.\end{split}

Now, using that
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
, we see that
\prod_{j=1}^{q}\sigma_{j}

is maximized in the following scenarios:

•

d_{0}=4
,
d=4
and
(p_{1},p_{2})=(2,2)
, in which case (
5.22
)
is upper bounded by
\epsilon^{2\theta}
,
consistently with the desired (
5.17
) with
\mu=1
;
in this case there is a
\beta
-cut at
v_{0}
as in (3) in the statement;

•

d_{0}=4
, no
\beta
-cut is possible at
v_{0}
, and
d<4
, so that in particular
p_{1}=d
and (
5.22
) is upper bounded by
1
if
d=1
,
by
\epsilon^{\theta}
if
d=2
;
these case are in (3) of the statement, and the bounds are consistent with (
5.17
) with
\mu=1
;

•

d_{0}=5
, and either
d=4
and
(p_{1},p_{2})=(2,2)
, or
d=5
and
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=(2,2,1)

which corresponds to the scenario (2) in the statement;
in both cases (
5.22
) is upper bounded by
T_{1}^{-1/2}(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{2}=\epsilon^{2\theta_{0}}

which is consistent with (
5.17
) with
\mu=1
.

In all the cases that are not those considered above (so that are not (2) and (3) in the statement)
we want to show that one has (
5.17
) with the improvement
\mu=\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}
.
Indeed, the remaining cases are

(a)

d_{0}(v_{0})=5
and
d_{0}(v_{0})<4
;

(b)

d_{0}(v_{0})>5
;

(c)

d_{0}(v_{0})=4=d_{0}(v_{0})
and there is no
\beta
-cut at
v_{0}
.

In case (a), using (
5.18
) when
d=3
, and considering all the possible
cases
(p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})=(1,1,1)
,
(p_{1},p_{2})=(2,1)
, and
p_{1}=3
,
we have the bound

\displaystyle T_{1}^{-1/2}\cdot\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\lesssim\epsilon^{(3/2)\theta_{0}}\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}

in view of our choice of
T_{1}
.
When
d=2
and
p_{1}=2
or
(p_{1},p_{2})=(1,1)
, we get the same bound above, and when
d=1
an even better one.

In case (b), from the general bound (
5.18
)
we can see that the worst case scenario is when
d=d_{0}
and all
p_{j}=2
(thus
q=d_{0}/2
)
in which case we obtain a bound by

\displaystyle T_{1}^{-(d_{0}-4)/2}(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{d_{0}/2}=T_{1}^{2}\epsilon^{d_{0}}\lesssim\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1},

where the last inequality is again due to our choice of
T_{1}
and
d_{0}\geq 6
.

In the last case (c), since there is no
\beta
-cut, we only have one connected component (
p_{1}=4
)
and the bound from (
5.18
) reads

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\lesssim(\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{3}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}

which again suffices for (
5.17
) in view of our choice of
T_{1}
.
This concludes the proof.

∎

Now we can prove Proposition
5.23
.

Proof of Proposition
5.23
.

We consider several cases. We may assume
G=1
, otherwise the proof below will be easier
when
G=0
(and thus
V_{\beta}=0
).

Case 1: Cycles
. Suppose
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains only
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms, then it must be a cycle.
If it is a cycle of length
2
(i.e. a double bond), by Proposition
5.22
(1),
it must be LG, and thus must have two
\beta
-atoms.
By Proposition
3.11
we can control the number of choices of decorations for this double bond
by
RT_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}
.
Plugging this into (
5.10
), since
\chi=1
, gives the bound on the QC

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\leq R^{-1}\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot RT_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}=\epsilon^{\theta};

this is smaller than the right-hand side of (
5.16
) since
V_{\beta}=2,G=1,E=2
in this case.

Next, assume
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least 3
\beta
-atoms.
Bounding trivially the number of decorations for the cycle by
R
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\leq(R^{-1}\epsilon^{2}T_{1})^{\chi}\cdot R=\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\leq\epsilon^{\theta E/4}\cdot\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{2-V_{\beta}},

which is consistent with (
5.16
),
having used for the last inequality that
\beta\geq 3

and
