\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1}\geq\epsilon^{-1/3}

(using that
\lambda\leq\epsilon^{2-\theta}
).

Next, assume
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least 2
\beta
-atoms and at least one
\alpha
-atom with gap
\sim\lambda_{1}

By Proposition
3.11
we get

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\lesssim R^{-1}\epsilon^{2}T_{1}\cdot\min(R,RT_{1}^{-1}\lambda_{1}^{-1})=\epsilon^{2}\min(T_{1},\lambda_{1}^{-1}).

Then note that

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\min(T_{1},\lambda_{1}^{-1})\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}T_{1}^{1/2}\leq\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1}

by our choice of
T_{1}
,
and since
V_{\beta}\geq 2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\epsilon^{\theta E/4}\cdot\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{2-V_{\beta}},

having used again that each factor in the above product is larger than
\epsilon^{-1/3}
.

Finally, assume
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least one
\beta
-atom
and at least 2
\alpha
-atoms with gaps
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
.
Then, again by Proposition
3.11
and similar to the above, we first bound

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\epsilon^{2}\min(T_{1},\lambda_{1}^{-1},\lambda_{2}^{-1})

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2})^{-1/2}\epsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\lambda_{1}^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\lambda_{2}^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})

having used that
T_{1}=\epsilon^{-8/3+\theta_{0}}
for the last inequality.
Using that
V_{\beta}\geq 1
we see that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\epsilon^{\theta E/4}\cdot\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{2-V_{\beta}}.

This completes the proof of (
5.16
) in the case when
\mathbb{M}_{0}
is a cycle.

Remark 5.26
.

Note how the last two bounds above are tight; these are the cases of triangles with at least a
\beta
-atom
and SG of size
\approx T_{1}^{-1}
at the
\alpha
-atoms.
This is consistent with the ‘bad’ scenario presented in Section
8
, see Figure

5
.

Case 2: At least one
\varepsilon
-atom
.
Suppose
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains at least one
\varepsilon
-atom.
In this case, we perform the following “selection for removal” operations:
first select all the
\varepsilon
-atoms in a certain order to be specified shortly below.
Then, in the order of selection, each time remove one
\varepsilon
-atom, as in Proposition
5.25
;
if any
\alpha
- or
\beta
-atom becomes degree 1 then we also remove them
(for which we always have
\Delta\chi=0
, see Proposition
5.25
),
and then proceed to remove the next
\varepsilon
-atom in the order, and so on.
For the selection ordering we require that:

(1)

If
\mathbb{M}_{0}
contains one atom
v
that is I-atom of degree 4, then we order it
first
;
then we order all the
N-atoms
, and then all the
remaining I-atoms
.

(2)

Otherwise, we first order all the N-atoms, and then all the I-atoms.
If some I-atom has degree 5 and is connected to other
\varepsilon
-atoms
by
at least two
bonds or by two chains of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms, then we order it
last
.

(3)

If none of (a)–(b) hold, then we set the order arbitrarily.

We then want to apply Proposition
5.25
repeatedly at each step.
Letting
j=1,\dots,K
be the steps, and letting
\mathfrak{A}
denote the counting quantity for the
molecule
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, we have

(5.23)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\prod_{j=1}^{K}\epsilon^{(d_{j}/2)\,\mathbf{1}(\Delta\chi_{j}<0)}\,\mu_{j}

where
d_{j}
is the degree of the atom removed at step
j
, and we denote
\mu_{j}\in\{1,\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\}

the quantity
\mu
from the statement of Proposition
5.25
associated to the step
j
,
and
\Delta\chi_{j}
is the increment in
\chi
at step
j
.

Sub-case 2.1:
V_{\beta}\geq 1
or
V_{\beta}=2
and
\mu_{j}\neq 1
for some
j
.
Noticing that
\Delta\chi\geq-d
, we see that
\sum_{j}d_{j}\geq\chi_{0}
where
\chi_{0}

is the initial circuit rank of
\mathbb{M}_{0}
. Since each atom is at least degree
2
, we have

E\geq 2V
and thus
\chi_{0}\geq E/2
; then, from (
5.23
) we obtain

(5.24)

\mathfrak{A}\lesssim\epsilon^{E/4}\prod_{j=1}^{K}\mu_{j}.

Since
V_{\beta}\geq 1
(from Proposition
5.22
(1)),
we see that (
5.24
) is already a stronger bound than the right-hand side of (
5.16
)
if
at least one

\mu_{j}=\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}
in (
5.24
) (recall
G=1
).
We also see that (
5.24
) is already sufficient for (
5.16
) if
V_{\beta}\geq 2
.

Sub-case 2.2:
V_{\beta}=1
,
\mu_{j}=1
for all
j
.
Let us now look at the remaining case with
V_{\beta}=1
and
\mu_{j}=1
for all
j=1,\dots K
in (
5.24
).
By Proposition
5.25
, this implies the following properties for
\mathbb{M}_{0}
:

(1)

\mathbb{M}_{0}
has no I-atoms of degree
4
;

(2)

\mathbb{M}_{0}
has no I-atoms of degree
6
or more;

(3)

\mathbb{M}_{0}
has no N-atoms of degree
4
and the degree (which is
3
) of
N
-atoms stays constant
through all the steps;

(4)

No two degree
3
N-atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
can be connected by a bond or chain of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms;

(5)

Each degree
5
I-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
can be connected to other
\varepsilon
-atoms
by at most one bond or chain of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms.

Let us briefly verify these properties.
Property (i) follows from (3) in Proposition
5.25
since no
\beta
-cuts are allowed
(this would increase
V_{\beta}
by
2
),
and since an atom
v_{0}
with initial degree
d_{0}(v_{0})=4
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}

is ordered first to be removed according to Step (a)
of the selection process described at the beginning of this Case 2 (so that
d(v_{0})=4
).

Property (ii) follows directly from (2) in Proposition
5.25
.
while property (iii) follows from (1) there.

To verify property (iv) notice that if two
N
-atoms are connected by a bond then the removal of one of them
will decrease the degree of the other, contradicting (iii).
Similarly, if they are connected via a chain
of
\alpha
or
\beta
atoms, removing one of them will force the removal of the chain
according to the selection algorithm (since the
\alpha
or
\beta
atoms become degree 1)
and eventually the degree of the
N
-atom at the other end of the chain will decrease, again contradicting (iii).

For property (v), assume by contradiction that a degree
5
I-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
, denoted
I_{0}
,
is connected to another
\varepsilon
-atom, denoted
\varepsilon_{0}
,
by more than one bond or chain of
\alpha
or
\beta
atoms;
then, according to (b) of the selection algorithm both
I_{0}
and
\varepsilon_{0}
are degree
5
.
Removing one of them, say
I_{0}
, will force the degree of
\varepsilon_{0}
to decrease by at least two
which contradicts (2) of Proposition
5.25
.

We can now proceed to conclude the proof of the proposition.
Consider a degree 3 N-atom
v
in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
(if there is any).
If
\mu=1
for the step of removing this atom then,
according to the argument after (
5.20
) in the proof of Proposition
5.25
,
we must have
q=1
in this step.
By properties (iv) and (v) above,
this means that
v
is connected to 3 different degree 5 I-atoms by
3 different chains of
\alpha
or
\beta
atoms (or bonds).
In this case we know there exist at least 3 degree 5 I-atoms.
On the other hand, if there is no degree 3 N-atom, then there also exist at least 2 degree 5 I-atoms
(by our assumption that one
\varepsilon
atom exists, and properties (i) and (ii)).

Now consider each of the at least two degree 5 I-atom in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
.
By property (v) it must have at least two self-connecting bonds
or chains of
\alpha
- and
\beta
-atoms connecting to itself.
Since
V_{\beta}=1
, we can find one degree 5 I-atom such that these chains do not contain any
\beta
-atom.
There are then two possibilities:
(a)
v
has two self-connecting bonds, or
(b)
v
is connected to itself by a chain containing at least one
\alpha
-atom.

In case (a), this atom
v
must correspond to a branching node
\mathfrak{n}
in
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathrm{sb}}

with its 4 children nodes forming two pairs, but this implies that

k_{\mathfrak{n}}=\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}+\cdots+\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{4}}k_{\mathfrak{n}_{4}}=0
in the decoration,
and thus the contribution of this term vanishes because the corresponding
symbol
\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{n}_{4}}}
vanishes thanks to the property (
2.11
)
which gives that
\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{1}\cdots\zeta_{4}}(\eta_{1},\dots,\eta_{4})=0

for
\eta_{1}+\cdots+\eta_{4}=0
.

Finally, let us look at case (b), where the degree
5
I-atom
v
has a self-connecting chain with
at least one
\alpha
-atom.
Let the gap at this
\alpha
-atom be
\lambda
, and consider the step of removing
v
as in Proposition
5.25
.
According to (
5.22
), with the same notation there, for this step we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\cdot T_{1}^{-1/2}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{q}\sigma_{j}.

Recall also the notation for the sets of bonds
A_{j}
connecting
v
to the connected components
created after its removal, and that
p_{j}=|A_{j}|
.
Let
A_{1}
be the set of bonds connecting
v
to the chain containing the
\alpha
-atom;
in particular
p_{1}=2
, and
p_{2}+\dots+p_{q}=3
.
By first counting the two vectors associated with this
\alpha
-atom
and using Proposition
3.11
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}\leq\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}\cdot T_{1}^{-1/2}\cdot\min(1,T_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{-1})\cdot\prod_{j=2}^{q}\sigma_{j}.

According to (
5.22
) the product in the above right-hand side is either
1
(in the case of
p_{2}=p_{3}=p_{4}=1
)
or
\varepsilon^{2}T_{1}
(in the case
\{p_{2},p_{3}\}=\{2,1\}
,
or
(\varepsilon^{2}T_{1})^{2}T_{1}^{-1}\epsilon^{-\theta}
(in the case
p_{2}=3
).
Given our choice of
T_{1}
, the worst case is the second one, for which we get

\frac{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pre}}}{\mathfrak{A}_{\mathrm{pos}}}\leq T_{1}^{-1/2}\cdot\min(1,T_{1}^{-1}\lambda^{-1})\cdot\varepsilon^{2}T_{1}\leq\lambda^{-1/2}\varepsilon^{2}.

This is then sufficient to obtain (
5.16
), because
V_{\beta}=1
and

\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{2}\leq(\lambda^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})

again thanks to our choice of
T_{1}
. The proof of the proposition is completed.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5.24
.

The proof follows by induction on the number of
\beta
cuts:
the case of no
\beta
cut is trivial by summing the conditions (
5.2
) over all atoms in
\mathbb{M}_{0}
.
Suppose we perform one more
\beta

cut which forms two paired
\beta
-atoms
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
from one degree 4 I-atom
v

(cf. Definition
5.19
), then by adding the values of
\Gamma_{w}

for all atoms
w
in one of the newly created components, and applying the induction hypothesis,
we get that
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}
equals the desired form;
by the assumption
\Gamma_{v^{\prime}}+\Gamma_{v^{\prime\prime}}=(\mathrm{const})+O(T_{1}^{-1})
,
we get that
\Gamma_{v^{\prime\prime}}
also has the desired form.
∎

5.5.
Proof of Proposition
3.8

With all the above ingredients we now complete the proof of Proposition
5.10
,
and then that of Proposition
3.8
.

Proof of Proposition
5.10
.

By combining (
5.6
) in Proposition
5.12
,
(
5.9
), and the definition of the CQ in (
5.10
), we obtain first

\displaystyle R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}\cdot\mathfrak{A}\cdot(\log R)^{C}

where, recall,
q_{\mathrm{tot}}
is the sum of the lengths
q_{j}
of the irregular chains
\mathcal{H}_{j}

(cf. Definition
4.1
and Proposition
4.5
).
Applying then the result of the cutting algorithm (
5.12
) from Proposition
5.20
we get

(5.25)

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}\cdot(\log R)^{C}\cdot\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}\\
\cdot\prod_{(\alpha)}(\lambda^{1/2}\epsilon^{2-\theta}T_{1})\cdot(\epsilon^{-2+\theta}T_{1}^{-1})^{V_{\beta}-2\Delta F},\end{split}

where
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is the CQ associated to the reduced molecule
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}

described in Proposition
5.22
.
Finally, applying the counting estimate (
5.16
) to each connected component
\mathbb{M}_{0}
in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
,
noticing that the last two factors in (
5.25
) exactly cancel the last two factors in (
5.16
),
we arrive at

(5.26)

R^{-1}\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{I}(t,k)|\leq\epsilon^{-2}T_{1}^{-1}\cdot\epsilon^{2q_{\mathrm{tot}}/3}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta|\mathcal{Y}|}\cdot(\log R)^{C}\cdot\epsilon^{\theta E/4},

where, recall,
E
is the number of edges in
\mathbb{M}^{\prime}
and
|\mathcal{Y}|
the number of edges in the set of double bonds
\mathcal{Y}
.
Finally, to show that (
5.26
) implies (
5.5
)
is suffices to verify that the rank of the original couple
\mathcal{Q}
satisfies

(5.27)

\displaystyle r(\mathcal{Q})\leq 2E+4|\mathcal{Y}|+\frac{8}{3\theta}q_{\mathrm{tot}}.

Indeed, let
\mathbb{M}
be the original molecule,
and
\mathcal{Y}
the set of double bounds removed in Step 1
of the cutting algorithm in Subsection
5.3
.
Denoting with
\chi(\mathbb{M})
the circuit rank of a molecule
\mathbb{M}
, we have

\displaystyle r(\mathcal{Q})=2\chi(\mathbb{M})=2\big(\chi(\mathbb{M}_{\mathrm{sb}})+2q_{\mathrm{tot}}\big)=2\big(\chi(\mathbb{M}^{\prime})+2|\mathcal{Y}|+2q_{\mathrm{tot}}\big)\leq 2\big(E+2|\mathcal{Y}|+2q_{\mathrm{tot}}\big)

which is sufficient.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.8
.

Proof of (
3.38
).

First, using the formula (
3.34
) for
(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}(t)
and (
3.30
) for
(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t)
,
we note that
\mathbb{E}|(\mathcal{J}_{r})_{k}(t)|^{2}
can be written as a sum
\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k)
over all admissible couples
\mathcal{Q}

such that the rank of each tree forming
\mathcal{Q}
is equal to
r
.
Note that this set of couples
\mathcal{Q}
is invariant under the congruence relation
from Definition
4.2
,
so this sum can be decomposed into finitely many sub-sums of form (
5.4
)
in Proposition
5.10
.
The bound (
3.38
) then follows from (
5.5
) in Proposition
5.10
.

Proof of (
3.39
) and (
3.40
)
.
Note that the definition of
\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}]
from (
3.37
)
involves the (weighted) sum of couples
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k_{1})
over
\mathcal{Q}
and
k_{1}

and, again, can be reduced to summation over
\mathcal{Q}
in a congruence class.
Therefore (
3.39
) follows from the same proof we did above to obtain (
5.5
).

Finally, the contraction mapping property (
3.40
)
also follows from the same arguments above, so we just indicate some of the
steps needed to adapt the previous estimates to bound
\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}]-\mathcal{X}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}]
.
Notice that this last expression is a (weighted) sum over
k_{1}
of finitely many terms of the form

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathcal{Q}\equiv\mathcal{Q}_{0}}\Big(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma_{1}](t,k_{1})-\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t,k_{1})\Big)

where the sum is over all admissible couples
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
such that the rank of each tree forming
\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is equal to
r
,
and where
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma](t,k)
denotes the expression for couples
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,k)
from (
3.36
)
with the phases
\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}
defined by (
3.25
) and (
3.28
)
using the given ‘gauge’
\Gamma=\Gamma_{1}
or
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}
. From (
3.36
) we see that the only terms that involve the gauge factors
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}

are in the time integrals, so we only need to pay attention to the differences of those,
the rest of the formula being identical for
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma_{1}](t,k_{1})
and
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t,k_{1})
.
More precisely, let us denote by

(5.28)

\mathcal{I}[\Gamma_{1}]:=\int_{\mathcal{E}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}},

the expression in (
3.46
) with
\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}]
defined as in (
3.47
).
The difference of the oscillatory time-integrals that appear in

\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma](t,k_{1})-\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}[\Gamma^{\prime}](t,k_{1})
is given by

(5.29)

\mathcal{I}[\Gamma_{1}]-\mathcal{I}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}]=\int_{\mathcal{E}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot\big(e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}-e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\Big)\,\mathrm{d}t_{\mathfrak{m}}.

We then claim that a similar result as in Proposition
3.10
holds, namely

(5.30)

\big|\mathcal{I}[\Gamma_{1}]-\mathcal{I}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}]|\leq\mathcal{A}\big((\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0})_{\mathfrak{m}}\big)\|\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}_{t}L^{\infty}_{k}},

where
\mathcal{A}
is an explicit function depending on
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}
only, and satisfying (
3.45
).

To see (
5.30
) we first notice that an integration by parts in
(
5.29
), as done in (
3.48
), generates a bulk term with a factor

(5.31)

\partial_{t}\big(e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}-e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\big)=i\frac{d}{dt}\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})}-i\frac{d}{dt}\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{m}})};

this is clearly bounded by
\|\Psi-\Psi^{\prime}\|_{C^{1}_{t}L^{\infty}_{k}}\lesssim\|\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}
,
consistently with the desired contraction property.
Moreover, the boundary terms generated by the integration by parts are of the form (cfr. (
3.49
))

(5.32)

\frac{1}{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}}e^{i\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}}\cdot\big(e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{q}})}-e^{i\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{q}})}\big),

and can be carried out to the next integration in time as in the proof of Proposition
3.10
.
In particular, the factor (
3.49
) will
shift the phases in the next integration to
\alpha_{\mathfrak{m}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}+\alpha_{\mathfrak{q}}^{0}t_{\mathfrak{q}}
,
exactly as explained after (
3.49
).
The rest of the arguments in the proof of Proposition
3.10
carry out verbatim
(since they are insensitive to the choice of
\Gamma_{1}
, as long as
\|\Gamma_{1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{\infty}}\leq 1
),
and one always picks up a factor like (
5.31
) for some node
\mathfrak{m}
,
or a factor bounded by the difference

\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}](t_{\mathfrak{q}})-\Psi_{\mathfrak{m}}[\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}](t_{\mathfrak{q}})
for some node
\mathfrak{m}
belonging to a sub-tree rooted at
\mathfrak{q}
.

The bound (
5.30
) above, with (
3.45
),
can then be used in Proposition
5.12
, to obtain a bound as in (
5.6
)
where the factor
(\log R)^{C}
is replaced by
(\log R)^{C}\|\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}_{t}L^{\infty}_{k}}
.
The proof can then proceed as in the rest of
the section, and we obtain (
5.5
) with an extra factor of
\|\Gamma_{1}-\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}\|_{C^{0}_{t}L^{\infty}_{k}}

on the right-hand side; this is the desired bound (
3.40
).
The proof of Proposition
3.8
is complete.
∎

6.
Treatment of the remainder

In this section we prove Proposition
3.9
.
To start, we first prove that
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)
is an approximate solution to the equation
(
3.24
) for the modified profile
(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}
.

Proposition 6.1
.

We have

(6.1)

\displaystyle(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}({t})

\displaystyle=(\epsilon R^{1/2})^{-1}\widehat{w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)+i\sum_{r=3}^{A}(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r-1}T_{1}\cdot\sum_{\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}k_{r}=k}\int_{0}^{t}e^{iT_{1}\cdot\Omega_{r}({t}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\times i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{1}\cdots\zeta_{r}}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r})\cdot\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\prod_{j=1}^{r}(a_{k_{j}})_{\mathrm{app}}({t}^{\prime})^{\zeta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle+iT_{1}\int_{0}^{t}(\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k)+T_{1}^{-1}\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k))(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}({t}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}+\mathcal{R}_{k}(t),

where, with the exception of a set of probability
\lesssim e^{-2R^{\eta_{0}}}
,
the remainder term
\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)
satisfies that

(6.2)

\|\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{k}^{1}}\leq\epsilon^{N\theta/10}.

Proof.

Recall that

(6.3)

(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over all admissible paired trees or rank
r(\mathcal{T})\leq N
,
and
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
is defined as in (
3.30
), see (
3.32
).

Consider the right hand side of (
6.1
) with the exclusion of the remainder
\mathcal{R}_{k}
;
by definition of initial data (
3.2
) and (
2.14
),
we see that the first term, which corresponds to
w_{\mathrm{in}}^{\mathrm{tr}}
, is equal to

(6.4)

I(k,t)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(I)}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over all admissible paired trees

\mathcal{T}
of sign
+

that do not have I-branching node
.

Then consider the second term on the right hand side of (
6.1
),
which corresponds to the multilinear expressions of
a_{\mathrm{app}}
.
By the definition of the trees
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
in the ansatz (
3.32
) for
a_{\mathrm{app}}
, this second term is equal to

(6.5)

II(k,t)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(II)}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over all paired trees
\mathcal{T}
such that:

(1)

The root
\mathfrak{r}
of
\mathcal{T}
is an I-branching node of sign
+
, and
\mathcal{T}
is formed by subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq r)
where
3\leq r\leq A
;

(2)

Each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is an admissible paired tree of rank
\leq N
.

Finally, consider the third term, which corresponds to the gauge. By definition of
\Gamma_{0}
(
3.23
) and
\Gamma_{1}
(
3.7
), we see that the third term is equal to

(6.6)

III(k,t)=-\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(III)}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over all paired trees
\mathcal{T}
such that:

(3)

The root of
\mathcal{T}
is an I-branching node of sign
+
with 3 children,
and
\mathcal{T}
is formed by by subtrees
\mathcal{T}_{j}\,(1\leq j\leq 3)
of signs
(+,+,-)
;

(4)

Each
\mathcal{T}_{j}
is an admissible paired tree of rank
\leq N
,
and the leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{3}
are completely paired with the leaves of
\mathcal{T}_{i}
for some
i\in\{1,2\}
.

Now we put
I\sim III
together and compare with the left hand side

(6.7)

(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(IV)}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t)

where the summation is taken over all admissible paired trees with rank
\leq N
. Then we know that:

•

If
\mathcal{T}
occurs in (
6.7
) then it either has no I-branching node
(and thus occurs in
I
), or its root
\mathfrak{r}
is an I-branching node and it occurs in
II
;

•

If
\mathcal{T}
occurs in
II
, then
\mathcal{T}
is
not admissible

(cf. Definition
3.3
) if and only if
\mathcal{T}
occurs in the summation defining in
III
since, by definition,
this forces
\mathfrak{r}
to be an I-branching node with 3 children and two
of its children have their subtree leaves completely paired;
note that the gauge factor
\Gamma_{0}
, respectively
\Gamma_{1}
, in
III
exactly correspond
to the cases when the (completely paired) trees
\mathcal{T}_{3}
and
\mathcal{T}_{i}

are both trivial or not.

Therefore, we conclude that

(6.8)

\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)=(a_{\mathrm{app}})_{k}(t)-\big[I(k,t)+II(k,t)+III(k,t)\big]=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}(\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over a
subset

\mathcal{A}
of paired trees
\mathcal{T}
,
such that each
\mathcal{T}\in\mathcal{A}

has rank
>N
,
and
the subtree rooted at any child node of the root of
\mathcal{T}
is admissible with rank
\leq N
.
Moreover, this set
\mathcal{A}
is invariant under any twist
(Definition
4.2
, which can be given also for irregular chains inside a paired tree),
as long as we
shorten the irregular chain such that it does not contain the root
\mathfrak{r}
.

Now, by (
6.8
), we have that

(6.9)

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)|^{2}\bigg)=\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}})_{k}(t),

where the summation is taken over all couples
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}^{+},\mathcal{T}^{-})
with
\mathcal{T}^{+}\in\mathcal{A}
,
and
\mathcal{T}^{-}
formed from a paired tree in
\mathcal{A}
by flipping the signs of all nodes.

Note that for any couple
\mathcal{Q}
, by the ‘admissibility’ property
of the subtrees rooted at each child of each root,
we know that it cannot have two children nodes of an I-branching node with their subtree
leaves completely paired,
unless this I-branching node is a root
.
This leads to
at most two
exceptional non-admissible instances,
apart from which the couple
\mathcal{Q}
is admissible.
We can then apply the same proof in Subsection
3.7
and Sections
4
and
5

with some small adjustments which we now describe.
In particular, we can write the expressions
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
on right hand side of (
6.9
)
as a sum of expressions of the form (
5.4
), with (i) the exception of the two above non-admissible instances, which lead to at most an
O(R^{2})
loss,
and (ii) there exist at most two irregular chains containing a root, which need to be
“cut short” by excluding the root, which leads to at most another
O(R^{2})
loss.
According to this, and the bound (
5.5
), we have

(6.10)

\mathbb{E}\bigg(\sum_{k}\langle k\rangle^{10}|\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)|^{2}\bigg)\leq R^{5}\epsilon^{\theta N/8}\leq\epsilon^{\theta N/9},

where we note that the rank
r=r(\mathcal{Q})\geq N
.

Finally, notice that
\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)
is a multilinear Gaussian random variable,
so that we can get large deviation estimates for
\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)
for fixed
k
and
t
from Proposition
2.2
;
this then leads to the large deviation estimates for the norm

\|\mathcal{R}_{k}(t)\|_{L_{t}^{\infty}L_{k}^{1}}
, yielding (
6.2
) with probability
\geq 1-Ce^{-2R^{\eta_{0}}}
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Next, we analyze the linearized operator associated with the approximate equation (
6.1
).

Proposition 6.2
.

Define the
\mathbb{R}
-linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{0}
to be the linearization of the nonlinearity in (
3.24
), i.e.

(6.11)

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}_{0}u)_{k}(t)=i\sum_{r=3}^{A}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r-1}T_{1}\cdot\sum_{\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}k_{r}=k}\int_{0}^{t}e^{iT_{1}\cdot\Omega_{r}({t}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\,\times i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{1}\cdots\zeta_{r}}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r})\cdot\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\prod_{j=1}^{r}\widetilde{a}_{k_{j}}({t}^{\prime})^{\zeta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\,iT_{1}\int_{0}^{t}(\epsilon^{2}\Gamma_{0}(k)+T_{1}^{-1}\Gamma_{1}({t}^{\prime},k))u_{k}({t}^{\prime})\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime},

where the functions
\widetilde{a}
in (
6.11
) are such that exactly one
\widetilde{a}

(i.e. the one corresponding to
j=i
) equals
u
and all the others equal
a_{\mathrm{app}}
.
Then, with probability
\geq 1-Ce^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
, the operator
1-\mathcal{L}_{0}
is invertible, and satisfies

(6.12)

\|(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})^{-1}\|_{C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}\to C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}}\lesssim R^{8}.

Moreover, the norm
C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}
can be replaced by any Lebesgue or Sobolev norm with fixed exponents,
such as the
L_{t}^{\infty}\dot{W}_{x}^{4,0}
norm appearing in Proposition
3.1
.

Proof.

Fix the norm
Z:=C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}
. We will construct a (random) parametrix
\mathcal{X}
to
1-\mathcal{L}_{0}
, such that

(6.13)

\|\mathcal{X}\|_{Z\to Z}\lesssim R^{8},\qquad\|\mathcal{X}(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})-1\|_{Z\to Z}\leq\epsilon,\qquad\|(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})\mathcal{X}-1\|_{Z\to Z}\leq\epsilon

with probability
\geq 1-e^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
. It then follows from (
6.13
)
and Von Neumann series that
1-\mathcal{L}_{0}
is invertible, and

\|(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})^{-1}\|_{Z\to Z}\leq\|[\mathcal{X}(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})]^{-1}\|_{Z\to Z}\cdot\|\mathcal{X}\|_{Z\to Z}\lesssim R^{8}.

To prove (
6.13
), we need to introduce the notions of
paired flower trees

as follows. A paired flower tree, still denoted by
\mathcal{T}
,
is a paired tree in the sense of Definition
3.2
but with one specific unpaired leaf

\mathfrak{f}
fixed, called the
flower
.
The unique path from the root to the flower is called the
stem
.
We require that all the branching nodes in the stem are I-branching nodes,
and define the number of such branching nodes to be the
depth
of the (paired) flower tree.
The paired flower couple is defined in the same way and is formed by two paired flower trees,
where we require that the two flowers have opposite signs and are paired.

For any paired flower tree
\mathcal{T}
, we define the following modification of the
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
in (
3.30
): the modification
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
is an operator defined by

(6.14)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(u))_{k}({t})

\displaystyle:=(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{T})-1}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{T})}\cdot\sum_{(k_{\mathfrak{n}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{P}}^{(+)}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{L}\backslash\mathcal{P}}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{h}})^{1/2}\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{T}}\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}[i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\cdots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})]^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}}\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\cdots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{D}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}u_{k_{\mathfrak{f}}}(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{+}})^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{f}}}\,\mathrm{d}{t}_{\mathfrak{m}}\cdot\Re\bigg(\prod_{\mathfrak{h}\in\mathcal{L}\backslash(\mathcal{P}\cup\{\mathfrak{f}\})}g_{k_{\mathfrak{h}}}^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{h}}}\bigg),

where
\mathfrak{f}
is the flower and
\mathfrak{f}^{+}
is the parent node of
\mathfrak{f}
(which is on the stem).

Note that if the paired flower tree
\mathcal{T}
has depth
1
, i.e. the parent of the flower is the root,
then the operator
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}
exactly corresponds to
the factor that is summed in (
6.11
) for fixed indexes
r
and
i
with
a_{\mathrm{app}}
replaced by the standard
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}
terms for various paired trees
\mathcal{T}
,
and the function
u
in the
i-th
position.
Moreover, the last gauging term in (
6.11
) exactly corresponds to the case when
the root
\mathfrak{r}
is an I-branching node with 3 children, and the two trees at the non-flower children
nodes of
\mathfrak{r}
have their leaves exactly paired (again the
\Gamma_{0}
term corresponds to
the case when these two trees are both trivial,
and the
\Gamma_{1}
term corresponds to the case when these two trees are not both trivial).
Therefore we have

(6.15)

\mathcal{L}_{0}(u)=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(I)}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(u),

where the summation is taken over all admissible paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}

of sign
+
with depth
1
, such that the subtree at each child node of the root has rank
\leq N
.

We define the parametrix
\mathcal{X}
by

(6.16)

\mathcal{X}(u):=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(II)}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(u),

where the summation is taken over all admissible paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}
of sign
+

with arbitrary depth (including
0
, in which case
\mathcal{T}
is trivial and
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}=1
)
and total rank
\leq N
.

To calculate
\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}\mathcal{L}_{0}
we use the correspondence between paired flower trees
and their expressions
\widetilde{\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Note that we can multiply two paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
by taking
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and replacing
the flower by the paired tree
\mathcal{T}_{2}
rooted at it
(assuming the flower of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
has the same sign as the root of
\mathcal{T}_{2}
;
if not we should attach this
\mathcal{T}_{2}
after flipping the signs of all nodes),
so the new flower is just the flower of
\mathcal{T}_{2}
.
Then we can turn this new tree
\mathcal{T}
into a paired tree by
assigning arbitrary pairings, while respecting the existing pairings in
\mathcal{T}_{j}
.
We denote by
[\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{2}]
the set of all paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}
obtained in this way.
By these definitions we can see that

(6.17)

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}_{1}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}_{2}}=\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in[\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{T}_{2}]}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}.

Then, by (
6.17
), we can calculate the products
\mathcal{X}\mathcal{L}_{0}
and
\mathcal{L}_{0}\mathcal{X}
, to get that

(6.18)

\mathcal{X}(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})-1=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(III)}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}},

where the sum is taken over all admissible paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}
of
sign
+
such that (i) it has rank
>N
,
(ii) the subtrees rooted at the siblings of the flower all have rank
\leq N
,
and (iii) let
\mathfrak{f}^{+}
be the parent of the flower
\mathfrak{f}
, then the rank of
\mathcal{T}
becomes
\leq N

if we collapse the subtree rooted at
\mathfrak{f}^{+}
to a single node
\mathfrak{f}^{+}
. Similarly

(6.19)

(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})\mathcal{X}-1=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(IV)}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}},

where the sum is taken over all admissible paired flower trees
\mathcal{T}
of sign
+
such that
(i) it has rank
>N
,
(ii) the subtrees rooted at the children nodes of the root all have rank
\leq N
.

Now consider either (
6.18
) or (
6.19
) and denote the operator on the right hand side by
\mathcal{I}
;
then it can be divided into two parts
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{I}^{+}+\mathcal{I}^{-}
, where
\mathcal{I}^{\zeta}
restricts the summation to

\mathcal{T}
whose flower has sign
\zeta
.
Each such operator has a kernel as follows:

(6.20)

(\mathcal{I}^{\pm}u)_{k}(t)=\sum_{k^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})u_{k^{\prime}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}):=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}),

where, cfr. (
6.14
),

\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}):=\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(\delta(s-t_{\mathfrak{f}^{+}})\mathbf{1}(k_{f}=k^{\prime}))\big)_{k}(t).

Then we have that

(6.21)

\mathbb{E}|\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\bigg|\sum_{\mathcal{T}}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})\bigg|^{2}=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}),

where we define, for any paired flower couple
\mathcal{Q}
, a modification of the
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
in (
3.36
)
given by

(6.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})

\displaystyle:=(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r(\mathcal{Q})-2}(T_{1})^{n_{I}(\mathcal{Q})}\cdot\sum_{(k_{\mathfrak{n}})}\prod_{\mathfrak{l}\in\mathcal{L}\backslash\{\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\}}^{(+)}\psi(k_{\mathfrak{l}})\prod_{\mathfrak{n}\in\mathcal{Q}}\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k_{\mathfrak{n}})

\displaystyle\times\mathbf{1}_{k_{\mathfrak{f}}=k_{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}=k^{\prime}}\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}[i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\cdots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})]^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}\cdot\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{N}}\widetilde{a}_{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}}\cdots\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}}}(k_{\mathfrak{m}_{1}},\cdots,k_{\mathfrak{m}_{r}})^{\zeta_{\mathfrak{m}}}

\displaystyle\times\int_{\mathcal{E}}\delta(t_{\mathfrak{f}^{+}}-s)\delta(t_{(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})^{+}}-s)\prod_{\mathfrak{m}\in\mathcal{I}}e^{iT_{1}\Omega_{\mathfrak{m}}(t_{\mathfrak{m}})}\,\mathrm{d}{t}_{\mathfrak{m}},

where
\mathfrak{f}
and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
are the flowers and
\mathfrak{f}^{+}
and
(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})^{+}
are the parents of the flowers,
and in (
6.21
) the summation is taken over all flower couples
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})

such that each of
\mathcal{T}_{1}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2}
satisfy
the requirements in the definitions of
(III)
or
(IV)
.

Now it suffices to estimate the right-hand side of (
6.21
).
We can apply the same arguments as in Subsection
3.7
, and Sections
4

and
5
used to estimate
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
,
with minor adjustments adapted to
\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Note that the set of
\mathcal{Q}=(\mathcal{T}_{1},\mathcal{T}_{2})
in (
6.21
) is invariant under twisting, provided that the irregular
chain involved in the twisting does not contain any child node of the root or the parent node of the flower.
These exceptions lead at most to an
O(R^{4})
loss, and the rest of the proof is the same as for the case of
\mathcal{K}_{\mathcal{Q}}
.
Since the rank
r(\mathcal{Q})>N
, we then get that

\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})\lesssim R^{4}\epsilon^{\theta N/8}.

By the large deviation estimate for
Gaussians, see Proposition
2.2
, in the same way as before we get

\sup_{t,s,k^{\prime}k^{\prime}}|\mathcal{I}^{\pm}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})|\lesssim R^{4}\epsilon^{\theta N/9}

with probability
\geq 1-Ce^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
.
Since
|k|,|k^{\prime}|\leq 2^{K_{\mathrm{tr}}}\leq R^{1/A}
and
0\leq t,s\leq 1
,
the above inequality and (
6.20
) imply the second and third bounds in (
6.13
).

To prove the first bound in (
6.13
), we write, similarly to the above

(6.23)

(\mathcal{X}u)_{k}(t)=\sum_{k^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{X}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})u_{k^{\prime}}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad\mathcal{X}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}):=\sum_{\mathcal{T}}^{(II)}\widetilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\mathcal{T}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime}),

where the sum is as in (
6.16
). We can then estimate

\mathbb{E}|(\mathcal{X}u)_{k}(t)|^{2}=\sum_{\mathcal{Q}}\widetilde{\mathcal{K}}_{\mathcal{Q}}(t,s,k,k^{\prime})\lesssim R^{4},

which leads to the bound for
\mathcal{X}
in (
6.13
) with probability
1-e^{-R^{\eta}}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

6.1.
Proof of Proposition
3.9

Using the above results we can now prove Proposition
3.9
.
Define
b_{k}(t):=(a_{k})_{\mathrm{tr}}(t)-(a_{k})_{\mathrm{app}}(t)
.
Then by (
3.24
) and (
6.1
) we get that

(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})b=\mathcal{R}+O(|b|^{2}),

where
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is defined as in (
6.11
),

\mathcal{R}
is defined as in (
6.1
), and
O(|b|^{2})
is a term that is at least quadratic in
b
.
By Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
and a fixed point argument,
we know that, with probability
\geq 1-Ce^{-R^{2\eta_{0}}}
, this equation for
b

has a unique solution in the space
\|b\|_{Z}\leq\epsilon^{N\theta/12}
;
the same holds if one replaces the
Z=C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}
norm by a norm with a
\langle k\rangle^{10}
weight.

6.2.
Proof of (
3.6
)

Suppose
W
solves the equation (
3.5
), and apply to it the transform (
3.18
)
(which transforms
w_{\mathrm{tr}}
to
a_{\mathrm{tr}}
) to get

\displaystyle a_{k}(t)=(R^{1/2}\epsilon)^{-1}e^{iT_{1}(|k|^{1/2}t+\Gamma(t,k)}\widehat{W}(T_{1}t,k)=:\mathcal{P}(W)_{k}.

This profile satisfies the equation

\displaystyle a_{k}(t)=(R^{1/2}\epsilon)^{-1}\widehat{W}(0)+\mathcal{L}(a)_{k}(t)+\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{k}(s)ds,\qquad\mathcal{R}^{\prime}_{k}(s):=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{R})_{k}

for
t\leq 1
, where here
\mathcal{L}
is defined in the same way as
\mathcal{L}_{0}
in (
6.11
), but with
a_{\mathrm{app}}

replaced by
a_{\mathrm{tr}}
.
We can then estimate, for
Z=C^{0}_{t}L^{1}_{k}
,

\displaystyle\|(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})a\|_{Z}\leq R^{-1/2}\epsilon^{-1}\|\widehat{W}(0)\|_{L^{1}_{k}}+\big\|(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}_{0})(a)\big\|_{Z}+\big\|\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\big\|_{Z}.

Using the estimate (
6.12
), the above equation implies

(6.24)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{W}\|_{Z}\lesssim R^{8}\big(\|\widehat{W}(0)\|_{L^{1}_{k}}+\|\mathcal{R}\|_{Z}\big)

provided we can bound

(6.25)

\displaystyle\big\|(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}_{0})u\big\|_{Z}\leq R^{-9}\|u\|_{Z}.

From the definitions of
\mathcal{L}_{0}
in (
6.11
) and
\mathcal{L}
above, we see that

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}-\mathcal{L}_{0})u

\displaystyle=i\sum_{r=3}^{A}\sum_{i=1}^{r}(\epsilon R^{-1/2})^{r-1}T_{1}\cdot\sum_{\zeta_{j}\in\{\pm\}}\sum_{\zeta_{1}k_{1}+\cdots+\zeta_{r}k_{r}=k}\int_{0}^{t}e^{iT_{1}\cdot\Omega_{r}({t}^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\times[i\widetilde{n}_{\zeta_{1}\cdots\zeta_{r}}(k_{1},\cdots,k_{r})]^{\zeta_{n}}\cdot\varphi_{\leq K_{\mathrm{tr}}}(k)\prod_{j=1}^{r}\widetilde{a}_{k_{j}}({t}^{\prime})^{\zeta_{j}}\,\mathrm{d}{t}^{\prime}

where, in the last product, exactly one of the
\widetilde{a}
is
u
, at least one of the
\widetilde{a}

is
b=a_{\mathrm{tr}}-a_{\mathrm{app}}
, and the others are
a_{\mathrm{app}}
.
Since
\|b\|_{Z}\lesssim\epsilon^{N\theta/12}
is small,
using also (
3.35
) and (
3.38
) to bound
a_{\mathrm{app}}
, via Proposition
2.2
,
we obtain (
6.25
) with probability
\geq 1-Ce^{-2R^{\eta_{0}}}.

Finally, the desired (
3.6
) follows in the same way as (
6.24
)
using that the bound (
6.12
) for
(1-\mathcal{L}_{0})^{-1}

also holds if we measure in the
L_{t}^{\infty}\dot{W}_{x}^{4,0}
norm of
W(t,x)

instead of the
C_{t}^{0}L_{k}^{1}
norm of the corresponding profile
a_{k}(t)=\mathcal{P}(W)
,
as stated in Proposition
6.2
, and that the same is true for the bound (
6.25
).

7.
Normal forms and proof of Proposition
2.1

We assume in this section that
A\geq 3
is a fixed integer and
(h,\phi)\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{2}}\times\dot{H}^{N_{2}+1/2,1/2})
is a solution of the system

(7.1)

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}h=G(h)\phi,\\
&\partial_{t}\phi=-h-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\frac{(G(h)\phi+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}}{2(1+(\partial_{x}h)^{2})},\end{split}

satisfying the bounds (
2.6
), i.e.

(7.2)

\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t)\|_{H^{N_{2}}}\leq B_{2},\qquad\|(h+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi)(t)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty},0}}\leq\epsilon_{\infty},

for some parameters
N_{2}\geq 2A
,
N_{\infty}\in[4,N_{2}-4]
,
B_{2}\geq 1
,
\epsilon_{\infty}\ll 1
, and for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
.

We recall a simple lemma from
[
22
]
, see Lemmas 2.1 and 2.2:

Lemma 7.1
.

(i) For any
m\in S^{\infty}
we define the associated multiplinear operator
M
by

(7.3)

\mathcal{F}\big[M_{m}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})\big](\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d-1}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{d}=\xi}m(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d})\widehat{f}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{f_{d}}(\eta_{d}).

Then

(7.4)

\begin{split}&M_{m}(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})(x)=\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}^{d}}K_{m}(x-y_{1},\ldots,x-y_{d})f(y_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot f(y_{d})\,dy_{1}\ldots dy_{d},\\
&K_{m}(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d}):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/R}e^{i(z_{1}\eta_{1}+\ldots+z_{d}\eta_{d})}m(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{d}).\end{split}

In particular
\|K_{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{R}^{d})}={\|m\|}_{S^{\infty}}
. Therefore, if
p_{1},\ldots,p_{d},r\in[1,\infty]
satisfy
1/p_{1}+\ldots+1/p_{d}=1/r
and
f_{1},\ldots,f_{d}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{T}_{R})
then

(7.5)

\|M(f_{1},\ldots,f_{d})\|_{L^{r}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\leq\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|f_{1}\|_{L^{p_{1}}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\cdot\ldots\cdot\|f_{d}\|_{L^{p_{d}}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}.

(ii) If
m,m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}
then
m\cdot m^{\prime}\in S^{\infty}
and

(7.6)

\|m\cdot m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}\leq\|m\|_{S^{\infty}}\|m^{\prime}\|_{S^{\infty}}.

(iii) Assume that
d\geq 1
,
m:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}
is a continuous compactly supported function, and
\check{m}\in L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Euclidean inverse Fourier transform of
m
. Then

(7.7)

\Big\|\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{d}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d}\in\mathbb{Z}/R}m(\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{d})e^{i(x_{1}\xi_{1}+\ldots+x_{d}\xi_{d})}\Big\|_{L^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{T}_{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|\check{m}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The first main issue is to understand the expansion of the Dirichlet-Neumann operator
G(h)
. We prove the following:

Lemma 7.2
.

(i) We have
G(h)\phi\in C([T_{1},T_{2}]:H^{N_{2}-3/2})
and, for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
,

(7.8)

\begin{split}&G(h)\phi=|\partial_{x}|\phi+\sum_{n=2}^{A}G_{n}(h,\phi)+G_{>A},\\
&G_{2}(h,\phi)=-\partial_{x}(h\partial_{x}\phi)-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi),\\
&\|G_{>A}\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{2}-3/2,-3/4}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A}B_{2},\qquad\|G_{>A}\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-3/2,-3/4}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A+1},\\
&\|G_{n}(h,\phi)\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{2}-3/2,-3/4}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{n-1}B_{2},\qquad\|G_{n}(h,\phi)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-3/2,-3/4}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{n},\qquad n\in\{2,\ldots,A\}.\end{split}

In addition, the functions
G_{n}(h,\phi)
,
n\in\{2,\ldots,A\}
are defined by multipliers
q_{n}
satisfying

(7.9)

\begin{split}&\widehat{G_{n}(h,\phi)}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{n}=\xi}q_{n}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})\widehat{\phi}(\eta_{1})\widehat{h}(\eta_{2})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{h}(\eta_{n}),\\
&q_{n}(\lambda\underline{\eta})=\lambda^{n}q_{n}(\underline{\eta}),\qquad\text{ for any }\,\,\lambda\in(0,\infty),\,\,\underline{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&\big\|q_{n}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})\varphi_{k}(\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{n})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi_{k_{n}}(\eta_{n})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k+\widetilde{k}_{1}+\ldots+\widetilde{k}_{n-1}}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n}-\widetilde{k}_{1}|)^{A}.\end{split}

The
S^{\infty}
inequality in the last line of (
7.9
) holds for any
k,k_{1},\ldots k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
\widetilde{k}_{1}\leq\widetilde{k}_{2}\leq\ldots\leq\widetilde{k}_{n}
denotes the nondecreasing rearrangement of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n}
.

(ii) As a consequence, the functions
h,\phi
satisfy the equations

(7.10)

\partial_{t}h=|\partial_{x}|\phi+\sum_{n=2}^{A}G_{n}(h,\phi)+G_{>A},\qquad\partial_{t}\phi=-h+\sum_{n=2}^{A}H_{n}(h,\phi)+H_{>A},

where
G_{n},G_{>A}
are as in (
7.8
)–(
7.9
) and the functions
H_{n}
and
H_{>A}
satisfy

(7.11)

\begin{split}&H_{2}(h,\phi)=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(|\partial_{x}|\phi)^{2},\\
&\|H_{>A}\|_{H^{N_{2}-3/2}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A}B_{2},\qquad\|H_{>A}\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-3/2,1/10}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A+1},\\
&\|H_{n}(h,\phi)\|_{H^{N_{2}-3/2}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{n-1}B_{2},\qquad\|H_{n}(h,\phi)\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-3/2,1/10}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{n},\qquad n\in\{2,\ldots,A\},\end{split}

for any
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
. In addition, the functions
H_{n}(h,\phi)
,
n\in\{3,\ldots,A\}
, are defined by multipliers
r_{n}
satisfying

(7.12)

\begin{split}&\widehat{H_{n}(h,\phi)}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\ldots+\eta_{n}=\xi}r_{n}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})\widehat{\phi}(\eta_{1})\widehat{\phi}(\eta_{2})\widehat{h}(\eta_{3})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{h}(\eta_{n}),\\
&r_{n}(\lambda\underline{\eta})=\lambda^{n}q_{n}(\underline{\eta}),\qquad\text{ for any }\,\,\lambda\in(0,\infty),\,\,\underline{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\\
&\big\|r_{n}(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi_{k_{n}}(\eta_{n})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n}}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n}-\widetilde{k}_{1}|)^{A}.\end{split}

Proof.

(i) We fix
t\in[T_{1},T_{2}]
and use some of the results in
[
22
, Section 8]
. Loss of derivative is not important here, so, for the sake of simplicity, we do not attempt to optimize this aspect.

Step 1.
We start from the formulas (8.18) and (8.14) in
[
22
]
,

(7.13)

G(h)\phi=-\partial_{x}\psi,\qquad(I+T_{1})\psi=(H_{0}+T_{2})\phi,

where the operators
H_{0}
(the Hilbert transform),
T_{1}
,
T_{2}
are defined by the formulas

(7.14)

(H_{0}f)(\alpha):=\frac{-1}{\pi}\mathrm{p.v.}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{f^{\#}(\beta)}{\alpha-\beta}\,d\beta,

(7.15)

\begin{split}&(T_{1}f)(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h^{\#}(\alpha)-h^{\#}(\beta)-\partial_{\beta}h^{\#}(\beta)(\alpha-\beta)}{|\gamma(\alpha)-\gamma(\beta)|^{2}}f^{\#}(\beta)\,d\beta,\\
&(T_{2}f)(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h^{\#}(\alpha)-h^{\#}(\beta)-\partial_{\beta}h^{\#}(\beta)(\alpha-\beta)}{|\gamma(\alpha)-\gamma(\beta)|^{2}}\frac{h^{\#}(\alpha)-h^{\#}(\beta)}{\alpha-\beta}f^{\#}(\beta)\,d\beta.\end{split}

Here
h^{\#}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f^{\#}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}
denote the periodic extensions of the functions
h
and
f
, and
\gamma(x):=x+ih^{\#}(x)
. The operators
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
admit expansions

(7.16)

T_{1}=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}R_{2n},\qquad T_{2}=\sum_{n\geq 0}(-1)^{n}R_{2n+1}.

where the homogeneous real operators
R_{n}
are given by

(7.17)

(R_{n}f)(\alpha):=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{h^{\#}(\alpha)-h^{\#}(\beta)-\partial_{\beta}h^{\#}(\beta)(\alpha-\beta)}{(\alpha-\beta)^{2}}\Big(\frac{h^{\#}(\alpha)-h^{\#}(\beta)}{\alpha-\beta}\Big)^{n}f^{\#}(\beta)\,d\beta.

The operators
R_{n}
can be defined in terms of Fourier multipliers, in the form

(7.18)

\widehat{R_{n}f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n+1}}\sum_{\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n+1}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{0}+\ldots+\eta_{n+1}=\xi}M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\underline{\eta})\widehat{f}(\eta_{0})\widehat{h}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{h}(\eta_{n+1}),

where
\underline{\eta}:=(\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})
and

(7.19)

M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\underline{\eta}):=\frac{-i\eta_{0}}{\pi(n+1)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i\eta_{0}\rho}\prod_{l=1}^{n+1}\frac{1-e^{-i\eta_{l}\rho}}{\rho}\,d\rho.

See the identities (8.41)-(8.42) in
[
22
]
.

The formula (
7.19
) shows that the symbols
M_{n+1}
are homogeneous of degree
n+1
,

(7.20)

M_{n+1}(\lambda\eta_{0},\lambda\underline{\eta})=\lambda^{n+1}M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\underline{\eta}),\qquad\lambda\in(0,\infty),\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{R},\underline{\eta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}.

Moreover, when
n=0
we calculate explicitly

(7.21)

\begin{split}&M_{1}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1})=\eta_{0}[\mathrm{sgn}\,(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1})-\mathrm{sgn}\,(\eta_{0})],\\
&\widehat{R_{0}f}(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}/R}\big[\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)-\mathrm{sgn}(\eta_{0})\big]\eta_{0}\widehat{f}(\eta_{0})\widehat{h}(\xi-\eta_{0}).\end{split}

Notice also that

(7.22)

\begin{split}&M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta_{0}\cdot\ldots\cdot\eta_{n+1}=0,\\
&M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})=0\qquad\text{ if }|\eta_{0}|>|\eta_{1}|+\ldots+|\eta_{n+1}|.\end{split}

Indeed, the identity in the first line follows directly from the definition (
7.19
), while the identity in the second line follows by induction over
n
once we observe that

\partial_{\eta_{1}}M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})=(-i)\frac{n}{n+1}M_{n}(\eta_{0}+\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\ldots,\eta_{n+1}).

We show now that if
n\geq 1
and
k_{0},\ldots,k_{n+1}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
then

(7.23)

\big\|M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})\cdot\varphi_{k_{0}}(\eta_{0})\ldots\varphi_{k_{n+1}}(\eta_{n+1})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\leq C_{1}^{n}2^{k_{0}}2^{\widetilde{k}_{1}+\ldots+\widetilde{k}_{n}}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n+1}-\widetilde{k}_{n}|),

where
\widetilde{k}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\widetilde{k}_{n+1}
is the nondecreasing rearrangement of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n+1}
and
C_{1}\geq 1
is a constant. Indeed, we have

(7.24)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}^{-1}(M_{n+1,\underline{k}})(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n+1})=C_{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\widetilde{\psi}_{k_{0}}(x_{0}-\rho)\prod_{l=1}^{n+1}\frac{\psi_{k_{l}}(x_{l})-\psi_{k_{l}}(x_{l}-\rho)}{\rho}\,d\rho,\\
&\psi_{k}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}e^{ix\eta}\varphi_{k}(\eta),\qquad\widetilde{\psi}_{k}(x):=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta\in\mathbb{Z}/R}e^{ix\eta}(\eta/2^{k})\varphi_{k}(\eta),\end{split}

where
M_{n+1,\underline{k}}(\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n+1}):=M_{n+1}(\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n+1})\cdot\varphi_{k_{0}}(\eta_{0})\ldots\varphi_{k_{n+1}}(\eta_{n+1})
,
\underline{k}:=(k_{0},\ldots,k_{n+1})
. This follows easily from (
7.19
). Using (
7.7
) we see easily that

\|\psi_{k}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}+\|\widetilde{\psi}_{k}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\lesssim 1,\qquad\|\psi_{k}(x)-\psi_{k}(x-\rho)\|_{L^{1}_{x}(\mathbb{T}_{R})}\lesssim\min(1,2^{k}|\rho|)

for any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
. The desired bounds (
7.23
) follow from (
7.24
) by integration in
\rho
.

Step 2.
To prove (
7.8
)–(
7.9
) we start from the formula (
7.13
), so

(7.25)

\psi=(I-T_{1}+T_{1}^{2}+\ldots+(-1)^{A-1}T_{1}^{A-1})(H_{0}+T_{2})\phi+(-1)^{A}T_{1}^{A}\psi.

We use the expansions (
7.16
) as well as the bounds

(7.26)

\begin{split}&\|R_{n}f\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{2}-1/2,1/10}}\leq B_{2}(C_{2}\epsilon_{\infty})^{n}\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}f\|_{\dot{W}^{1,0}},\\
&\|R_{n}f\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-1/2,1/10}}\leq(C_{2}\epsilon_{\infty})^{n+1}\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}f\|_{\dot{W}^{1,0}},\end{split}

for some constant
C_{2}\geq 1
and any
n\geq 0
, which follow from (
7.21
)–(
7.23
) and (
7.5
). Since
\||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\psi\|_{\dot{W}^{1,0}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}
(due to estimate (8.20) in
[
22
]
), we have

(7.27)

\|T_{1}^{A}\psi\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{2}-1/2,1/10}}\lesssim B_{2}\epsilon_{\infty}^{A},\qquad\|T_{1}^{A}\psi\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-1/2,1/10}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A+1}.

We examine now the main term in (
7.25
), expand the operators
T_{1},T_{2}
as in (
7.16
), and use the bounds (
7.26
) to estimate all the terms of homogeneity
\geq A+1
as in (
7.27
). We can thus write

(7.28)

\begin{split}&\psi=H_{0}\phi-R_{0}H_{0}\phi+\sum_{n=3}^{A}\widetilde{G}_{n}(h,\phi)+\widetilde{G}_{>A},\\
&\|\widetilde{G}_{>A}\|_{\dot{H}^{N_{2}-1/2,1/10}}\lesssim B_{2}\epsilon_{\infty}^{A},\qquad\|\widetilde{G}_{>A}\|_{\dot{W}^{N_{\infty}-1/2,1/10}}\lesssim\epsilon_{\infty}^{A+1},\\
&\widetilde{G}_{n}(h,\phi)=\sum_{l\geq 1,\,\iota\in\{0,1\},\,a_{1}+\ldots+a_{l}=n-l-1}c_{\iota,a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}R_{a_{1}}\ldots R_{a_{l}}H_{0}^{\iota}\phi,\end{split}

for some suitable coefficients
c_{\iota,a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then we can finally define

(7.29)

G_{2}(h,\phi):=\partial_{x}R_{0}H_{0}\phi,\qquad G_{n}(h,\phi):=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}_{n}(h,\phi),n\geq 3,\qquad G_{>A}:=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}_{>A}.

The identities and the estimates in (
7.8
) follow from (
7.28
), (
7.26
), and the observation that
\partial_{x}R_{0}H_{0}\phi=-\partial_{x}(h\partial_{x}\phi)-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi)
.

The formulas (
7.18
)–(
7.19
) show that if
a_{1}+\ldots+a_{l}+l=n-1
then

(7.30)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}\big[R_{a_{1}}\ldots R_{a_{l}}f\big](\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{n-1}}\sum_{\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n-1}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{0}+\ldots+\eta_{n-1}=\xi}\widetilde{q}_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n-1})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\widehat{f}(\eta_{0})\widehat{h}(\eta_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\widehat{h}(\eta_{n-1}),\\
&\widetilde{q}_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}(\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{n-1}):=M_{a_{1}+1}(\eta_{0}+s_{1},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{a_{1}+1})\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\ldots\times M_{a_{l}+1}(\eta_{0}+s_{l},\eta_{a_{1}+\ldots+a_{l-1}+l},\ldots,\eta_{a_{1}+\ldots+a_{l}+l}).\end{split}

where

s_{m}:=\eta_{a_{1}+a_{2}+\ldots+a_{m}+m+1}+\ldots+\eta_{n-1}\qquad\text{ for }m=1,\ldots,l.

The homogeneity property (
7.9
) follows from (
7.20
). To estimate the localized
S^{\infty}
norms we assume
k,k_{0},k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and let
K_{a_{1}\ldots a_{l};\iota}^{k;k_{0}k_{1}\ldots k_{n-1}}
denote the kernels defined by the localized multipliers
\widetilde{q}_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}
, i.e.

(7.31)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}(K_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\iota}^{k;k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}})(\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n-1})&=\widetilde{q}_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}}(\eta_{0},\ldots,\eta_{n-1})\varphi_{k_{0}}(\eta_{0})\cdot\ldots\cdot\varphi_{n-1}(\eta_{n-1})\\
&\times\varphi_{k}(\eta_{0}+\ldots+\eta_{n-1})(i\mathrm{sgn}\,\eta_{0})^{\iota}.\end{split}

We will prove that

(7.32)

\|K_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\iota}^{k;k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}\lesssim 2^{k_{0}+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n-1}}2^{-\widetilde{k}_{n-1}}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n-1}-\widetilde{k}_{1}|)^{l},

where
\widetilde{k}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\widetilde{k}_{n-1}
denotes the nondecreasing rearrangement of
k_{1},\ldots,k_{n-1}
.

We prove the bounds (
7.32
) by induction over
l
. If
l=1
then
n=a_{1}+2
, and the bounds (
7.32
) follow from (
7.21
)–(
7.23
) and (
7.5
). To prove the induction step, assume that the bounds (
7.32
) hold for all
l^{\prime}\leq l
. Assume that
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l+1}
are given,
a_{1}+\ldots+a_{l+1}+l+1=n-1
,
k,k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
, and let
K_{a_{1};0}^{k;p,k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1}}
and
K_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{p;k_{0},k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1}}
denote the localized kernels corresponding to the operators
R_{a_{1}}
and
R_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1}}H_{0}^{\iota}
respectively. The main observation is that

\begin{split}K_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{k;k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}}(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1})&=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{\mathbb{T}_{R}}K_{a_{1};0}^{k;n,k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1}}(\alpha,x_{1},\ldots,x_{a_{1}+1})\\
&\times K_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{n;k_{0},k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1}}(x_{0}-\alpha,x_{a_{1}+2}-\alpha,\ldots,x_{n-1}-\alpha)\,d\alpha.\end{split}

In particular

(7.33)

\|K_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\iota}^{k;k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}\leq\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\|K_{a_{1};0}^{k;p,k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{a_{1}+2})}\|K_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{p;k_{0},k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n-a_{1}-1})}.

Moreover,
K_{a_{1};0}^{k;p,k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1}}\equiv 0
unless
2^{p}\lesssim 2^{k_{max,1}}
where
k_{max,1}:=\max(k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1})
and

K_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{p;k_{0},k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\equiv 0
unless
2^{p}\lesssim 2^{k_{max,2}}
where
k_{max,2}:=\max(k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1})
, due to (
7.22
). Using (
7.33
) and the induction hypothesis on the kernels
K_{a_{1};0}^{k;p,k_{1},\ldots,k_{a_{1}+1}}
and
K_{a_{2},\ldots,a_{l+1};\iota}^{p;k_{0},k_{a_{1}+2},\ldots,k_{n-1}}
, we can finally estimate

\begin{split}\|K_{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l};\iota}^{k;k_{0},\ldots,k_{n-1}}\|_{L^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{n})}&\lesssim\sum_{2^{p}\lesssim 2^{\min(k_{max,1},k_{max,2})}}\frac{2^{p}2^{k_{1}+\ldots+k_{a_{1}+1}}}{2^{k_{\max,1}}}\frac{2^{k_{0}}2^{k_{a_{1}+2}+\ldots+k_{n-1}}}{2^{k_{\max,2}}}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n-1}-\widetilde{k}_{1}|)^{l+1}\\
&\lesssim 2^{k_{0}+k_{1}+\ldots+k_{n-1}}2^{-\max(k_{max,1},k_{max,2})}(1+|\widetilde{k}_{n-1}-\widetilde{k}_{1}|)^{l+1}.\end{split}

This completes the induction step and the proof of (
7.32
).

(ii) The functions
\partial_{t}h
and
\partial_{t}\phi
can be expressed using the main equations (
7.1
) and the expansion (
7.8
). The bounds in (
7.11
) follow from the bounds in (
7.8
) and simple algebra properties (see
[
22
, Lemma 2.4]
), while the
S^{\infty}
bounds in (
7.12
) follow from (
7.9
).
∎

7.1.
The first normal form

As in
[
30
, Lemma 5.1]
we define the new variables

(7.34)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&v:=h^{\ast}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi^{\ast},\\
&h^{\ast}:=h+|\partial_{x}|\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}h\big)/2,\qquad\phi^{\ast}:=\phi+H_{0}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big).\end{split}

Then we calculate

(7.35)

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h^{\ast}&=\partial_{t}h+|\partial_{x}|\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}\partial_{t}h\big),\\
\partial_{t}\phi^{\ast}&=\partial_{t}\phi+H_{0}\big(H_{0}\partial_{t}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big)+H_{0}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\partial_{t}\phi\big).\end{split}

We can thus calculate

(7.36)

(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})v=[\partial_{t}h^{\ast}-|\partial_{x}|\phi^{\ast}]+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}[\partial_{t}\phi^{\ast}+h^{\ast}].

Using the identities (
7.10
), we calculate

\begin{split}\partial_{t}h^{\ast}-|\partial_{x}|\phi^{\ast}&=-|\partial_{x}|\big[\phi+H_{0}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big)\big]+|\partial_{x}|\phi+\sum_{n=2}^{A}G_{n}(h,\phi)+G_{>A}\\
&+|\partial_{x}|\Big[H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}\Big(|\partial_{x}|\phi+\sum_{n=2}^{A}G_{n}(h,\phi)+G_{>A}\Big)\Big]\\
&=\sum_{n=2}^{A}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{n}(h,\phi)+\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{>A},\end{split}

where, with
n\in\{3,\ldots,A\}
,

(7.37)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{2}(h,\phi)&:=-\partial_{x}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big)+G_{2}(h,\phi)+|\partial_{x}|(H_{0}h\cdot\partial_{x}\phi),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{n}(h,\phi)&:=G_{n}(h,\phi)+|\partial_{x}|\big[H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}G_{n-1}(h,\phi)\big],\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{>A}&:=G_{>A}+|\partial_{x}|\big[H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}\big(G_{A}(h,\phi)+G_{>A}\big)\big].\end{split}

Similarly, we also calculate

\begin{split}&\partial_{t}\phi^{\ast}+h^{\ast}=h+|\partial_{x}|\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}h\big)/2-h+\sum_{n=2}^{A}H_{n}(h,\phi)+H_{>A}\\
&+H_{0}\Big[H_{0}\Big(|\partial_{x}|\phi+\sum_{n=2}^{A}G_{n}(h,\phi)+G_{>A}\Big)\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\Big]+H_{0}\Big[H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|\Big(-h+\sum_{n=2}^{A}H_{n}(h,\phi)+H_{>A}\Big)\Big]\\
&=\sum_{n=2}^{A}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{n}(h,\phi)+\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{>A},\end{split}

where, with
n\in\{3,\ldots,A\}
,

(7.38)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{2}(h,\phi)&:=|\partial_{x}|\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}h\big)/2+H_{2}(h,\phi)+H_{0}(\partial_{x}\phi\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi)-H_{0}(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|h),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{n}(h,\phi)&:=H_{n}(h,\phi)+H_{0}\big(H_{0}G_{n-1}(h,\phi)\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big)+H_{0}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|H_{n-1}(h,\phi)\big),\\
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{>A}&:=H_{>A}+H_{0}\big[H_{0}(G_{A}(h,\phi)+G_{>A})\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big]+H_{0}\big[H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|(H_{A}(h,\phi)+H_{>A})\big].\end{split}

We show now that the quadratic terms
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{2}(h,\phi)
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{2}(h,\phi)
vanish. Indeed,

\mathcal{F}(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{2}(h,\phi))(\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta,\rho\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta+\rho=\xi}n_{2}^{1}(\eta,\rho)\widehat{h}(\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\rho)

where, recalling that
G_{2}(h,\phi)=-\partial_{x}(h\partial_{x}\phi)-|\partial_{x}|(h|\partial_{x}|\phi)
,

\begin{split}n_{2}^{1}(\eta,\rho)&:=(\eta+\rho)\,\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)|\rho|-|\eta+\rho|\,\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\rho+(\eta+\rho)\rho-|\eta+\rho||\rho|\\
&=|\eta+\rho||\rho|\big[\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)-\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)+\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)-1\big]=0.\end{split}

The last identity follows by examining explicitly the cases
\rho>0
and
\rho<0
. Moreover

|\partial_{x}|\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}h\big)/2-H_{0}(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|h)=H_{0}\partial_{x}\big(H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}h\big)/2-H_{0}(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|h)=0

and, recalling that
H_{2}(h,\phi)=-(\partial_{x}\phi)^{2}/2+(|\partial_{x}|\phi)^{2}/2
,

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}[H_{2}(h,\phi)+H_{0}(\partial_{x}\phi\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi)](\xi)=\frac{1}{2\pi R}\sum_{\eta,\rho\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta+\rho=\xi}n_{2}^{2}(\eta,\rho)\widehat{\phi}(\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\rho),\\
&n_{2}^{2}(\eta,\rho)=\frac{1}{2}|\eta||\rho|+\frac{1}{2}\eta\rho-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)\eta|\rho|-\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)|\eta|\rho\\
&\qquad\quad\,\,=(1/2)|\eta||\rho|\big[1+\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)-\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)-\mathrm{sgn}(\eta+\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)]=0.\end{split}

Therefore
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{2}(h,\phi)=0
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{2}(h,\phi)=0
. To summarize, we proved the following:

Lemma 7.3
.

With
v
defined as in (
7.34
) we have

(7.39)

(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})v=\sum_{n=3}^{A}\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{n}(h,\phi)+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{n}(h,\phi)\big]+\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{>A}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{>A}\big],

where the nonlinear terms
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{n}(h,\phi),\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{n}(h,\phi),\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{>A},\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{>A}
are defined in (
7.37
)–(
7.38
).

7.2.
The cubic nonlinearities

The cubic terms
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{3}(h,\phi)
and
|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{3}(h,\phi)
and be calculated explicitly in terms of the variable
u
. Using (
7.37
) and (
7.29
) we write

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{3}(h,\phi)=G_{3}(h,\phi)+|\partial_{x}|\big[H_{0}h\cdot H_{0}G_{2}(h,\phi)\big]=-\partial_{x}\widetilde{G}_{3}(h,\phi)-|\partial_{x}|\big[H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|R_{0}H_{0}\phi\big].

We examine the expansion (
7.25
) and identify the cubic term (using also (
7.16
)),

\widetilde{G}_{3}(h,\phi)=R_{1}\phi+R_{0}^{2}H_{0}\phi.

The identity (8.47) in
[
22
]
shows that

(7.40)

\begin{split}\widehat{R_{1}f}(\xi)&=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma=\xi}\frac{i}{2}\rho\big[|\sigma|+|\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma|-|\eta_{1}+\sigma|-|\eta_{2}+\sigma|\big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\widehat{f}(\sigma)\widehat{h}(\eta_{1})\widehat{h}(\eta_{2}).\end{split}

Using also (
7.21
) and symmetrizing in the variables
\eta_{1},\eta_{2}
(corresponding to the two copies of
h
) we have

(7.41)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}(G_{3}(h,\phi))(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma=\xi}q_{3}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\sigma)\widehat{\phi}(\sigma)\widehat{h}(\eta_{1})\widehat{h}(\eta_{2}),\\
&q_{3}(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\sigma):=\frac{|\sigma||\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma|}{2}\big[|\eta_{1}+\sigma|+|\eta_{2}+\sigma|-|\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma|-|\sigma|\big].\end{split}

Moreover, using (
7.21
) again,

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}&\big\{-|\partial_{x}|\big[H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|R_{0}H_{0}\phi\big]\big\}(\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma=\xi}\widehat{\phi}(\sigma)\widehat{h}(\eta_{1})\widehat{h}(\eta_{2})\\
&\times\big\{|\eta_{1}+\eta_{2}+\sigma|\mathrm{sgn}(\eta_{1})|\eta_{2}+\sigma||\sigma|\big[\mathrm{sgn}(\eta_{2}+\sigma)-\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\big]\big\}.\end{split}

We symmetrize this formula in
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{2}
. Using also (
7.41
) we derive the identity

(7.42)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{3}(h,\phi)](\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta,\rho,\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta+\rho+\sigma=\xi}\widehat{h}(\eta)\widehat{h}(\rho)\widehat{|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi}(\sigma)a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma),\\
&a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma):=\frac{|\xi||\sigma|^{1/2}}{2}\big\{|\sigma+\eta|+|\sigma+\rho|-|\xi|-|\sigma|-|\sigma+\eta|\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)\\
&\qquad\qquad\,\,-|\sigma+\rho|\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)+(\sigma+\eta)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)+(\sigma+\rho)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\big\}.\end{split}

Similarly, using equation (
7.38
) we have

\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{3}(h,\phi)=H_{3}(h,\phi)+H_{0}\big(H_{0}G_{2}(h,\phi)\cdot|\partial_{x}|\phi\big)+H_{0}\big(H_{0}h\cdot|\partial_{x}|H_{2}(h,\phi)\big).

Recalling the definitions (
7.10
) and the equations (
7.1
), we have

H_{3}(h,\phi)=|\partial_{x}|\phi\cdot\big[G_{2}(h,\phi)+\partial_{x}h\cdot\partial_{x}\phi\big].

Using also the formulas for
G_{2}(h,\phi)
and
H_{2}(h,\phi)
in (
7.8
) and (
7.11
), we have

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{3}&(h,\phi)](\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta,\rho,\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta+\rho+\sigma=\xi}\widehat{\phi}(\eta)\widehat{\phi}(\rho)\widehat{h}(\sigma)\big\{|\eta|\big[\rho(\sigma+\rho)-|\rho||\sigma+\rho|-\sigma\rho\big]\\
&-\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)|\eta|\big[\rho|\sigma+\rho|-|\rho|(\sigma+\rho)\big]-(1/2)\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)|\eta+\rho|(\eta\rho+|\eta||\rho|)\big\}.\end{split}

We symmetrize in
\eta
and
\rho
to derive our second main formula

(7.43)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{F}[|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{3}(h,\phi)](\xi)=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\eta,\rho,\sigma\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\eta+\rho+\sigma=\xi}\widehat{|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi}(\eta)\widehat{||\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi}(\rho)\widehat{h}(\sigma)a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma),\\
&a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma):=\frac{|\xi|^{1/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}}{2}\big\{|\eta|+|\rho|+(\xi+\sigma)\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)-|\sigma+\rho|\big(1+\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)\big)\\
&\qquad\qquad-|\sigma+\eta|\big(1+\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\big)-|\eta+\rho|\mathrm{sgn}(\xi)\mathrm{sgn}(\sigma)\big(1+\mathrm{sgn}(\eta)\mathrm{sgn}(\rho)\big)\big\}.\end{split}

Since

h=\frac{u+\overline{u}}{2},\qquad|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\phi=\frac{u-\overline{u}}{2i},

we can use the identities (
7.42
)–(
7.43
) to calculate

(7.44)

\begin{split}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{3}(h,\phi)+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{3}(h,\phi)=\mathcal{N}_{3,+++}+\mathcal{N}_{3,++-}+\mathcal{N}_{3,+--}+\mathcal{N}_{3,---},\\
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N}_{3,+++})(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}p_{+++}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{u}(\xi_{1})\widehat{u}(\xi_{2})\widehat{u}(\xi_{3}),\\
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N}_{3,++-})(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}p_{++-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{u}(\xi_{1})\widehat{u}(\xi_{2})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{3}),\\
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N}_{3,+--})(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}p_{+--}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{u}(\xi_{1})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{2})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{3}),\\
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N}_{3,---})(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}p_{---}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{1})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{2})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{3}),\end{split}

where the symbols
p_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}
are given by

(7.45)

\begin{split}p_{+++}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})&:=\frac{1}{8i}\big[a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})+a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big],\\
p_{++-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})&:=\frac{1}{8i}\big[-a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})+a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{3},\xi_{1},\xi_{2})+a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{3},\xi_{2},\xi_{1})\\
&\qquad\quad\,+a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})-a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{1},\xi_{2})-a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{3},\xi_{2},\xi_{1})\big],\\
p_{+--}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})&:=\frac{1}{8i}\big[-a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})-a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{2})+a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{1})\\
&\qquad\quad\,-a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})-a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{2})+a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\xi_{1})\big],\\
p_{---}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})&:=\frac{1}{8i}\big[-a_{3}^{1}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})+a_{3}^{2}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big].\end{split}

The identities (
7.39
) and (
7.44
) show that

(7.46)

\begin{split}&(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})v=\mathcal{IN}_{3}+\mathcal{IN}_{\geq 4},\\
&\mathcal{IN}_{3}:=\mathcal{N}_{3,+++}+\mathcal{N}_{3,++-}+\mathcal{N}_{3,+--}+\mathcal{N}_{3,---}\\
&\mathcal{IN}_{\geq 4}:=\sum_{n=4}^{A}\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{n}(h,\phi)+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{n}(h,\phi)\big]+\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{1}_{>A}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}^{2}_{>A}\big].\end{split}

We can calculate explicitly the symbols
a_{3}^{1}
and
a_{3}^{2}
using the formulas (
7.42
)–(
7.43
). Assuming that
\xi=\eta+\rho+\sigma\geq 0
, we calculate

(7.47)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\,\,\text{ and }\,\,a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=-|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\qquad\text{ if }\eta,\rho,\sigma>0,

(7.48)

\begin{split}a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=|\xi||\sigma|^{1/2}\big[|\sigma+\eta|+|\sigma+\rho|-|\sigma|\big]\,\,&\text{ and }\,\,a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=3|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\\
&\text{ if }\eta,\rho>0\text{ and }\sigma<0,\\
\end{split}

(7.49)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta<0\text{ and }\rho,\sigma>0,

(7.50)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=-|\xi|^{2}|\sigma|^{1/2}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta,\sigma<0\text{ and }\rho>0,

(7.51)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta,\sigma>0\text{ and }\rho<0,

(7.52)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=-|\xi|^{2}|\sigma|^{1/2}\,\,\text{ and }\,\,a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta>0\text{ and }\rho,\sigma<0,

(7.53)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\,\,\text{ and }\,\,a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\qquad\text{ if }\eta,\rho<0\text{ and }\sigma>0,

(7.54)

a_{3}^{1}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=a_{3}^{2}(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\qquad\text{ if }\eta=0\text{ or }\rho=0\text{ or }\sigma=0.

7.3.
The second normal form

The symbols
a_{3}^{j}
in (
7.47
)–(
7.54
) are homogeneous of degree
5/2
and satisfy good
S^{\infty}
bounds of the form

(7.55)

\big\|\varphi_{k}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\varphi_{k_{3}}(\xi_{3})a_{3}^{j}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k}2^{\widetilde{k}_{2}/2}2^{\widetilde{k}_{3}},

for any
k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and
j\in\{1,2\}
, where
\widetilde{k}_{1}\leq\widetilde{k}_{2}\leq\widetilde{k}_{3}
denotes the nondecreasing rearangement of
k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
. This can be easily verified using the explicit formulas. The main issue, however, is that these symbols do not satisfy the
C^{1/2}
bounds (
2.13
), which are important in some of the combinatorial arguments involving cancellation. To correct this problem we use a second normal form to eliminate the cubic terms
\mathcal{N}_{3,+++}
,
\mathcal{N}_{3,+--}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{3,---}
, as well as part of the cubic term
\mathcal{N}_{3,++-}
. More precisely:

Lemma 7.4
.

(i) We define

(7.56)

\begin{split}&w:=v+A_{3,+++}(u,u,u)+A_{3,++-}(u,u,\overline{u})+A_{3,+--}(u,\overline{u},\overline{u})+A_{3,---}(\overline{u},\overline{u},\overline{u}),\\
&\mathcal{F}(A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(f,g,h))(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{f}(\xi_{1})\widehat{g}(\xi_{2})\widehat{h}(\xi_{3}),\end{split}

where the symbols
q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}
are defined for
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\in\{(+++),(++-),(+--),(---)\}
by

(7.57)

\begin{split}&q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}):=\frac{ip_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})}{|\xi|^{1/2}-\iota_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{1/2}-\iota_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{1/2}-\iota_{3}|\xi_{3}|^{1/2}}\qquad\text{ if }\,\,(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\neq(++-),\\
&q_{++-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}):=\frac{ip_{3,++-}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big[\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{R}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})+\mathbf{1}_{{}^{c}\mathcal{R}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\Upsilon(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\big]}{|\xi|^{1/2}-|\xi_{1}|^{1/2}-|\xi_{2}|^{1/2}+|\xi_{3}|^{1/2}},\\
&\mathcal{R}:=\{(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\,x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}(x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3})\geq 0\},\\
&\Upsilon(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}):=\varphi\Big(\frac{2^{10}(\xi_{1}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2}+\xi_{3}^{2}+\xi^{2})\min(\xi_{1}^{2},\xi_{2}^{2},\xi_{3}^{2},\xi^{2})}{\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}\xi}\Big),\end{split}

where
\xi=\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}
. Then

(7.58)

\begin{split}&(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})w=\mathcal{W}_{3}+\mathcal{W}_{\geq 4},\\
&\widehat{\mathcal{W}_{3}}(\xi):=\frac{1}{(2\pi R)^{2}}\sum_{\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}/R,\,\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}=\xi}[p_{3,++-}\mathbf{1}_{{}^{c}\mathcal{R}}(1-\Upsilon)](\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\widehat{u}(\xi_{1})\widehat{u}(\xi_{2})\widehat{\overline{u}}(\xi_{3}),\\
&\mathcal{W}_{\geq 4}:=\mathcal{IN}_{\geq 4}+\sum_{(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\in\{(+++),(++-),(+--),(---)\}}\big\{A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}((\partial_{t}u+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u)^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})\\
&\qquad\,\,+A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},(\partial_{t}u+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u)^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})+A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},(\partial_{t}u+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u)^{\iota_{3}})\big\},\end{split}

where
\mathcal{IN}_{\geq 4}
is defined in (
7.46
) and
f^{+}=f
,
f^{-}=\overline{f}
.

(ii) Moreover the symbols
q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}
are homogeneous of degree
2
and satisfy the
S^{\infty}
bounds

(7.59)

\begin{split}&q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\lambda\xi_{1},\lambda\xi_{2},\lambda\xi_{3})=\lambda^{2}q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\qquad\text{ for any }\lambda>0,\,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3}\in\mathbb{R},\\
&\big\|q_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3})\varphi_{k}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\varphi_{k_{3}}(\xi_{3})\big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{k/2}2^{3\max(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})/2}\end{split}

for any
k,k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Proof.

(i) Using the definitions (
7.56
) we calculate

\begin{split}(\partial_{t}&+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})w=(\partial_{t}+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2})v+\sum_{(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})}\big\{i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})\\
&+A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\partial_{t}u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})+A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},\partial_{t}u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})+A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},\partial_{t}u^{\iota_{3}})\big\},\end{split}

where the sum is taken over
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\in\{(+++),(++-),(+--),(---)\}
. We use the first identity in (
7.46
) and notice that

\begin{split}\mathcal{N}_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}&+i|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})-i\iota_{1}A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(\{|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})\\
&-i\iota_{2}A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u^{\iota_{2}},u^{\iota_{3}})-i\iota_{3}A_{3,\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}(u^{\iota_{1}},u^{\iota_{2}},|\partial_{x}|^{1/2}u^{\iota_{3}})=0\end{split}

for any
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\in\{(+++),(+--),(---)\}
, due to the definitions (
7.56
)–(
7.57
). The desired identity (
7.58
) follows.

(ii) The homogeneity property in (
7.59
) follows from the definitions (
7.57
), since the symbols
p_{\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3}}
are homogeneous of degree
5/2
(see the formulas (
7.45
) and (
7.42
)–(
7.43
)).

To prove the
S^{\infty}
bounds in the second line we notice the elementary inequality

(7.60)

|x_{1}|^{1/2}+|x_{2}|^{1/2}+|x_{3}|^{1/2}-|x_{1}+x_{2}+x_{3}|^{1/2}\geq|x_{2}|^{1/2}/2

for any
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\mathbb{R}
with
|x_{1}|\leq|x_{2}|\leq|x_{3}|
. In fact, using Lemma
7.1
(iii) it is easy to see that

(7.61)

\Big\|\frac{\varphi_{k_{0}}(\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3})\varphi_{k_{1}}(\xi_{1})\varphi_{k_{2}}(\xi_{2})\varphi_{k_{3}}(\xi_{3})}{|\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}+\xi_{3}|^{1/2}-\iota_{1}|\xi_{1}|^{1/2}-\iota_{2}|\xi_{2}|^{1/2}-\iota_{3}|\xi_{3}|^{1/2}}\Big\|_{S^{\infty}}\lesssim 2^{-\widetilde{k}_{1}/2},

for any
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}\in\overline{\mathbb{Z}}
and triplets
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})\in\{(+++),\,(+--),\,(-+-),\,(--+),(---)\}
, where
\widetilde{k}_{0}\leq\widetilde{k}_{1}\leq\widetilde{k}_{2}\leq\widetilde{k}_{3}
is the nondecreasing rearrangement of
k_{0},k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}
. The desired bounds in (
7.59
) follow using also (
7.55
) and (
7.6
).

In the remaining case
(\iota_{1}\iota_{2}\iota_{3})=(++-)
we need to be more careful because we have to exploit certain cancellations in order to estimate the contribution of the Benjamin-Feir resonances. The formulas (
7.47
)–(
7.54
) show that if
\xi=\eta+\rho+\sigma\geq 0
then

(7.62)

\begin{split}&(a_{3}^{1}-a_{3}^{2})(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\quad\text{ if }\eta,\rho,\sigma>0,\\
&(a_{3}^{1}-a_{3}^{2})(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=|\xi||\sigma|^{1/2}\big[|\sigma+\eta|+|\sigma+\rho|-|\sigma|\big]-3|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\quad\text{ if }\eta,\rho>0\text{ and }\sigma<0,\\
&(a_{3}^{1}-a_{3}^{2})(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=0\quad\text{ if }\eta\cdot\rho<0\text{ and }\sigma>0,\\
&(a_{3}^{1}-a_{3}^{2})(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=-|\xi|^{2}|\sigma|^{1/2}\quad\text{ if }\eta\cdot\rho<0\text{ and }\sigma<0,\\
&(a_{3}^{1}-a_{3}^{2})(\eta,\rho,\sigma)=-|\xi|^{3/2}|\eta|^{1/2}|\rho|^{1/2}\quad\text{ if }\eta,\rho<0\text{ and }\sigma>0.\end{split}

Using now the formulas (
7.45
), if
